## 📚 Paper List

- **[Asymmetric Decomposition for Multi-Agent Path Finding with Unassigned Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24108)**  `arXiv:2611.24108`  `cs.RO` `cs.MA`  
  _Xi Jin, Chen Qian, Renjun Zhang, Guowei Wu, Xiaobo Li, Jun Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots without assigned tasks may obstruct active robots, while routing them to designated parking locations can cause unnecessary travel. This article proposes flow-oracle conflict-based search (FOCBS) for Multi-Agent Path Finding with unassigned agents (MAPF-UA). FOCBS searches over assigned-agent paths using conflict-based search (CBS) and routes targetless unassigned agents through a min-cost flow oracle. When the selected assigned-agent paths leave unassigned agents no collision-free paths, flow-conflict branching converts min-cut blockers into assigned-agent constraints, which guide the search without excluding optimal solutions. A batched zero-cost warm start accelerates the oracle, and start-avoiding tie-breaking reduces unassigned-agent movement. For feasible instances on finite graphs, we prove termination and global minimization of assigned-agent sum of service time, measured by last-arrival times. Unassigned-agent movement is minimized conditional on the selected assigned-agent paths, without a global lexicographic optimality guarantee. Benchmark experiments show that FOCBS scales well with the number of unassigned agents, and ablation studies show that, compared with discarding non-completable assigned-agent paths, flow-conflict branching solves substantially more instances while retaining optimality. Simulation and physical-robot experiments demonstrate plan executability, with laboratory tests reducing fleet travel by 40.8% relative to the parking-based coordination baseline. The source code and related resources are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[How Uncertain Is My Action? Self-Correcting Visual Planning with Frozen World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23620)**  `arXiv:2611.23620`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Siqing Li, Luming Yang, Yijia Jiao, Junze Deng, Alper Yilmaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A frozen latent world model combined with sampling-based model predictive control is a visual planner. At deployment, the executed action can depart from the commanded one, and the frozen model has no way to notice. We show that the model can be run in reverse: for any hypothesized actuation shift, it predicts what it should have observed under that shift, and comparing this prediction with what was actually observed quantifies the shift and lets the planner correct itself. Unlike the residual energy, which mixes the shift with the model's own error, the score keeps only the part of the residual that a shift can produce. This shift score detects the shift with a locally optimal test, estimates it with Cramer-Rao error bars, names the dominant generator, and compensates the planner once an alarm fires. On PushT, TwoRoom and OGBench-Cube with a frozen latent world model, measured detection follows the prediction to within 0.05 AUROC. Self-correction raises success from 0.650 to 0.862 on PushT and from 0.378 to 0.432 on the harder Cube task, levels with an oracle that is told the true shift at the same block, better than five common test-time adaptation methods. On a real robotic arm, the score identifies a base-joint offset that no joint encoder registers and reduces the reach error from 36 mm to 3-5 mm, and under shifts drawn at random and sealed before the session, estimates recover 92-94% of the error reduction achieved with the true shift. The broader implication is that a frozen world model can act as a filter by predicting the future, testing action-interface uncertainty and validity, and finally correcting failures.
  </details>

- **[Heave-Ho: Decentralized Long-Horizon Multi-Humanoid Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22275)**  `arXiv:2611.22275`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ting-Hsuan Chen, Siheng Zhao, Han Jiang, Qi Pan, Louison Lu, Jiaheng Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaboration promises to extend humanoid capabilities beyond what individual robots can achieve. However, realizing this potential requires coordinated whole-body interactions, coherent long-horizon planning, and precise synchronization across robots. We introduce Heave-Ho, a decentralized framework for long-horizon collaborative multi-humanoid loco-manipulation. At the low level, we develop a multi-agent post-training framework that adapts a humanoid foundation tracking model to physically coupled interactions between humanoids, enabling coordinated decentralized control from local observations alone. At the high level, we train a vision-language planner and design a speech-based leader-follower coordination mechanism to synchronize decentralized execution without network communication. Trained with two identical humanoids, our system scales to teams of up to ten robots and generalizes to heterogeneous teams without retraining. To benchmark these capabilities, we introduce HH-Bench, comprising 12 tasks involving teams of 2 to 10 humanoids, including heterogeneous teams. Systematic evaluations show that Heave-Ho consistently outperforms baseline methods, while real-world experiments demonstrate robust decentralized collaboration across challenging tasks.
  </details>

- **[AERR-Nav: Adaptive Exploration-Recovery-Reminiscing Strategy for Zero-Shot Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17712)**  `arXiv:2603.17712`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingzhi Huang, Junkai Huang, Haoyang Yang, Haoang Li, Yi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-Shot Object Navigation (ZSON) in unknown multi-floor environments presents a significant challenge. Recent methods, mostly based on semantic value greedy waypoint selection, spatial topology-enhanced memory, and Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) as a decision-making framework, have led to improvements. However, these architectures struggle to balance exploration and exploitation for ZSON when encountering unseen environments, especially in multi-floor settings, such as robots getting stuck at narrow intersections, endlessly wandering, or failing to find stair entrances. To overcome these challenges, we propose AERR-Nav, a Zero-Shot Object Navigation framework that dynamically adjusts its state based on the robot's environment. Specifically, AERR-Nav has the following two key advantages: (1) An Adaptive Exploration-Recovery-Reminiscing Strategy, enables robots to dynamically transition between three states, facilitating specialized responses to diverse navigation scenarios. (2) An Adaptive Exploration State featuring Fast and Slow-Thinking modes helps robots better balance exploration, exploitation, and higher-level reasoning based on evolving environmental information. Extensive experiments on the HM3D and MP3D benchmarks demonstrate that our AERR-Nav achieves state-of-the-art performance among zero-shot methods. Comprehensive ablation studies further validate the efficacy of our proposed strategy and modules.
  </details>

- **[$Z$-sight: Unifying Vision-Language-Action and Latent World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24940)**  `arXiv:2611.24940`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Junjie Ye, Yiqi Zhao, Muchen Xu, Celina Shiyu Wang, Hao Dong, Ruohai Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce $Z$-sight, a unified robot foundation model that couples vision-language-action (VLA) and generative latent world modeling. Video-based world action models (WAMs) show the value of joint future-and-action generation for control, yet inherit the heavy cost of video generators. Recent latent-space policies avoid pixel generation, yet typically align intermediate features or rely on frozen target encoders, decoupling future prediction from the VLM's native feature space. $Z$-sight instead predicts the future directly in the visual token space in which its VLM perceives the present. Coupling a foresight expert and a continuous action expert with the VLM in a mixture-of-transformers, $Z$-sight jointly denoises future spatial tokens and actions at inference without pixel reconstruction or external encoders. Trained on human video and robot trajectories, $Z$-sight achieves 81.9% success on SIMPLE (+9.6 points over the strongest baseline) and 73.3% across six Unitree G1 tasks (+20.0 points) at 110 ms per chunk, while remaining competitive on tabletop benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[WAM-Policy: World Action Models with On-Policy Distillation for Quadrupedal Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24011)**  `arXiv:2611.24011`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Junlin Long, Xu Deng, Yiran Wang, Yue Yang, Yang Zhao, Zeyu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Arm-free quadrupeds can perform loco-manipulation, walking on three legs while manipulating with a front leg, yet no existing policy for it follows language instructions. World action models (WAMs) have emerged as effective language-conditioned manipulation policies, but are pretrained for robot arms and sample slowly. WAM-Policy turns one WAM into a quadrupedal loco-manipulation policy via two stages. The first, Cross-Morphology Policy Adaptation and Acceleration, adapts the arm-centric WAM to quadrupeds and distills the transferred policy into a student with one video and two action steps by on-policy self-distillation (OPSD). This student, however, is bounded by its teacher, which only imitates demonstrations. The second stage, Task-Specific Improvement and Merging, therefore tunes the teacher by reinforcement learning (RL) and merges the slow specialists into one few-step student by multi-teacher on-policy distillation (M-OPD). On QLM-Bench, ten simulated tasks with 1,000 demonstrations synthesized without teleoperation, the transferred policy reaches 77.0%, and the OPSD student keeps 76.7% while sampling 14 times faster. Task-specific RL and M-OPD lift it to 85.0%. We also deploy the OPSD student on a real Unitree Go2 Air. WAM-Policy thus achieves a stronger quadrupedal policy at a fraction of the inference cost.
  </details>

- **[RePlan-VLA: Progress-Aware Replanning for Long-Horizon Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23843)**  `arXiv:2611.23843`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Apoorva Vashisth, Manav Kulshrestha, Damon Conover, Zachary Kingston, Aniket Bera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet their performance often degrades on long-horizon tasks that require multiple interdependent manipulations. Instead of burdening the VLA policies with long-horizon reasoning, memory, action generation, progress tracking, and error recovery, we argue for leveraging their short-horizon manipulation capabilities within an execution-aware framework. To this end, we introduce \textnormal{\textbf{RePlan-VLA}}, a VLA-agnostic framework that translates a VLA's short-horizon capabilities into reliable long-horizon execution. RePlan-VLA explicitly monitors the robot's motion for progress evaluation, and adaptively revises the future plan based on observed outcomes. We preserve the underlying VLA policy entirely, requiring no additional fine-tuning. Experiments on RoboCasa365 and CALVIN benchmarks show an improvement of 21.9% and 10.4% in success rate over the next strongest baseline. We also validate our approach for real-world deployment on a Franka Research 3 robot.
  </details>

- **[On Agentic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892)**  `arXiv:2611.09892`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Towards Pose-Robust Few-Shot Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24360)**  `arXiv:2611.24360`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Wenhao Li, Zhongyi Han, Yuhao Wei, Liang Han, Yilong Yin, Liqiang Nie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning a vision-language-action (VLA) model on a few dozen demonstrations is the standard way to put a manipulation policy on a robot, yet the resulting policy silently assumes that the camera and the robot base stay exactly where they were during data collection: a bumped tripod or a base re-docked a few centimetres away makes it reach for the wrong place. We show that both failures are one phenomenon, a change of the camera-to-base extrinsic that the policy has memorised, and that the robot carries everything needed to undo it. We introduce WACO, a world-anchored canonicalization framework for VLA models: the arm, placed by forward kinematics at the measured joint angles, serves as a metric calibration target, and the static scene serves as the world anchor that tells a moved camera from a moved robot. From a single RGB-D frame, WACO estimates both shifts without ground-truth extrinsics or extra data, canonicalises the image to the calibration viewpoint and the state, action and start pose to the calibration base frame, and fine-tunes the unmodified policy under the artefacts of its own estimator. We also introduce LIBERO-PoseShift, the first benchmark for RGB VLAs with graded camera, base and compound shifts. Across LIBERO-PoseShift, the LIBERO-Plus camera track and four real-world tasks on a UR5e robot, WACO consistently outperforms the standard few-shot recipe and prior viewpoint-robust methods, raising shifted success by 13.2 points in simulation and by 36.7 points on the real robot, recovering 90% of the simulation gap to a ground-truth oracle and 87% of the real-robot gap, and establishing a new state of the art among methods that use no perturbed training data, with no test-time calibration. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MANGO-Grasp: Mahalanobis Fields over Geometry-Oriented 3D Gaussians for Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02014)**  `arXiv:2608.02014`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Heng Zhang, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Mike Zheng Shou, Weisi Lin, Yan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-embodiment dexterous grasping aims to synthesize stable grasps across heterogeneous multi-fingered hands with little or no embodiment-specific tuning. Existing interaction-centric methods achieve promising results, but their object representations often underrepresent local surface geometry, hand descriptors do not explicitly capture both morphology and kinematics, and hand-object interaction modeling is typically isotropic. We propose MANGO-Grasp, an anisotropic interaction framework that represents objects as geometry-oriented 3D Gaussian primitives to better preserve local surface geometry, and encodes hands into pretrained descriptors that capture both morphology and kinematics. Hand-object interaction is modeled using anisotropic Mahalanobis fields that reflect the directional nature of contact and guide a shared optimization for grasp realization across embodiments. On the CMAP and MultiGripperGrasp benchmarks, MANGO-Grasp outperforms the strongest seen-hand baseline by up to 8.24 percentage points in simulation. It also transfers zero-shot to the unseen SharpaWave hand, improving over the strongest zero-shot baseline by up to 16.57 percentage points, and achieves 86% success in real-world experiments. The code and additional materials are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Phoenix: Learning Scene Structure Representations for Generalizable World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24938)**  `arXiv:2611.24938`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yupeng Zheng, Chaoyue Li, Linbo Wang, Xiang Li, Yuhang Zheng, Xiaotong Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models jointly predict future observations and actions, yet reconstruction-oriented visual representations do not explicitly emphasize the spatial relationships and object-state changes needed for manipulation. We introduce Phoenix, which learns representations of scene structure for world-action modeling. Specifically, Phoenix Encoder enriches DINOv3's general visual semantics through dual-teacher distillation of geometric and spatiotemporal semantic knowledge. Next, CS-VAE causally aggregates and compresses Phoenix Encoder features into generative latents while preserving scene information and spatial layout. Finally, Phoenix jointly predicts these latents, complementary appearance latents, and robot actions, linking scene evolution with action generation. To study pretraining data efficiency, we curate approximately 39,000 hours of egocentric and robot manipulation data for representation learning and use the robot demonstration subset for WAM pretraining. Following benchmark-specific post-training, Phoenix achieves state-of-the-art performance across simulation and real-robot benchmarks spanning single-arm, bimanual, and dexterous manipulation. It demonstrates strong robustness to perturbations, achieving success rates of 94.8% on LIBERO-Plus and 72.4% on RoboTwin 2.0 C2R, alongside strong cross-embodiment transfer and real-world manipulation performance on Bench2Dex and RoboDojo-RealWorld, respectively. Pretraining-scale analysis shows larger performance gains from data scaling than a video-VAE-only baseline, indicating stronger scaling benefits and more effective data utilization. Moreover, Phoenix maintains high success rates when adapted with limited downstream demonstrations, demonstrating strong few-shot adaptation. Our code, pretraining data, and models are available on the project page to help the community reproduce our results.
  </details>

- **[DiWALI: Diffusion Policy Learning in World Models via Latent Action Inference from Human and Robot Play](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24836)**  `arXiv:2611.24836`  `cs.RO`  
  _Akshay L Chandra, Iman Nematollahi, Lingyue Zhao, Daria Kulikova, Angela P. Schoellig, Fabien Despinoy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning robot manipulation is constrained by the availability of action-labeled robot data, for both imitation and world models. Humans can demonstrate skills in seconds, but their demonstrations lack the required robot actions. We introduce DiWALI, a framework that learns and improves diffusion policies within a world model trained on human and robot play. DiWALI infers latent actions from consecutive frames using an inverse dynamics model, enabling world model training without action labels, and grounds them in robot commands through a decoder trained separately on robot play. Skill policies are trained over latent actions from action-free human demonstrations and improved with reinforcement learning in imagination. On the CALVIN benchmark, DiWALI policies trained solely from human demonstrations substantially outperform baselines trained with robot demonstrations. Incorporating human play into the world model training also yields stronger policies after fine-tuning than using the same amount of robot play alone. Experiments on a physical robot show that skills learned primarily from human demonstrations are further improved in imagination with a world model trained on human and robot play. Together, these results demonstrate that human experience can support both manipulation skill acquisition and subsequent policy improvement, reducing reliance on action-labeled robot data. Videos, dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RoboMorpher: Scaling Robot Morphology Representations for Cross-Embodiment Action Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24816)**  `arXiv:2611.24816`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wenwei Lin, Yan Qin, Weiliang Deng, Zixuan Li, Kaixuan Wang, Zanxin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning shared action representations is central to cross-embodiment manipulation. However, identical joint values can produce different spatial configurations across robots, limiting representations learned from joint values alone. We introduce RoboMorpher, a morphology-conditioned action tokenizer that jointly reconstructs joint angles and link poses to associate control coordinates with their kinematic effects. MorphGen supplies scalable kinematic supervision by generating serial-arm descriptions from survey-derived geometric priors. We pretrain RoboMorpher on 40 million morphology-state pairs and integrate the frozen tokenizer into a vision-language-action policy for discrete prediction and continuous refinement. We also introduce EmbodiBench, a ten-task benchmark with 30 generated URDFs per task. With downstream URDFs excluded from tokenizer pretraining, the policy achieves 56.40% success on RoboTwin 2.0 and 23.27% on EmbodiBench, exceeding continuous joint-space control by 9.73 and 15.34 percentage points. Removing link-pose supervision reduces EmbodiBench success by 17.94 percentage points, supporting morphology-aware geometric supervision for joint-action representation learning.
  </details>

- **[Planner-Controller Interaction for Ackermann Navigation: A Matched Simulation and Hardware Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24800)**  `arXiv:2611.24800`  `cs.RO`  
  _Moaiz Saeed, Marah Saleh, Mounis Shawgi, Mohamed Al Musleh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Global planners and path-tracking controllers are often evaluated independently, although their interaction determines how a vehicle follows a planned path. Whether simulation accurately predicts the performance of specific planner-controller combinations on Ackermann-steered hardware remains uncertain. This study evaluated three geometric controllers (Regulated Pure Pursuit, Stanley, and Vector Pursuit) paired with two global planners (Hybrid A* and NavFn) on a 1/10-scale Quanser QCar and in a matched Gazebo simulation. Both environments used shared custom planning and control algorithms, matched route geometry and goals, ground-truth pose references, and a common evaluation pipeline. Five runs per configuration and environment yielded 60 benchmark runs. Controller rankings for tracking accuracy and command smoothness transferred consistently between environments, with Spearman correlations of 0.77-0.94. However, planner-dependent effects showed substantially weaker agreement. In simulation, planner selection changed the root-mean-square (RMS) angular acceleration of the commanded velocities by no more than 0.030 rad/s^2, whereas Hybrid A* increased it by 0.141-0.843 rad/s^2 on hardware, depending on the controller. These differences coincided with planner-dependent achieved speeds that the simulation's idealised longitudinal response did not reproduce. Mean controller computation times remained below 0.5 ms on the Jetson TX2. The findings demonstrate that agreement in controller rankings does not guarantee accurate prediction of planner-dependent performance for specific controller pairings on hardware, highlighting the importance of physical validation.
  </details>

- **[TinyARM-X: A Series of Open-Source, Configurable, and Transparent Torque-Controlled Desktop-Scale Robotic Manipulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24554)**  `arXiv:2611.24554`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nicolas Kosanovic, Jean Chagas Vaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visuomotor policies have demonstrated that imprecise low-cost robots can autonomously complete complex manipulation tasks. However, most inexpensive manipulators rely on compact and rigidly position-controlled servos for actuation, which complicate simulation, operation, and safety. This work introduces TinyARM-X, a framework for easily manufacturable, configurable, and torque-controllable low-cost (<350 USD) serial manipulators. By using a single hardware/software architecture, a <7 Degree of Freedom (DoF) TinyARM can be manually reconfigured to an operational >3 DoF robot within 10 min. Actuator modeling and dynamic compensation enable torque-controlled applications like mixed-reality impedance control teleoperation and simulator force feedback rendering. Experiments include mechanical benchmarking and real-world force-aware telemanipulation. The TinyARM-7 has a payload of 33.5 g, a max speed of 0.77 m/s, and a reach of 210 mm. Moreover, with dynamic compensation, the robot has a backdrive-breakaway force of 1.4 N and a backdrive-running force of 0.7 N. Additionally, Cartesian-Impedance Control (CIC) attains a translational stiffness of >15 N/m. Lastly, the TinyARM-X stack is open-sourced to disseminate accessible desktop-scale torque-controlled robotic manipulation to the community.
  </details>

- **[Remote Teleoperation of Endovascular Robots: A Network Emulation Framework for Systematic Performance Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24280)**  `arXiv:2611.24280`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xingyu Chen, Nikola Fischer, Harry Robertshaw, Weiyuan Deng, Christos Bergeles, Thomas Booth_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote endovascular teleoperation requires network performance to be evaluated in relation to physical navigation and operator experience, while the effects of network degradation on the usability of remote endovascular systems remain insufficiently characterized from the perspective of clinical users. We developed a network emulation and benchmarking framework that combines an endovascular robot with data streaming. We conducted a user study involving eight clinicians from interventional neuroradiology and general interventional radiology and eight engineering students, who performed 480 navigation attempts across 15 network conditions. Communication performance and received video quality were assessed alongside navigation outcomes, confidence, workload and clinician rated acceptability. Under the most impaired combined condition, success was 78.1\%, compared with 93.8\% under the reference condition, and mean attempt time increased from 63.5 to 94.3\,s. At 5\% configured packet loss, 87.5\% of attempts succeeded, although all eight clinicians rated the condition unacceptable. Perceived video quality was associated with clinician confidence and acceptability, with mean within participant Spearman correlations of 0.72 and 0.78, respectively. Framework integration on a second robotic platform additionally supported successful remote navigation over a real network. Our framework provides a reusable basis that can help identify communication bottlenecks and assess their effects on navigation and clinician acceptance for endovascular intervention telesurgery tasks.
  </details>

- **[BiPathCPG: A Novel Load-Sharing Design Architecture for Compound Planetary Gearboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24142)**  `arXiv:2611.24142`  `cs.RO`  
  _Deepak Kapa, Aman Singh, Saketh Vegesna, Suryank Joshi, Mayank Goswami, Shishir Kolathaya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quasi-Direct Drive (QDD) actuators are widely used in legged robots due to their low reflected inertia, high backdrivability, and high torque density, commonly employing planetary or cycloidal gearboxes. Single-stage planetary gearboxes offer high efficiency but limited torque density; multi-stage designs improve torque density at the cost of efficiency; and cycloidal gearboxes provide high stiffness but suffer from torque ripple. Compound Planetary Gearboxes (CPG) offer a compromise, but conventional designs result in increased actuator width, mass, and reduced torsional stiffness in 3D-printed actuators. This paper proposes the BiPath Compound Planetary Gearbox (BiPathCPG), a novel architecture that introduces an idle second-stage sun gear and load-sharing second-stage planets within the otherwise unused space of a conventional CPG to simultaneously improve stiffness, reduce actuator width, and reduce mass. An optimization framework computes BiPathCPG parameters for a given motor and target gear ratio. The architecture is validated through a 3D-printed proof-of-concept actuator design, benchmarked against a conventional CPG using custom-built test rigs. The BiPathCPG achieves a torsional stiffness of 1394 Nm/rad, a 1.70x improvement over the conventional CPG, with a 32% reduction in backlash, 25 g lower mass, and approximately 6.4 mm reduction in axial width, while achieving a peak stall torque of 36.49 Nm.
  </details>

- **[Modelling the human-exoskeleton interaction interface - Benchmarking MuJoCo's deformable model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24008)**  `arXiv:2611.24008`  `cs.RO`  
  _Lisette Masselink, Xiaolong Zhu Thomas George Thuruthel, Mohamed Irfan Refai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The human-exoskeleton interaction interface, consisting of the exoskeleton attachment and the underlying human soft tissue, can affect the transfer of assistance. MuJoCo's \textit{flexcomp} has been previously used to model the soft tissue, but its accuracy has not been benchmarked against a finite element model. We benchmarked the \textit{flexcomp} Saint Venant-Kirchhoff (StVK) and Stable Neo-Hookean (SNH) models against COMSOL's Yeoh model in three cases: \textit{case 1}) spherical indentation, \textit{case 2}) compression, and \textit{case 3}) compression of a thigh. We compared force and strain energy as a function of indentation depth, hysteresis and computation time. MuJoCo's models capture force trends with a correlation of 0.97-0.99, but showed a stiffer response, which could be attributed to the volumetric locking of the linear finite elements. The MuJoCo models systematically underestimated hysteresis as MuJoCo's Rayleigh damping could not substitute a viscoelastic model. Moreover, MuJoCo's computational advantage was speed dependent: it was 2-2.5 times faster for spherical indentation, but COMSOL was faster for the other cases, which have more degrees of freedom and contacts. Although MuJoCo's \textit{flexcomp} offers a combination of soft-tissue modelling and multibody dynamics, the limitations regarding the stiffening and hysteresis should be addressed to be used for the analysis of the human-exoskeleton interaction interface.
  </details>

- **[LaST1.0: A Unified Latent World-Action Model with Control-Centric SpatioTemporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23974)**  `arXiv:2611.23974`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hao Chen, Xuheng Zhang, Zhuangzhe Wu, Jiaming Liu, Shaobo Zhang, Jialin Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-Action Models couple action learning with future prediction, enabling robotic policies to capture environmental dynamics for robust control. However, prevailing methods take a reconstruction-oriented approach to model future videos, allocating substantial capacity to appearance details that are incidental to control. In this work, we reformulate world modeling for manipulation as control-centric future prediction in latent space, and present LaST1.0, a unified latent World-Action Model. Rather than modeling an observation-complete video future, LaST1.0 predicts compact, geometry-aware latent targets derived from future wrist views, focusing the supervision on the evolving robot-object interactions required for fine-grained manipulation. To better support this control-centric paradigm, LaST1.0 revisits the visual input interface and attention mechanism of its unified architecture. It adopts a vision-encoder-free design with a lightweight patch embedder, exposing fine-grained visual cues directly to the backbone, and introduces LaST-Attention for structured spatiotemporal interactions across modalities. Evaluations across two robot embodiments, five challenging manipulation tasks, and three simulation benchmarks---RLBench, LIBERO, and LIBERO-Plus---demonstrate that LaST1.0 achieves state-of-the-art success rates and generalizes to novel scenarios. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of control-centric latent world modeling for robust manipulation.
  </details>

- **[CoRA-VTLA: Contact-Guided Attention and Queryable Response Memory for Tactile Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23963)**  `arXiv:2611.23963`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chengye Song, Rui Song, Shengqi Wang, Jiawei Zhang, Hongzhou Wang, Xueyang Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In precision manipulation, small errors in alignment or force can cause jamming or deformation, making contact feedback essential for adapting pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models to these tasks. A single tactile observation leaves the response to recent actions ambiguous. Direct tactile fusion during post-training can also disperse the action expert's visual attention and reduce the share it allocates to contact regions. To address these challenges, CoRA-VTLA augments pretrained VLAs with lightweight tactile modules. A queryable action-response memory retrieves recent contact responses so that the action expert can combine them with immediate tactile feedback. Contact-guided gaze supervision concentrates the visual attention of the tactile semantic token on image regions corresponding to fingertip contact. CoRA-VTLA achieves 73.33% mean success across three MuJoCo tasks, compared with 50% for direct tactile fusion with the same sensor inputs. It also achieves 67% success on an optical-tactile task from the public UniVTAC benchmark and 66.7% mean success across three real-robot tasks. These results show that tactile post-training helps pretrained VLAs adjust to changing contact.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Iterative Multi-Agent Path Finding under Turn Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23911)**  `arXiv:2611.23911`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zheyu Tao, Chunlong Yu, Yicheng Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale multi-mobile-robot systems require fast and reliable path planning under realistic motion constraints. Multi-Agent Path Finding with turn actions (MAPF$_T$) explicitly models orientation changes and turn-induced delays, but these constraints increase the computational burden of search-based methods. Priority-inheritance-based planning offers a highly scalable alternative, yet directly extending it to MAPF$_T$ is nontrivial because turn actions break the immediate node-vacation assumption underlying conventional priority inheritance. To address this challenge, this paper proposes PIBT$^*_T$, an efficient iterative solver for MAPF$_T$. PIBT$^*_T$ advances scalable multi-agent coordination by preserving the lightweight single-timestep planning mechanism of Priority Inheritance with Backtracking (PIBT), while introducing dedicated mechanisms to handle turn-induced dependencies, local deadlocks, livelock, and inefficient repeated pushing. Experiments on standard MAPF benchmarks show that PIBT$^*_T$ achieves high success rates and strong scalability, solving instances with up to 1000 agents within seconds. For realistic engineering environments, Action Dependency Graph (ADG)-based execution simulation and a flexible manufacturing system case study show that turn-aware planning better reflects robot execution and reduces actual makespan compared with classical MAPF planning. Experiments with TurtleBot4 robots further validate the practical feasibility of the proposed method.
  </details>

- **[Navigating Robots from Unknown and Uncalibrated Exocentric Cameras via Map Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23894)**  `arXiv:2611.23894`  `cs.RO`  
  _Raul Steinmetz, Raphael Druon, Yusuke Yoshiyasu, Akihisa Ohya, Ayanori Yorozu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exocentric cameras, mounted on walls or ceilings in indoor environments, offer wider vantage points and are less easily occluded than robot-mounted cameras, making them an appealing alternative for perception in robot navigation algorithms. Previous methods that use such cameras, however, require their pose and intrinsics to be known before deployment, demanding calibration that is time-consuming, requires specialized personnel, and must be repeated whenever a camera moves or a new room is navigated. We propose Robot-centric Obstacle Maps from Unknown and uncalibrated eXocentric cameras (ROMUX), an agent that navigates a robot toward point goals while avoiding obstacles using exocentric cameras that are both uncalibrated, meaning the system does not require their pose or intrinsic information as input, and unknown, meaning it is not fit to a specific camera during training and does not require pre-deployment fine-tuning. We train a cross-view transformer on large-scale simulation data to predict metric robot-centric obstacle maps from exocentric images, without conditioning image tokens on camera information. A small policy trained with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) then navigates on the predicted maps toward a goal coordinate relative to the robot. We benchmark our agent in simulation with cameras randomized per episode, and it matches the success rates of a PPO policy that navigates from a robot-mounted camera. Further, ROMUX works with one camera or multiple cameras with partial views and has been transferred zero-shot to a real robot using a camera at varying positions and orientations across various room layouts.
  </details>

- **[CoT-WAM: Collaborative Training of Vision-Language Models and World-Action Models via Plan Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23803)**  `arXiv:2611.23803`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jinhao Zhang, Yushen Zuo, Ke Rui, Weitao Zhou, Haibao Yu, Honglong Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General robot manipulation requires both semantic competence and physical foresight. World action models (WAMs) acquire an understanding of and predictive capability over the physical world by jointly modeling visual foresight and action distributions. However, existing WAMs still lack semantic competence: they have limited ability to perform open-world scene understanding, semantic reasoning, and long-horizon planning. Although vision-language models (VLMs) provide these capabilities, effectively integrating their semantic priors into WAM prediction and control remains challenging. To address this challenge, we propose CoT-WAM, a jointly trained WAM-VLM framework. CoT-WAM uses a learnable embedding to exchange information between the VLM and the WAM, which is supervised by textual and goal-image signals during training to encode rich intent information. A tailored two-stage training strategy enables joint optimization and more effective collaboration between the VLM and WAM. We evaluate CoT-WAM on six benchmarks covering control performance, zero-shot generalization, and long-horizon planning. CoT-WAM achieves SOTA performance on LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboCasa-Kitchen, and demonstrates stronger zero-shot generalization on LIBERO-Plus and LIBERO-Pro without fine-tuning. On RoboCasa365, it further improves long-horizon planning over baselines with and without external planners. Real-world experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in practical deployments.
  </details>

- **[Improve How You Improve: Recursive Policy Self-Improvement for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Multi-Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23774)**  `arXiv:2611.23774`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yang Li, Ziju Han, Aming WU, Zihao Zhang, Sijia Zhang, Yahong Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To advance the development of embodied general navigation, zero-shot open-vocabulary multi-object navigation has attracted increasing attention, aiming to enable robots to efficiently search multiple objects in unseen environments without training. For this task, the key lies in improving navigation policies from earlier searches to locate subsequent goals more efficiently. Recent methods automate this process through trajectory reflection and rule revision, enabling policy self-improvement. However, the mechanisms for diagnosing problems and generating revisions are typically designed by humans and remain fixed across iterations. Consequently, the system's capacity for continued self-improvement remains constrained, even as its navigation policy evolves. To this end, we propose RPSI-Nav, a Recursive Policy Self-Improvement framework for zero-shot multi-object navigation that revises both the navigation program and its revision mechanism. RPSI-Nav diagnoses search behavior, requests diagnostic views when existing observations cannot distinguish competing causes, and generates targeted navigation revisions. Feedback from subsequent goal searches is linked to the rules that produced these revisions, guiding changes to diagnosis, observation acquisition, and revision planning. The updated improver governs subsequent navigation improvements, forming a recursive loop in which RPSI-Nav revises its own improvement process. Across goals, the robot conditionally inherits accepted program updates and spatial memory, enabling search experience to improve both future navigation and adaptation. Benchmark experiments and qualitative real-robot evaluations validate RPSI-Nav's effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[RecallFlow: Episodic Memory as the Source Distribution of Generative Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23731)**  `arXiv:2611.23731`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuji Zhang, Qilin Zhong, Zichen Zhang, Wei Zhai, Jian Di, Kun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow-matching policies have become a standard choice for visuomotor imitation learning, owing to their ability to model multimodal action distributions. In memory-dependent manipulation, the robot must act on information that is no longer visible. Existing methods feed the observation history to the policy as an additional condition, while action generation still starts from Gaussian noise. However, the policy must then pick out the relevant target from a long and redundant history, leading to spurious correlations and unreliable decisions as the memory load grows. Additionally, the initial noise carries no information about what the robot has seen, leaving generation without guidance toward the remembered target. To address these issues, we propose RecallFlow, a flow-policy framework that builds the source distribution from episodic memory. Recall-Relocalization relates the memory to the current view through a few recall slots, so that the source reflects where the remembered targets are now. Recall Anchors then turns each slot into a Gaussian component of the source, enabling generation to start close to the intended action instead of from noise. The transport network then completes the action from the current observation alone, keeping inference nearly as cheap as a memoryless policy. We further build a suite of memory-dependent tasks grouped by memory load and compare all policies under a matched protocol. Extensive experiments show that RecallFlow achieves a state-of-the-art average success rate of 80.4% on the public MIKASA-Robo memory benchmark, and outperforms the strongest baseline by 20.3 points on our suite and by 18.8 points on four real-robot tasks.
  </details>

- **[NIF-VIO: Visual--Inertial Odometry Across Inertial and Non-Inertial Reference Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13662)**  `arXiv:2611.13662`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zichao Shu, Lijun Li, Zhuochen Li, Zetao Chen, Jianyu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional visual--inertial odometry (VIO) assumes that observations from cameras and an inertial measurement unit (IMU) are jointly consistent in an inertial reference frame. However, this assumption breaks down under non-inertial conditions. Inside accelerating or rotating carriers, visual observations of the interior reflect carrier-relative motion, while IMU measurements contain additional carrier-induced components, which can compromise conventional fusion. We therefore present NIF-VIO for localization relative to the local environment across inertial and non-inertial reference frames. It adapts visual--inertial fusion based on measurement consistency. As inertial conditions change, early constraint isolation is combined with local reoptimization to limit error propagation before inconsistency confirmation and repair the affected local geometry. Under non-inertial conditions, a compact model with temporally shared parameters is introduced to account for carrier motion while preserving inertial information that complements visual constraints. Experiments on public benchmarks and a self-collected dataset demonstrate that NIF-VIO achieves competitive performance under inertial conditions and improved localization accuracy and availability under non-inertial conditions.
  </details>

- **[EmbodiedLab: Beyond Manipulation, Benchmarking Embodied Agents That Act to Know](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35385)**  `arXiv:2610.35385`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hongcheng Gao, Zelin Zheng, Alex Zhou, Jay Zhu, Shijia Ge, Yazhe Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical action not only changes the state of an environment but is also the way to know its hidden properties: what cannot be seen must be inferred from the feedback to the change. As embodied agents advance from executing procedures to pursuing goals, success depends on properties neither given nor visible, so they must act to know. Existing benchmarks separate changing from knowing: manipulation benchmarks give the target state, so nothing has to be found out, whereas exploration and discovery benchmarks let the agent find out by looking or querying, so nothing physical has to be changed. Quantitative science experiments offer a natural testbed: the properties sought are new in every instance, appear only in instrument readings under conditions the agent sets up, and can be checked exactly. We introduce EmbodiedLab, a simulated laboratory of 35 such experiments. Given only a goal, the agent must decide what to measure, operate the apparatus with a robot arm, read instruments through cameras and judge when its readings suffice. Its 30 determination tasks specify what to find out, whereas its 5 optimization tasks ask only for the best result the apparatus allows. With native control, the strongest of seven frontier agents succeeds on 41.4% of the tasks. Ablations reveal two bottlenecks: contact-rich manipulation, where ground-truth depth and motion planning change the success rate by at most 10 points, whereas executing every operation for the agent on five of the tasks raises it from at most 20% to at least 70%, so what an agent can know is bounded by how reliably it acts; and closed-loop experimental rigor, where with manipulation supplied, agents fail to gather sufficient readings or read instruments accurately, and most optimization runs stop at one design. Beyond evaluation, EmbodiedLab offers a resamplable environment with verifiable rewards for training embodied agents.
  </details>

- **[SPARSER: Sparse Variable Projection by Exploiting Separable Structure in Robotic Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24708)**  `arXiv:2609.24708`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nikolas R. Sanderson, Andrew Fishberg, Haoyu Han, Heng Yang, Jonathan P. How, Hanumant Singh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic perception often requires solving large nonlinear least-squares (NLS) problems. While sparsity has been well exploited to scale solvers, separability remains underexploited: some variables (e.g., landmarks) appear linearly in the residuals and admit a closed-form solution once the others (e.g., poses) are fixed. Variable projection (VarPro) exploits this by analytically eliminating the linear variables, yielding a reduced problem with favorable properties. However, VarPro is rarely used in robotic perception, largely because gauge symmetries (e.g., invariance to global shifts and rotations), common in perception, complicate standard VarPro approaches. We present SPARSER (Sparsity Preserving Analytic Reduction for Separable Estimation in Robotics), a VarPro scheme for problems with gauge symmetries that jointly exploits separability and sparsity. Via one-time preprocessing, it builds a matrix-free Schur complement operator that evaluates costs, gradients, and Hessian-vector products of the reduced problem and integrates with standard iterative NLS solvers. We characterize the applicable problem class and common special cases admitting further analytical savings. The method extends to robust costs via iteratively reweighted least squares (IRLS): symbolic preprocessing is reused across reweightings, so each IRLS iteration needs only a numerical refactorization. On 42 benchmark datasets spanning pose-graph optimization, range-aided SLAM, sensor network localization, and structure from motion, SPARSER is fastest on 41 on CPU and 35 on GPU, and is the only method to solve all. On 24 real-world multi-robot SLAM datasets with outlier-corrupted loop closures, our robust variant is the only method to solve all and is fastest on each, running 1.1-3.1x faster than the graduated non-convexity (GNC) solver in GTSAM. We release open-source C++ code and all datasets.
  </details>

- **[Real-Time QP Solvers: A Concise Review and Practical Guide Towards Legged Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21773)**  `arXiv:2510.21773`  `cs.RO`  
  _Van Nam Dinh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quadratic programming (QP) underpins real-time robotics by enabling efficient, constrained optimization in state estimation, motion planning, and control. In legged locomotion and manipulation, essential modules like inverse dynamics, Model Predictive Control (MPC), and Whole-Body Control (WBC) are inherently QP-based, demanding reliable solutions amid tight timing, energy, and computational resources on embedded platforms. This paper presents a comprehensive analysis and benchmarking study of QP solvers for legged robotics. We begin by formulating the standard convex QP and classify solvers into principal algorithmic approaches: interior-point methods, active-set strategies, operator-splitting schemes, and augmented Lagrangian/proximal approaches, while also discussing solver code generation for fixed-structure QPs. Each solver is examined in terms of algorithmic structure, computational characteristics, and its ability to exploit problem structure and warm-starting. Performance is reviewed using publicly available benchmarks, with a focus on metrics such as computation time, constraint satisfaction, and robustness under perturbations. Unified comparison tables yield practical guidance for solver selection, underscoring trade-offs in speed, accuracy, and energy efficiency. Our findings emphasize the synergy between solvers, tasks, and hardware -- e.g., sparse structured IPMs for long-horizon MPC and dense active-set for high-frequency WBC to advance agile, autonomous legged systems, with emerging trends toward ill-conditioned, conic, and code-generated deployments.
  </details>

- **[Kaer: Security-Aware Replanning for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24839)**  `arXiv:2611.24839`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Tushin Mallick, Evan Rose, Matthew Laws, Alina Oprea, Cristina Nita-Rotaru_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One popular architecture for multi-agent systems (MAS) relies on an orchestrator that generates an initial plan, coordinates its execution, and dynamically revises the plan based on agent feedback when tasks cannot be fully specified in advance. This dynamic replanning introduces a critical vulnerability: malicious agents can embed instructions within their feedback to hijack the replanning process. Existing defenses focus exclusively on per-tool access control or static, pre-execution policy enforcement, leaving the replanning phase unsecured.We introduce Kaer, a multi-agent framework featuring security-aware replanning. Kaer shields the planner from untrusted agent feedback by conveying diagnostics to the replanning procedure strictly through abstract templates. Plan revisions are restricted to a set of deterministic repair operators, and all modified plans must satisfy preset policies. We create Cintra, a benchmark of simulated user tasks requiring replanning paired with adversarial objectives targeting the replanning phase. Using Cintra, we evaluate both task completion rates and system vulnerability to replanning exploits by malicious agents. Evaluating Kaer on Cintra against Magentic-One (an undefended MAS) and ControlValve (the state-of-the-art defense) demonstrates that Kaer achieves higher robustness than both systems while maintaining comparable utility to ControlValve. Although abstract failure templates slightly constrain utility on select tasks, Kaer enables MAS to perform secure, dynamic plan revisions without exposing the orchestrator to untrusted agent feedback.
  </details>

- **[TRACE-MAS: Diagnosing and Repairing Collaboration Failures in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23362)**  `arXiv:2611.23362`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Zekai Chen, Yihan Sun, Xunkai Li, Xun Wu, Xinmo Jin, Yinlin Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on not only individual agent behaviors but also the interactions that connect agents, including message exchanges and task dependencies. Existing MAS safeguards mainly perform agent-level anomaly detection, which becomes insufficient when failures originate from agent interactions and detected anomalies do not necessarily reveal their true sources. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{TRACE-MAS}, a finer-grained framework for diagnosing and repairing MAS failures. TRACE-MAS learns normal collaborative execution patterns from benign trajectories and detects deviations in agent state changes, message transitions, and task dependency evolutions. It further identifies responsible execution elements through source-aware attribution and selects minimal interventions to restore collaboration while preserving task-critical capabilities. Experiments on five MAS benchmarks demonstrate that TRACE-MAS significantly improves root-cause localization accuracy by \textbf{60.2}\% and achieves consistent gains in task recovery over strong MAS safeguards.
  </details>

- **[Survival of the Fittest Perturbations: Uncertainty-Aware Evolution of Adversarial Distributions for Robust Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02871)**  `arXiv:2411.02871`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Junhao Dong, Zicheng Yu, Xinghua Qu, Z. Jane Wang, Yew-Soon Ong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite deep learning's broad successes across diverse scenarios, its vulnerability to adversarial examples impedes deployment, making adversarial training a leading approach to improve robustness. However, pointwise adversarial training maps each clean sample to a single adversary, overlooking surrounding adversarial variability and the associated shifts in feature statistics. To address these issues, we propose Evolutionary Uncertainty-Aware Distributional Adversarial Training (EvoUAD-AT). Specifically, we design an evolutionary adversarial uncertainty modeling module that maintains an instance-wise search distribution over adversarial feature statistics around an adversarially generated anchor. Candidates are evaluated under the current model, selected by their threat to a benign reference, and used to adapt the distribution; strategy parameters are retained across epochs. To avoid anchoring on misclassified clean samples, we refine the alignment reference and match benign and adversarial distributions. Introspective gradient matching further regularizes input gradients without external supervision. Our analysis characterizes the elite fitness surrogate and gives a conditional certificate for latent adversarial features. Extensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in both clean accuracy and robustness, with thorough analyses confirming the effectiveness of each proposed component.
  </details>

- **[SANTA++: Sampling Attention through Representative Keys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35629)**  `arXiv:2609.35629`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Kyle Lee, Christian Z. Pratt, Ruoyu Fang, Heekyung Lee, Avinash Lohitsa, Ryan Modafe, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention often concentrates on a small subset of tokens in the context, but which subset matters changes from one query to the next. To exploit this changing structure, we introduce SANTA++, a training-free stochastic attention method that uses representative keys for memory-efficient selection without scanning the entire key-value (KV) cache. Cached keys are organized into teams, and the query scores one representative from each team to decide which teams to sample. We compute exact attention scores within the sampled teams and reweight each team's contribution by the inverse of its inclusion probability. This importance sampling correction estimates attention over the full cache, with a sampling budget that lets us trade memory reads for accuracy. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at 32K context, contiguous SANTA++ at S=1024 retains 95-98% of dense aggregate scores on LongBench v2, HELMET RAG, and RULER using 28-31% logical KV access. On RULER, SANTA++ outperforms Quest across the tested accuracy-access sweep, using 48.7% fewer KV reads at approximately 79% accuracy. It also outperforms MagicPIG at low access. At S=1024, our Triton implementation gives a 1.38x attention-operator speedup over the Flash backend of PyTorch scaled dot-product attention (Flash SDPA) in FP16. At batch eight, full-model decoding is 1.23x faster than dense Flash SDPA with matched INT4 model weights and FP16 KV-cache precision on an RTX 5090 Laptop GPU. Our kernels are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Meta-RL with Bayesian Linear Task Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20974)**  `arXiv:2512.20974`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Jingyang You, Hanna Kurniawati_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep Bayesian reinforcement learning adapts to unseen tasks by inferring latent transition and reward models, but existing methods typically rely on variational posteriors and evidence lower bounds, introducing approximation error and unstable task representations. We introduce GLiBRL, a deep Bayesian RL framework that combines generalised linear task models with learnable non-linear basis functions. GLiBRL features conjugate Bayesian inference, yielding exact, sequential posterior updates over task parameters and model noise, together with a closed-form marginal likelihood that eliminates variational inference. The update is naturally permutation-invariant, allowing GLiBRL to integrate with both off- and on-policy algorithms. GLiBRL also learns task representation admitting an exact kernel identity, relating distances between task representations to kernel discrepancies over the task contexts. Compared against eight representative or recent meta reinforcement learning methods, GLiBRL achieves the highest aggregate zero-shot test performance on both the MuJoCo locomotion and MetaWorld manipulation benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Openhagen Worlds: Calibrated Environments for Measuring and Training Scientific Discovery Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24711)**  `arXiv:2611.24711`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Alexander Boesgaard Lorup_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents that do science must choose informative experiments and reason from their results, but most discovery benchmarks cannot separate that skill from recall, unstated scoring rules or shortcut policies. We introduce Openhagen Worlds, a suite of 27 procedurally generated environments (some with finite truth spaces), from classic puzzles of experimental reasoning to simulator-grounded physics and chemistry, in which an agent runs experiments under a budget and commits an answer that a deterministic verifier checks against a hidden truth. Every observation states the complete scoring rule, and each of the 178 difficulty levels is calibrated: it is admitted only if, on 64 development instances, a scripted oracle succeeds on at least 0.75, the strongest plausible non-deductive policy on at most 0.25 and each gated shortcut on at most 0.35. The 92 core levels span all 27 families and are sized to train small models; the 86 frontier rungs extend the 15 discovery worlds with harder questions and with campaigns of three, five and eight or more committed stages on one hidden system. On the same 84 withheld frontier instances, one per family and rung, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash solves 32 at its default reasoning effort with 16,384 tokens per call and 57 at its maximum effort with up to 393,216 tokens per call (oracle: 79); the headroom that remains is largest in eight-stage campaigns. On-policy expert iteration gives a 9B model modest gains: its core-tier success on trained families rises from 0.090 to 0.148, partly through better action format, with no detectable loss on GPQA Diamond. A hosted service plays the core tier's withheld instances.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Fixed-Horizon Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24001)**  `arXiv:2611.24001`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tianxiang Chen, Jingyuan Zhou, Weiming Yang, Kaidi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing latent reasoners typically run for a preset latent horizon during inference. Intuitively, a longer horizon should provide more opportunity for reasoning. However, in this work, we find that extending latent Chain-of-Thought (CoT) to the full preset horizon may in fact degrade reasoning performance. We observe that latent CoT evolves through distinct functional stages, with earlier computation forming frontier reasoning states, while later computation mainly reorganizes existing candidates and can weaken gold candidate support. These findings motivate a rethink of fixed-horizon latent inference. To address this, we characterize the reasoning benefits of latent CoT by realized and remaining constructive progress. Empirically, we find that higher realized constructive progress corresponds to higher current reasoning accuracy, while more remaining progress corresponds to larger future gains from continued latent CoT. To this end, we introduce a lightweight method that estimates the remaining reasoning progress and adaptively adjusts the latent horizon during inference. Across representative latent CoT reasoners and multiple arithmetic benchmarks, our method improves reasoning accuracy by up to 7.2 percentage points while reducing latent computation by up to 29.0%, without retraining the LLM.
  </details>

- **[Learning through Discernment: when a small language model learns from user feedback on the job, and when it decays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23716)**  `arXiv:2611.23716`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sirisilp Kongsilp_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models on devices could keep improving by learning, overnight, from their own daily work. Without a true outcome, however, something must decide which attempts are worth learning from, and both the model's own judgment and the user's reaction can be wrong. We study a small model (Qwen3 0.6B and 1.7B) with a LoRA adapter, fine-tuned each night on the attempts a judge accepts, over 127 lives of up to a year on WebShop and $\tau^2$-bench. We compare the true outcome, the user's stated verdict and the model's own judgment with a separate, frozen "feedback sense" that reads the user's reaction, built in three versions. Judging itself, the model improved and then decayed, over a year and whatever its adapter. A sense that took polite give-ups for success collapsed 3 of 5 lives; one built for discernment, which first asks whether the user gave a clear verdict, kept 4 of 5 near the oracle for two weeks, then faded on clean but scarce lessons, to the level of self-judging by day 60. When the user cannot see the outcome ($\tau^2$-bench), the sense read reactions correctly, yet 80% of its lessons were failures the customer had thanked. Across 97 lives, the share of failed attempts among the lessons predicted the outcome (Spearman $\rho$ = -0.81). In our experiments, learning from users held up only where the judge kept failures out and the user could see the outcome; lopsided lessons may also narrow the model.
  </details>

- **[Communicative On-Policy Distillation: Distilling Black-Box Teacher Capabilities through Communication for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23673)**  `arXiv:2611.23673`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Lirui Luo, Kelong Mao, Xinwei Yang, Rongqing Li, Luyu Chen, Kieran Wong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is a powerful paradigm for improving language-model agents on long-horizon tasks, yet its conventional formulation relies on teacher logits that text-only black- box models do not expose. To address this limitation, we introduce Communicative On-Policy Distillation (COPD), which uses natural-language communication as a medium for distilling a stronger model's capabilities into a smaller student. During task execution, the student au- tonomously decides when to consult a black-box teacher and formulates open-ended natural- language requests without predefined question templates or intervention schedules. Successful student-teacher interactions are retained as privileged references to supervise fresh adviser-free student rollouts through on-policy self-distillation, jointly with task-reward reinforcement learning. On CAMG-II, COPD achieves 2.46 times the gain of the strongest black-box OPD baseline. The capabilities it learns through communication generalize to Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SWE-Bench Pro V2, improving task success without adviser access.
  </details>

- **[MINT: Meeting-time Indicators for Truncation in Multi-Step Off-Policy RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24413)**  `arXiv:2611.24413`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Seungyub Han, Taehyun Cho, Dohyeong Kim, Kyungjae Lee, Jungwoo Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many multi-step off-policy temporal-difference methods handle policy mismatch through products of per-step trace coefficients, leading to a trade-off between variance control and long-horizon signal. The maximum trace length consequently becomes a sensitive hyperparameter. We propose MINT (Meeting-time INdicators for Truncation), which replaces these products with a binary stopping indicator: one until coupled behavior and target trajectories first meet, zero thereafter. At the true value function, the indicator's idempotence yields a variance bound governed by meeting-time survival. For practical implementation, a single sampled action provides a model-free surrogate for the stopping rule, requiring no transition model or mixing-time estimate. We derive separate sufficient conditions for contraction of the state-coupled operator and its practical surrogate under fixed-policy evaluation. On MuJoCo and DeepMind Control tasks, MINT outperforms the reported multi-step baselines; the main MuJoCo comparison uses a common cap without per-task cap-length tuning. We also demonstrate the effectiveness of MINT on Atari 100k, RWRL, and OGBench experiments; MINT adapts the backup horizon to reduce target variability relative to long fixed-horizon backups across tasks and dynamics.
  </details>

- **[The 7th International Verification of Neural Networks Competition (VNN-COMP 2026): Summary and Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24114)**  `arXiv:2611.24114`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tobias Ladner, Konstantin Kaulen, Stanley Bak, Julien Girard-Satabin, Taylor T. Johnson, Edoardo Manino, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This report summarizes the seventh International Verification of Neural Networks Com- petition (VNN-COMP 2026), held as part of the Symposium on AI Verification (SAIV) co-located with the International Conference on Computer-Aided Verification (CAV) at FLoC 2026 in Lisbon, Portugal. The goal of the competition is to provide an objective comparison of the state-of-the-art methods in neural network verification, in terms of scal- ability and speed. Along this line, we used standard formats (ONNX for neural networks and VNNLIB for specifications), standard hardware (all tools are run by the organizers on AWS), and tool parameters provided by the tool authors. This report summarizes the rules, benchmarks, participating tools, results, and lessons learned from this competition.
  </details>

- **[FairPGT: Partitioned Graph Transformers for Dual-Level Fair Graph Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23631)**  `arXiv:2611.23631`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Siamak Ghodsi, Amjad Seyedi, Sahar Tahmasebi, Eirini Ntoutsi, Sören Auer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clustering on attributed graphs can produce demographically biased clusters, particularly when communities align with protected groups. Graph transformers can amplify this bias through same-group attention, while global attention limits scalability. We propose FairPGT, a partitioned graph transformer for fair graph clustering. FairPGT restricts attention to METIS partitions and connects them through routing tokens, which divides the quadratic cost of attention by the number of partitions. A graph-aware self-training target guides clustering, with a provably bounded deviation from the standard DEC target. Fairness is imposed at two levels: a mutual-information term promotes demographic parity in cluster assignments, while a normalized cross-group attention ratio (nCGAR) guides attention toward group neutrality through self-distillation. We prove that the nCGAR correction moves attention toward neutrality and establish a condition preventing overshooting. Across seven benchmarks, FairPGT achieves the best clustering performance and statistical parity on the three largest graphs (Credit, Pokec-n, Pokec-z), with a statistical-parity gap approximately ten times lower than DMoN, and is Pareto-dominated on only one dataset. On controlled stochastic block models, FairPGT attains 97% of the derived upper bound on NMI for exactly fair clustering when communities and protected groups coincide. These graphs further show that, under strong alignment, assignment-level fairness alone makes the attention group-heterophilous, whereas attention-level fairness alone improves neutrality without ensuring fair clusters. Code for reproducibility is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing World-Model Explanations in Retail Forecasting: Faithfulness, Accuracy and Price Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20811)**  `arXiv:2611.20811`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Karthik Iyer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting models that read the price and promotion plan explain each forecast as a baseline, what would sell with nothing planned, plus the lift the plan adds, and merchants order stock and plan promotions on that lift. We show that an explanation can pass the checks used to trust it and still be wrong enough to cost money. We define eight checks for any forecaster that states a plan lift, six with a pass rule fixed before any result, and run them on a world model and on LightGBM. In generated store data with known effects, the world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet when merchants plan into high-demand weeks it states 1.33 to 1.40 times the true lift, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world; LightGBM overstates it too (1.26, and 1.16 to 1.35). Both still pick the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to the world model's lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. The world model's stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, an error in kind that only a measured cause finds. Sanity checks for explanations test whether an explanation depends on the model; a decision needs to know whether it is right about the world, and these results show the first does not give the second. For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan on every window, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05). A noisy benchmark of price episodes in Dominick's data is reported as a check of direction only.
  </details>

- **[TabBench-Bio: A Living Benchmark for Machine Learning on High-Dimensional Biomedical Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07441)**  `arXiv:2609.07441`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jules Kreuer, Sofiane Ouaari, Julia Hellmig, Julius Braitinger, Nico Pfeifer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical tables often combine thousands of measured variables with only tens or hundreds of labelled samples, a regime that is poorly represented in general-purpose tabular benchmarks. We introduce TabBench-Bio, a living and interactive benchmark of 43 biomedical datasets spanning multiple domains. Under a shared cross-validation protocol, we compare classical estimators, neural networks, and tabular foundation models across 28 feature-by-sample operating points. At the reference cell of 10,000 features and 100 training samples, RealTabPFN 2.5 has the highest point estimate, closely followed by TabPFN 3 and Logistic Regression. A paired bootstrap over the target pool separates RealTabPFN 2.5 from TabPFN 3 by 87 Elo (95\% interval [43, 129]).Tabular foundation models generally occupy the leading ranks, while the strongest configuration depends on the operating point and biomedical modality. The AutoML framework AutoGluon, using its one-hour "extreme" preset, is configured as a separate resource-intensive reference and reported here at the reference and full cell. Fold-level predictions, run status, and deterministic aggregations make every reported result reproducible and reusable. The benchmark is open to contributions of new biomedical datasets.The interactive leaderboard is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Measuring What Matters: Synthetic Benchmarks for Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04326)**  `arXiv:2606.04326`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Julian Skirzynski, Harry Cheon, Shreyas Kadekodi, Meredith Stewart, Berk Ustun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept bottleneck models predict using high-level concepts detected in inputs. Although these models provide a simple way to reap benefits from interpretability, they are hard to train and evaluate because very few real-world datasets include concept labels. In this paper, we develop synthetic benchmarks for concept bottleneck models. With our benchmarks, researchers can generate labeled datasets and control properties that affect performance, such as data modality, the choice of concepts, or annotation quality. They can thus find problems that suit these models and isolate the factors behind their failures. We demonstrate how our benchmarks can be used to evaluate major use cases for concept bottleneck models: decision support, i.e., helping human experts make better decisions; and automation, i.e., handling routine tasks without supervision. Our demonstrations show how the benchmarks can identify new failure modes and attribute them to either the architecture or the data.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Path-Specific Fairness Across Causal Discovery Algorithms: A Heart Failure Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15926)**  `arXiv:2603.15926`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Nitish Nagesh, Elahe Khatibi, Thomas Hughes, Mahdi Bagheri, Pratik Gajane, Amir M. Rahmani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal discovery is increasingly applied to healthcare data, where the recovered relationships can encode biased pathways that propagate to downstream prediction. Path-specific fairness analysis, through direct, indirect, and spurious effect decompositions along with fairness-utility ratio assessment allows bias quantification and associated prediction cost. However, existing evaluations of causal discovery under fairness constraints have largely used non-clinical datasets and have not separated the contribution of confounders from that of mediators, leaving open how discovery-algorithm choice propagates through path-specific fairness to the utility trade-off in clinical settings. This work addresses that gap by establishing an expert-elicited causal graph benchmark for the heart failure clinical records dataset and tracing discovery algorithms through composite, per-variable fairness decompositions and fairness-utility ratio. ICA-LiNGAM achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance and highest recall, while GES achieves the highest precision. For path-specific effects, there was a 1.2 percentage-point difference in the mediated effect on ejection fraction on overall causal fairness between the original and discovered graphs. This ultimately led to variations in the fairness-utility ratio highlighting need for fine-grained evaluation. These results indicate that discovery-aware, path-specific fairness evaluation is consequential in clinical settings and warrants inclusion in routine auditing of clinical risk models.
  </details>

- **[On Evaluating User Simulators for Interactive Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24892)**  `arXiv:2611.24892`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hengguang Zhou, I-Hung Hsu, Li Chen, Priyanshi Shrivastava, Anastasios Angelopoulos, Wei-Lin Chiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User simulators offer a scalable way to evaluate interactive agents, but how to assess their quality remains underexplored. In particular, it is unclear whether existing user simulator evaluation frameworks reliably detect meaningful failures in simulated user behavior. This uncertainty makes it difficult to interpret simulator evaluation. To understand what a high benchmark score tells us about simulator quality, we (meta-)evaluate the metrics themselves. To instantiate this meta-evaluation, we deliberately perturb simulated replays to induce failures such as altered goals, omitted constraints, premature stopping, and acceptance of broken work, then test whether the metrics penalize these failures.
