Evaluation methods from three prior studies detect only 5-6 of the 10 perturbations; their combined scores even favor populations instructed to accept broken work over unmodified controls. Motivated by these gaps, we propose a new evaluation suite that combines utility of user simulators for evaluating agents and two measures of fidelity to real users: population-level fidelity assesses collective interaction patterns, while trace-level fidelity assesses the preservation of individual users' goals, constraints, and persona. Under the same detection criterion, our evaluation suite detects all 10 perturbations. Evaluating six user simulators on 351 real multi-turn sessions in which 348 distinct users across 23 languages and agents work together to create and refine artifacts such as full-stack web apps, games, design assets, and automated workflows, we find that population-level measures can miss when a simulated user pursues the wrong goal, ignores a constraint, or behaves out of character, highlighting the need to assess fidelity at comprehensive granularities.
  </details>

- **[Atomic MathReasoner: Instance-Level Composition of Atomic Reasoning Skills for Mathematical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24846)**  `arXiv:2611.24846`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiashu Li, Tianyu Gao, Qinglong Liu, Jinping Wang, Ye Wei, Zhiqiang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most existing approaches enhance mathematical reasoning in large language models (LLMs) as a monolithic capability, despite the fact that different mathematical problems require different reasoning skills. Recent work has explored a more fine-grained perspective by decomposing mathematical reasoning into atomic skills and developing specialized modules for individual capabilities. However, how to effectively utilize these atomic abilities remains underexplored. To address this challenge, we propose Atomic MathReasoner (AMR), an instance-level framework for composing atomic reasoning skills. AMR first trains a library of capability-specific LoRA experts and performs gradient-free optimization over held-out validation subsets to discover a diverse set of low-loss composition prototypes. It evaluates the preference of each prototype using the token-level negative log-likelihood of reference solutions, filters ambiguous assignments through a confidence margin, and distills the resulting pseudo-labels into a lightweight router. During inference, the router selects an appropriate composition prototype for each problem, enabling problem-specific utilization of atomic skills without costly online optimization. Experiments on the AtomicMath benchmark across general and mathematics-specialized LLMs demonstrate consistent improvements. On Qwen2.5-Math-Instruct-7B, AMR increases average field accuracy from 64.4% to 68.1% at Level 1 and from 59.3% to 63.7% at Level 2, while improving average logical reasoning performance from 39.4% to 46.8%. These results demonstrate that effectively composing specialized atomic skills provides a promising approach to strengthening mathematical reasoning in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[What Survival Benchmarks Don't Tell You: Impact of Model Selection and Dataset Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24837)**  `arXiv:2611.24837`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sandra Dening, Emre Kavak, Christian Wachinger, George H. Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the proliferation of survival analysis methods, current benchmarks often operate under narrow conditions: they heavily rely on the concordance index (C-index) for evaluation and model selection, and frequently either exclude deep learning (DL) methods or underrepresent challenging dataset regimes. To address these limitations, we conduct a large-scale, neutral benchmark comparing 10 classical, tree-based, and DL methods across 49 diverse datasets. Crucially, we treat the model selection criterion (optimizing for C-index, Integrated Brier Score (IBS), or Mean Absolute Error (MAE-PO)) as an independent experimental variable. Using Bayesian mixed-effects modeling, we demonstrate that evaluation design heavily dictates conclusions. For complex DL and tree-based architectures, selecting hyperparameters to optimize for IBS instead of C-index yields substantial improvements in probabilistic accuracy and calibration at a negligible cost to discriminative ranking. Furthermore, method performance (C-index, IBS, MAE-PO, and X-CAL) is fundamentally governed by dataset characteristics. While DL models scale exceptionally well with larger sample sizes, they suffer notable performance drops in high-dimensional feature spaces. Additionally, we expose a structural vulnerability where heavy censoring artificially inflates absolute calibration metrics, creating overly conservative tests. Ultimately, our findings challenge the utility of global single-number performance summaries and advocate for survival analysis results that transparently report the evaluation setup. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Morpho-Spectral Projection Analysis (MSPA) for Plant Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24599)**  `arXiv:2611.24599`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lucas Prado Osco, Marcelo Santos da Silva, Filipe Vieira Silva, Liang Zhao, Ana Paula Marques Ramos, Ednaldo Jose Ferreira, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plant reflectance spectroscopy across the visible, near-infrared, and shortwave infrared domain encodes foliar biochemical and anatomical traits into contiguous spectral signatures with locally differentiable structure; however, extreme inter-band collinearity and unfavorable sample-to-feature ratios challenge predictive modeling. Conventional dimensionality reduction methods routinely adopted in spectroscopy, are column-permutation invariant: they treat adjacent wavelengths as unordered tabular columns and discard the sequential differential geometry of absorption features. Here, we propose Morpho-Spectral Projection Analysis (MSPA), a supervised dimensionality reduction operator that projects contiguous spectra into a compact morpho-spectral subspace through three complementary blocks: (i) additive continuum-removed absorption depth, area, and red-edge inflection kinematics; (ii) local multi-order singular eigenmodes (0th, 1st, and 2nd derivatives) partitioned across contiguous radiative sub-windows; and (iii) global Fisher-weighted canonical subspaces regularized via trace-normalized isotropic covariance shrinkage. We validated MSPA across 8,963 spectra spanning five plant systems, coupled with six classical machine learning classifiers and four Tabular Foundation Models (TabPFN v3, TabICL v2, TabDPT v1.2, and TabFM). Despite compressing the 1,580-band spectrum by 96%, MSPA outperformed the full spectrum in 92.0% of all benchmarks and conventional reducers in 90.0% of all model-crop evaluations, raising the global mean macro-F1 from 0.7756 (full spectrum) to 0.8520, accelerating in-context inference by up to 126.5x. Designed as a self-contained spectral projection operator, the MSPA method can be directly integrated into spectroscopy workflows via the open-source code available at GitHub:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Activity Recognition to Shift Detection: Variational Wi-Fi Sensing with Evidential Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24596)**  `arXiv:2611.24596`  `cs.LG`  
  _Luca Cotti, Marco Cominelli, Francesco Gringoli, Lance M. Kaplan, Mani B. Srivastava, Trevor Bihl, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human motion perturbs wireless channels, making commodity Wi-Fi devices a practical platform for device-free activity recognition. Yet Channel State Information (CSI) also depends strongly on the person, receiver position, recording session, and surrounding environment, so a classifier can continue producing activity labels after its deployment conditions have moved away from those seen during training. We propose Wi-HAR, a multi-antenna architecture in which four Variational Auto-Encoders learn compact distributions from raw Wi-Fi 6 CSI magnitudes and a delayed-fusion classifier combines their means and log-variances. On twelve activities from a public benchmark, Wi-HAR reaches 96.6% median recognition accuracy, compared with 70.8-81.5% for independently optimized single-antenna models, showing that the antennas provide complementary views of the same activity. We then use the same latent backbone to detect deployment shifts without using shifted samples for model selection or calibration. A class-conditional uncertainty score derived from Evidential Deep Learning achieves a macro-average ROC-AUC of 0.897 across eleven datasets that change receiver position, participant, recording session, or room, and detects 82.5% of shifted samples at a realized 5.2% in-distribution rejection rate. Detection performance nevertheless varies substantially across receiver positions, showing that the same physical change does not induce a uniform uncertainty response. The compact representation also supports shallow decision-tree analysis using variables from several antennas.
  </details>

- **[ICICLE: In-Context Inference for Curve-Learning Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24469)**  `arXiv:2611.24469`  `cs.LG`  
  _Laurits Fredsgaard, Michael Riis Andersen, Mikkel N. Schmidt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Freeze-thaw Bayesian optimization allocates training budget by predicting how partially observed learning curves will continue. LC-PFN first applied prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) to learning-curve extrapolation, and FT-PFN extended this approach to configuration-aware freeze-thaw optimization with a ten-slot hyperparameter encoder. We introduce In-Context Inference for Curve-Learning Extrapolation (ICICLE), an in-context surrogate that accepts a variable number of hyperparameters through a shared set encoder and is trained on a synthetic prior covering typed hyperparameters, heavy-tailed noise, quantization, and divergence. ICICLE has 1.28 million parameters (8.7% of FT-PFN's count) and produces similar mean-regret trajectories to FT-PFN on LCBench, PD1, and TaskSet. On the 86-task Meta-Album benchmark with 33 hyperparameters, ICICLE reaches lower mean normalized regret than Quick-Tune overall and on extended, with no clear difference on mini or micro. It does so without pre-training or meta-training on Meta-Album tasks, whereas Quick-Tune is meta-trained on related Meta-Album tasks. Our release samples synthetic training tasks on the fly and provides a freeze-thaw scheduler for Ray Tune and an ONNX model for inference without JAX or PyTorch.
  </details>

- **[DyNA-Mixer: Decoupling Time and Topology for Scalable Dynamic Node Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24426)**  `arXiv:2611.24426`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuanxing Zheng, Liping Wang, Xuemin Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time dynamic graphs preserve the ordering, timing, and evolving strength of interactions that static snapshots discard, making dynamic graph mining essential for evolving systems such as recommender systems, trade networks, and transaction platforms. Research in this area has centered on dynamic link prediction, which forecasts only whether a future interaction will occur, whereas many applications also need its intensity. The dynamic node affinity prediction (DNAP) problem generalizes this task to continuous targets and full-candidate ranking, making it more broadly applicable but substantially harder. This raises two coupled difficulties: ranking requires a predictor expressive enough to capture both multi-scale temporal evolution and graph-wide collaborative patterns, yet running such a predictor over every source--destination pair is computationally prohibitive. We propose DyNA-Mixer, which decouples temporal forecasting from collaborative structure modeling. It adopts a multi-scale temporal predictor for pair-specific affinity evolution, constructs a Virtual Collaborative Graph that infers query-time relationships and aggregates trends from related interactions, and fuses the two through a gated residual mechanism. A sparsity-aware evaluation strategy further restricts expensive forecasting to pairs with recent non-zero affinities. Experiments on four Temporal Graph Benchmark datasets for the DNAP problem show that DyNA-Mixer achieves new state-of-the-art NDCG@10 on every dataset, including a 17.8\% relative test improvement over the strongest baseline on tgbn-token, the largest dataset.
  </details>

- **[RSIBench-Infra: Can Agents Optimize Their Own Training and Inference Infrastructure at Production Scale?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24395)**  `arXiv:2611.24395`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hanlin Wu, Yifei Wang, Zhicheng Cai, Huan-ang Gao, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving the efficiency of training and inference infrastructure is a practical component of recursive self-improvement. Existing benchmarks for agent-driven optimization largely focus on individual software layers such as kernel, leaving unclear whether agents can deliver end-to-end performance gains under production workloads. We introduce RSIBench-Infra, a benchmark of 20 training and inference tasks spanning diverse model families and modalities, with deployments of up to 32 accelerators on NVIDIA GPUs and Ascend NPUs. Agents start from runnable baselines derived from official recipes and can modify the software stack across engine, kernel, and instruction levels. An OCI image-layer submission protocol captures these changes for reproducible deployment, while independent evaluation measures end-to-end performance under task-specific quality constraints. Across all task results, 66.2% exceed $1.10\times$ validated speedup. These results demonstrate that current agents can improve production-scale training and inference performance, but identifying effective optimization strategies and consistently translating local improvements into end-to-end gains remain challenging.
  </details>

- **[Distribution-Free Uncertainty Quantification for Weather Forecasting: Conformal Prediction Under Spatiotemporal Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24329)**  `arXiv:2611.24329`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mariya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal prediction gives finite-sample, distribution-free marginal coverage and has begun to be applied to gridded weather forecasts. Marginal coverage concerns the long-run fraction of covered points at a location, not how coverage behaves jointly across space and time on a given day. I benchmark eight interval-construction procedures (Gaussian, pooled and per-cell split conformal, per-season, adaptive conformal inference and rolling windows) for gridded 2 m temperature forecasts at eight lead times from 6 hours to 10 days, using ERA5 at 5.625 degree resolution, a deliberately modest convolutional forecaster and ECMWF's operational HRES, with calibration in 2017-2018 and testing in 2019-2021. Alongside coverage, width and interval score, I report dependence diagnostics: a spatial overdispersion index, quantiles of daily spatial coverage and miss persistence relative to a shuffled-days null. Per-cell methods reach near-nominal marginal coverage, yet the day-to-day variance of the fraction of cells covered is roughly 3 to 10 times what independent cells would give, and misses cluster in time; both effects grow with lead time for the CNN, also appear for HRES, and are shared by all methods. An oracle that divides each day's normalised errors by that day's mean removes 81 to 92 percent of the excess dispersion for the CNN and 93 to 98 percent for HRES, so days of large errors across much of the grid account for most of it. A day-scale predicted from seasonal terms and already-verified errors lowers the overdispersion index without widening intervals but improves interval score by at most 0.6 percent; on HRES from 12 hours to 3 days it has the lowest overdispersion index while ACI or season-conditional calibration has the best interval score, so no method is best on both. The study covers one variable, one coarse grid, a reanalysis target and two forecasters.
  </details>

- **[TabBioMed: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Biomedical Tabular Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23779)**  `arXiv:2611.23779`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pau Mateo-Bernadó, Saivenkata Nagavyjayanthi Polapragada, David Bonet, Pol Arbiol Rakuljic, Laia Mogas Pladevall, Marçal Comajoan Cara, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in machine learning have accelerated biomedical prediction, but evaluation on structured biomedical data remains fragmented, especially for datasets with high dimensionality, large sample size, missingness, and class imbalance. We introduce TabBioMed, a large-scale benchmark of 94 curated public biomedical tabular datasets spanning electronic health records, drug discovery, genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, metabolomics, single-cell omics, and systems biology. TabBioMed unifies these datasets through a standardized, dataset-aware preprocessing and evaluation framework, enabling reproducible comparison across classification, regression, and multi-target tasks. We benchmark 23 models across classical baselines, gradient-boosted trees, neural tabular architectures, and tabular foundation models. Foundation models deliver the strongest family-level predictive performance. However, their gains are regime-dependent: tree-based models and tuned multilayer perceptrons remain highly competitive, often dominating under inference-time or training-cost constraints. Overall, TabBioMed shows that biomedical tabular learning is strongly context-dependent, with optimal model choice depending on data regime, task type, and computational budget. We release TabBioMed with curated datasets and splits, preprocessing code, baselines, and results, providing a reproducible benchmark for practical model selection and future methods development in biomedical machine learning, available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Normal-Only Updates Are Not Enough: Test-Time Adaptation in Reconstruction-Based Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23761)**  `arXiv:2611.23761`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jianhuan Mao, Mengxiao Zhu, Yue Pei, Lei Li, Haogang Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Normal operating conditions can evolve after deployment, motivating test-time adaptation (TTA) of time-series anomaly detectors using incoming data. Yet the same stream may contain anomalies, so TTA with online updates must decide which test samples should be used for adaptation. This leaves a more basic question: even if updates are restricted to samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, does TTA reliably improve anomaly detection? We study this question with a controlled intervention that replaces the practical update selector with a rule that uses only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, while keeping the rest of the online adaptation procedure unchanged. Across nine benchmark families, four optimizer configurations, and five random seeds, we obtain three main findings: (1) using only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark does not reliably improve detection; (2) practical TTA failures exhibit two distinct responses to this intervention, with 63 of 180 settings recovering a clear detection gain while 90 still show no clear detection gain; and (3) the updates reduce their immediate reconstruction objective across the evaluated settings, yet these reductions do not consistently lead to better anomaly ranking. Both failure patterns persist across alternative practical adaptation variants and modest perturbations of the benchmark labels. Together, these findings show that improving sample selection alone is not a complete solution to reconstruction-based TSAD TTA.
  </details>

- **[Simpler Tiny Recursive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23677)**  `arXiv:2611.23677`  `cs.LG`  
  _Torben Glass, Zhengxi Zhang, Paul Swoboda_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small models that execute deep looped computational graphs have shown great experimental results on puzzle-like problems like Sudoku, finding shortest paths through mazes, etc. The most well-known one is TRM (tiny recursive model), a simplification of HRM (hierarchical reasoning model). However, the underlying recursion is complicated and involves a high-level and a low-level state, a nested loop recursion, and the network calls with different input and output signatures depending on where we are in the loops. These mechanisms are needed to avoid memorization and overfitting. We propose STRM, a simplified design with a single state and a uniform update. STRM does not sacrifice generalization, improves training speed and final performance. Our key architectural choices that allow our simpler recursion to work are exponential moving updates on the states with varying rates that are jittered during training and readout of the solution on slow-updating channels only. We test STRM on Sudoku-Extreme, PPBench and Maze-Hard with the same data, parameters and compute as TRM: we improve over TRM by $3.8$ points on Sudoku-Extreme and $8.4$ points on PPBench and match it on Maze-Hard. On ARC-AGI-1 and ARC-AGI-2, STRM improves over the published TRM results by $5.4$ and $0.5$ points. We will release our code upon acceptance of the paper.
  </details>

- **[Agent Verifiers in Counterfactual Analysis: A Pilot Study Benchmarking Agentic Heterogeneous Treatment Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23621)**  `arXiv:2611.23621`  `cs.LG`  
  _Satiyabooshan Murugaboopathy, Entejar Alam, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents can expand the covariate space of randomized experiments by retrieving fast-moving local context missed by slow-changing survey baseline covariates, helping characterize who benefits most from an intervention. Verifying such covariates is difficult: individual treatment effects are unobserved, retrieval leakage can pull post-treatment information into nominally pretreatment summaries, and encoder leakage can inject post-treatment pretraining knowledge into representations even after temporal filtering of inputs. Guided by a two-channel information bound separating summary provenance from encoder weights, we propose a three-layer benchmark protocol: (i) retrieval admissibility via pre-treatment date filtering; (ii) heterogeneity validation scored rank-weighted average treatment effects (RATEs); and (iii) encoder admissibility checked with chronologically vintaged encoders, future-fact probes, and weight perturbations. Across 11 datasets anchored by Uganda's Youth Opportunities Program, we compare session-isolated retrieval (sandboxed, bounded-budget agent runs per study) against a persistent workspace teammate (xAI's GrokBot) that builds one date-verified corpus per study while blinded to outcomes. The workspace teammate yields higher mean RATE ratios in 6/11 datasets for text alone and 7 for combined features, though paired tests show neither workflow universally dominates. Even on fixed retrieved text, encoder choice significantly shifts heterogeneity signal. Chronologically vintaged probes show no clear evidence of weight leakage, but single-record adapter perturbation analyses show that memorized future facts, once present, persist until injected noise degrades model output. Temporal filtering of retrieval is necessary but insufficient: verifying agent-generated covariates requires jointly examining source provenance, out-of-sample treatment-effect signal, and encoder weights.
  </details>

- **[FACT: Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23463)**  `arXiv:2611.23463`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hadi Fares, Mayssa Hajj Hassan, Ali Salman, Ammar Mohanna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates language-model generation by verifying multiple draft tokens in a single target-model pass. However, better draft predictions and longer accepted continuations do not necessarily produce faster generation. To examine this gap, we present FACT (Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training), an experimental framework for studying how drafter training, candidate allocation, and execution conditions jointly affect acceptance and latency. Using a lightweight feature-prediction drafter with a frozen Llama-3-8B-Instruct target under greedy decoding, we first examine how training translates into accepted tokens. Backpropagating through predicted-feature rollouts with greater weight on early steps improves acceptance by 15-24% over uniformly weighted detached training, yet average rollout proxy cross-entropy does not reliably rank drafters by acceptance. Beyond prediction quality, the placement of draft candidates also matters: with a fixed 256-node budget and matched per-depth widths, replacing static allocation with confidence-guided allocation increases speedup over autoregressive decoding from $1.46\times$ to $2.02\times$ on HumanEval and from $1.31\times$ to $1.75\times$ on MT-Bench. This allocation benefit extends to an independently trained drafter, but stronger training does not consistently amplify it, showing that the two improvements do not necessarily reinforce each other. Even these speed gains depend on execution conditions: changing only host-thread settings can nearly eliminate speedup without changing acceptance. Together, these findings show why training and allocation must be evaluated jointly using both acceptance and end-to-end latency under controlled execution conditions.
  </details>

- **[GridForge: An Open Physics-Informed Digital Twin and Benchmark Suite for UK 11 kV Underground Distribution Cables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23241)**  `arXiv:2611.23241`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lilliane Linnet Musoke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The UK distribution-cable monitoring sector is data-rich but framework-poor: each Distribution Network Operator (DNO) collects megabytes of telemetry per asset per day, but there is no public benchmark dataset, no shared reference model, and no commonly agreed evaluation protocol for benchmarking sectoral, data-intensive models for operational decision support; notably for maintenance planning and prioritisation. We address this gap with GridForge, an open-source physics-informed digital twin and benchmark suite for fault diagnosis and prediction, integrating four building blocks: (i) a verified steady state and transient thermal model based on IEC 60287 and IEC 60853; (ii) a calibrated synthetic dataset of 64 cables (covering 320 cable-years) representing four canonical UK 11 kV thermal archetypes and four condition modes; (iii) a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) surrogate that satisfies the algebraic balance defined by IEC 60287 during training and reaches an RMSE of 0.148 °C on an independent held out set against the reference analytical oracle; and (iv) a five-task benchmark suite that includes three reference baseline models (pure physics, gradient-boosted trees, and the PINN). All numerical parameters are sourced from public standards, the code is licensed under the MIT licence, and the trained PINN converges in only two minutes (as run on the computing platform in Section 10.1), enabling independent verification by DNO engineering and research practitioners. At present only the virtual-sensor task (T4) discriminates between the reference baselines; the other tasks tie or are undefined on this test split, so extending the baselines with task-specific logic is a priority future enhancement. For open validation, the dataset, test sets and models are made available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Calibrated Uncertainty Quantification on TDC ADMET: What Can Be Resolved Across 22 Datasets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09373)**  `arXiv:2611.09373`  `cs.LG`  
  _Laurits Fredsgaard, Michael Riis Andersen, Mikkel N. Schmidt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) models often rely on small, heterogeneous datasets, yet their predictions may determine which compounds proceed to experimental testing. Useful evaluations must therefore assess both predictive performance and the quality of reported probabilities or intervals. We ask which common uncertainty-quantification methods perform best on the 22 Therapeutics Data Commons (TDC) ADMET datasets and what this benchmark can resolve. We compare eleven calibrated pipelines using nested five-fold cross-validation across ten chemical partitions and five model seeds. Ensemble Monte Carlo (MC) dropout has the best average rank for TDC performance, Brier score, and 90% interval score. For TDC performance, however, it is not separated from the other ensemble pipelines; for Brier and interval score, LightGBM is also not ruled out as best. LightGBM wins more individual datasets than any other method for TDC performance and Brier score, but performs poorly on several endpoints. Smaller evaluations can identify a different leader and often fail to recover the broader comparison. These results make LightGBM and an ensemble neural model complementary baselines. Comparative claims should be checked across multiple chemical partitions using statistical analyses matched to the question being asked.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35801)**  `arXiv:2610.35801`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang, Chunsheng Zuo, Anqi Liu, Benjamin Van Durme_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($\lambda$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).
  </details>

- **[CommitKV: Commit-Aligned KV Cache Compression for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07855)**  `arXiv:2608.07855`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents gather visual evidence through reasoning and tool use, extending their ability to solve tasks beyond the initial input. However, accumulating observations increases KV cache memory and inference costs. Token eviction reduces these costs by retaining useful cached tokens. Existing methods use attention, redundancy, or stage-specific queries to rank tokens, without explicitly comparing the same history across tool interactions. When observations shift attention, they may mistake temporarily reduced influence for lost usefulness and evict information needed later. We propose CommitKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that accounts for this changing evidence use. Commit-aligned measurement compares the deletion effects of the same page before a tool call completes and after its observation returns. Lifecycle-aware compression retires pages with low influence in both windows and temporarily protects pages influential before the call. Joint scoring combines attention importance, key redundancy, and token-level deletion effects to fill the remaining budget. Extensive experiments cover four open-weight multimodal models and eight benchmarks. CommitKV achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared compression methods on all four models. On Qwen3-VL-8B, it achieves 69.85% at a 512-token budget, only 0.91% below FullKV, and exceeds the strongest compression baseline by 8.44% at 256 tokens. Deploying the Qwen3-VL-8B agent with SGLang and vLLM at a 256-token budget reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 54.37%-55.23% and accelerates end-to-end inference by 2.12-2.17$\times$ over FullKV on the matched workload.
  </details>

- **[When Do Foundation Models Pay Off? A Break-Even Analysis of Pretrained Time Series Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04919)**  `arXiv:2607.04919`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nicholas Tan Jerome, Frank Simon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying a time series foundation model (FM) requires GPU infrastructure and engineering overhead, with no guarantee of improvement over classical forecasting methods. We provide the first systematic break-even analysis of when this investment pays off. Across 30 benchmark datasets, we compare zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned FMs (Chronos-Bolt, Moirai, Lag-Llama) against classical baselines (Naive, ETS, ARIMA, XGBoost) at six training set sizes from 2% to 100% of available data. FMs outperform classical methods at every evaluated training fraction on 16 of 30 datasets. On 6 datasets, classical methods surpass zero-shot FMs with at most 5% of the training data (21--6,324 samples); on the remaining 8, break-even ranges from 24 to 22,177 samples. No classical method dominates: ARIMA, Naive, ETS and XGBoost are each best on 6--11 datasets. One robust rule requires no model training: if n_train < 700 and seasonality is non-negligible, FM zero-shot is the right low-data choice -- below 20% of the history, classical methods either lose to it (4 of these 10 datasets) or cannot be fitted at all (6) -- and it stays best at full history on 6 of them; fine-tuning should be skipped. Contrary to common practice, LoRA fine-tuning rarely helps and degrades short series by up to 52%. We operationalise these findings as a two-step decision framework -- compute dataset length and seasonality strength, run a brief 5--10% pilot only if needed. Cheap dataset features do not predict the break-even category at this benchmark scale, so outside the rule a pilot remains necessary. Code and benchmark:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting for Alleviating Over-smoothing in Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10592)**  `arXiv:2606.10592`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xingyu Zhang, Jingyao Wang, Xin Yu, Zeen Song, Jianqi Zhang, Changwen Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting often suffers from over-smoothing, especially when future dynamics are multi-modal. Forecasts may follow the coarse trend of the observed future, but fail to preserve sharp changes, oscillations, turning points, and regime transitions that define plausible dynamic evolution. In this work, we revisit over-smoothing from the perspective of latent dynamical mode compression: under partial observation and single-realization supervision, multiple plausible future modes can be weakened, merged, or averaged during forecasting. Based on this view, we propose Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting (DGF), a mode-preserving forecasting framework that explicitly models multiple mode-conditioned predictive distributions and uncertainty over their selection probabilities. DGF uses a Dirichlet-guided hierarchical sampling mechanism and reward-based optimization to encourage forecasts that are accurate, dynamically consistent, and mode-distinct. Extensive experiments on real-world forecasting benchmarks show that DGF reduces over-smoothing while improving forecasting accuracy, diversity, and dynamical consistency. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[The Silent Hyperparameter: Quantifying the Impact of Inference Backends on LLM Reproducibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19537)**  `arXiv:2605.19537`  `cs.LG`  
  _David Pape, Jonathan Evertz, Lea Schönherr_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in LLMs is increasingly measured through standardized benchmarks, where state-of-the-art improvements are often separated by fractions of a percentage point. At the same time, the computational cost of evaluating modern LLMs has driven widespread adoption of specialized inference backends, software systems that execute trained models efficiently at inference time. While critical for scalability, system-level optimizations, such as custom CUDA kernels and reduced-precision arithmetic, can alter token probabilities and introduce non-determinism, possibly cascading into divergent generation. In this work, we first survey the inference landscape, identifying $200$ distinct engines, and analyze $35,000$ ML publications, finding that the specific inference stack is rarely reported despite this widespread diversity. We then present a systematic empirical study of how inference backends affect LLM benchmark results. Holding model weights, decoding parameters, and hardware constant, we evaluate five widely used inference engines, including vLLM, SGLang, and llama$.$cpp, across multiple open-weight models spanning 4B-30B parameters and established benchmarks. We show that the choice of backend alone can shift benchmark scores by up to $16.6$ percentage points and induce high rates of output disagreement. By isolating backend optimizations and tracing the execution pipeline, we find this divergence is driven by system-level optimizations such as prefix caching and CUDA graphs, custom kernels, and engine-specific defaults in logit processing. Our findings identify the inference backend as a previously unreported yet consequential hyperparameter in LLM evaluations and advocate standardized reporting of inference stacks to improve the reproducibility and interpretability of benchmark comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Towards Generalizable Reasoning: Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06475)**  `arXiv:2602.06475`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jingyao Wang, Xingyu Zhang, Peizheng Guo, Jiahuan Zhou, Zhenghao Zhang, Changwen Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at complex tasks with advances in reasoning capabilities. However, existing reward mechanisms remain tightly coupled to final correctness and pay little attention to the underlying reasoning process: trajectories with sound reasoning but wrong answers receive low credit, while lucky guesses with flawed logic may be highly rewarded, affecting reasoning generalization. From a causal perspective, we interpret multi-candidate reasoning for a fixed question as a family of counterfactual experiments. Building on this, we propose Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization to explicitly train LLMs to learn generalizable reasoning patterns. It proposes an episodic causal counterfactual reward that jointly captures (i) robustness, encouraging the answer distribution induced by a reasoning step to remain stable under counterfactual perturbations; and (ii) effectiveness, enforcing sufficient variability so that the learned reasoning strategy can transfer across questions. We then construct token-level advantages from this reward and optimize the policy, encouraging LLMs to favor reasoning patterns that are process-valid and counterfactually robust. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate its advantages.
  </details>

- **[Hyperparameter Optimization via Budget Allocation in Lipschitz Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10605)**  `arXiv:2510.10605`  `cs.LG`  
  _MohammadHossein Bateni, Hossein Esfandiari, Samira HosseinGhorban, Alireza Mirrokni, Radin Shahdaei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperparameter optimization and model selection often require allocating a limited evaluation budget across many candidate configurations, where performance improves with additional training or resources. We study this setting when each configuration has an unknown performance curve over budget. Similarity between configurations induces similarity between their curves, capturing geometric structure in the search space. We formalize the resulting decision problem as Unknown Value Probing and propose budget allocation strategies that combine principled exploration of the configuration space with value-informed concentration on regions supported by observed evidence. We prove worst-case approximation guarantees for our approach and show these guarantees are near-optimal by matching corresponding hardness barriers. Extensive experiments on large benchmark suites spanning more than 250 experimental settings demonstrate consistent gains over strong baselines in both anytime behavior and final incumbent performance.
  </details>

- **[NereidVLA: Learning to Understand and Navigate the Underwater World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24864)**  `arXiv:2611.24864`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Chenjie Zhao, Zhiliang Zhu, Quan Chen, Yuquan Xue, Xingxue Shi, Puyue Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underwater navigation requires translating visual spatial cues into motion, yet marine vision-language datasets rarely include paired robot trajectories. We present NereidVLA, a vision-language-action model that transfers marine spatial knowledge to language-conditioned 3D navigation. We first train Sea2Know on approximately two million marine examples to learn target grounding, spatial relations, and scene structure without paired robot-motion supervision. We then adapt it using expert trajectories executed under simulated underwater vehicle dynamics. Given RGB histories and a language instruction, NereidVLA predicts discrete motion tokens that decode into local 3D trajectories for closed-loop tracking. Evaluation combines an independent six-dimensional underwater navigation understanding benchmark with offline prediction and closed-loop navigation. Under matched navigation training and execution settings, marine spatial pretraining increases simulation success from 72.4% to 84.4%. Without fine-tuning on the real test environment, NereidVLA achieves 78.75% success in laboratory-pool robot trials, compared with 47.50% for InternVLA-N1. These results show that marine spatial supervision can support downstream motion learning and physical underwater navigation.
  </details>

- **[TrajWAM: Trajectory-Conditioned Future Learning for Vision-Language Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24192)**  `arXiv:2611.24192`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Xuerui Fang, Gulimila Kezierbieke, Chen Wangxing, Hui Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) connect future scene prediction with action planning for autonomous driving. Each driving record pairs an executed ego trajectory with its observed future, providing a factual outcome for learning this connection. We present TrajWAM, a trajectory-conditioned WAM that improves vision-language driving through auxiliary bird's-eye-view (BEV) future learning. An ego-trajectory query conditions DINOv3-aligned BEV predictions from shared planner representations. Training combines factual reconstruction under the executed trajectory with one-sided feature separation under an unlabeled perturbed trajectory. Together, these objectives train the shared planner to generate driving waypoints. Native inference directly generates waypoints and bypasses the auxiliary future-prediction branch. On all 220 Bench2Drive routes, TrajWAM achieves 91.53 Driving Score and 75.45% Success Rate, exceeding a schedule-matched planning-only fine-tuning baseline by 5.31 Driving Score points and eight successful routes.
  </details>

- **[Training-free motion statistics match self-supervised behavior encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24792)**  `arXiv:2611.24792`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised encoders for animal behavior are often trained on, or fed, hand-built movement features. We ask whether they beat those ingredients when compared fairly. A training-free representation, made of generic per-frame measurements summarized over fixed time windows and reduced with PCA, is compared with retrained and released encoders (BAMS, hBehaveMAE) under a preregistered protocol: frozen representations of equal size and one shared classifier. On CalMS21 it reaches 85.8 macro F1 against 82.4 for the best encoder, ties with four labelled videos, and leads when the annotator changes or new behaviors must be learned from a few videos; the reading holds for encoders retrained with three seeds. On the MABe22 mouse triplets it scores 83.7 against 80.3 for the released hBehaveMAE (gap +3.4, 95% interval +2.1 to +4.7). On a second species, the MABe22 fly groups, the same recipe scores 51.6 mean F1 over 50 tasks against 48.2 for BAMS, the best published method. On Shot7M2, the synthetic basketball benchmark built by hBehaveMAE's authors, it scores 68.8 against 64.7 for hBehaveMAE, the best method in that paper, though hBehaveMAE remains better on the shortest movements. Short time windows carry most of the result. Raw keypoints in each body's own frame, summarized over the same windows, already match or beat the encoders on both mouse datasets and on Shot7M2; on the fly groups they fall short, and the measurements add 5.3 points. On these benchmarks, current behavior encoders have yet to beat the hand-built features they learn from, and such baselines should be reported alongside them.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Contrastive Targets in Radiology Language-Image Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24016)**  `arXiv:2611.24016`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yunhe Gao, Ashwin Kumar, Jiaming Liu, Chong Wang, Maya Varma, Fuying Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CLIP-style contrastive image-text pretraining is widely used in radiology data, where each image is paired with one report and every other report in the batch is treated as a negative. Under this recipe, two failures appear, neither of which is systematically addressed by existing contrastive learning theory: zero-shot evaluation peaks then degrades while training loss keeps decreasing, and training fails to learn from large batches. We hypothesize the cause is structural rather than radiology-specific: many unpaired reports share clinical findings with a given image, so CLIP's paired-identity target is systematically biased, not merely noisy. To test this, we construct CLEVR-Radiology, a synthetic testbed that preserves only this overlapping-fact structure and reproduces both failures. With evidence that the structure is sufficient to reproduce the failures, we develop a target-misspecification theory that explains both failures from a single deterministic gradient bias amplified by batch size, and use it to motivate a minimal compatibility-based correction, CorLIP: text is treated as positive whenever its clinical assertions agree with the image, with no architecture changes. Beyond benchmark improvements, CorLIP largely removes the post-peak degradation, makes large-batch training succeed where it previously collapsed, substantially amplifies the gains from more data, and is the only configuration that remains stable up to B=2048. The correction transfers from chest X-ray to 3D CT modalities.
  </details>

- **[GRAFT: Graph-Regularized Prototype Fields for Realistic Test-Time Adaptation of VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23591)**  `arXiv:2611.23591`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Maxx Richard Rahman, Wolfgang Maass_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlabeled test data can improve the zero-shot classifier of a vision-language model, yet transductive and test-time adaptation methods often fall below the zero-shot baseline when only a few vocabulary classes are present in a batch or when streams are correlated. We trace this failure to a shared design: every class owns independent parameters estimated from its own samples, so absent or rare classes are left at biased text anchors or fitted to foreign samples. We propose Graph-Regularized Anchor-displacement Field Transduction (GRAFT), which models the adapted prototypes as text anchors plus a displacement field that is partially pooled over a semantic class graph through a Gaussian Markov random field prior, together with an explicit class-presence model. Inference is closed-form and gradient-free on cached features. Anchor-only shrinkage and a single global shift are the two extreme settings of the prior; absent classes are provably harmonic extensions of the present ones, and the pooling strength can be chosen by empirical Bayes. Under the realistic batch and online protocols on eleven benchmarks and four ImageNet shifts, GRAFT improves over the zero-shot model in every setting and over anchor-based transduction where few classes are present.
  </details>

- **[What Does a Temporal Benchmark Score Measure? Decomposing Channel Use in Video VLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12304)**  `arXiv:2607.12304`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Farrukh Rahman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A score on a temporal video question answering benchmark is meant to measure that a model has temporal understanding, but it conflates two questions. (1)~is the question even temporal, does it need several frames and their order? And (2)~when it does, does the model recover the order from the pixels, or read it off the positional encoding (RoPE)? Most of a temporal score answers neither, since a single frame and answer priors often carry it, and the field's validity checks, frame-shuffle sensitivity and multi-frame gain, speak only to (1). For (2) we score a reversal conflict, frames reversed while RoPE stays forward, two ways: label-free, as the accuracy lost against the forward labels, and against reversed labels wherever labels transform deterministically under time reversal. This separates a model that follows the reversed content from one merely disrupted. The two readings dissociate two similarly scoring models at both model scales. Qwen3-VL answers the reversed event it actually sees, reading the visual sequence. Molmo2 correctly reports a backwards video when positions agree, and flipping only the RoPE indices flips its prediction to the forward events, i.e. it reads positions. We call these regimes \emph{visual-sequence-dominant} and \emph{position-dominant}. Controls show reversed content is processable whenever the channels agree, and that both families share a bias toward describing events as running forward (arrow-of-time prior) that, being similar in strength, cannot by itself account for the dissociation. The distinction matters because the two channels fail on opposite inputs: two models with the same score are not interchangeable.
  </details>

- **[DLWM: Diverse Latent World Models for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15160)**  `arXiv:2606.15160`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _David Huang, Lianlei Shan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have improved considerably in recent years. Existing approaches typically rely on explicit chain-of-thought or continuous latent-space trajectories to enhance multi-step reasoning. However, these methods generally assume that an input admits a single latent interpretation and unfold reasoning along a fixed path or under a uniform computation budget. In real-world multimodal settings, visual observations are often subject to occlusion, blur, viewpoint variation, or semantic ambiguity, giving rise to multiple plausible interpretations. A uniform reasoning strategy not only limits the model's ability to explore multiple hypotheses but also incurs high memory usage and rollout cost. We present DLWM (Diverse Latent World Models), a multimodal reasoning framework that combines latent-space reasoning with reinforcement learning. First, we construct a set of diverse latent world hypotheses in continuous latent space, each representing a candidate interpretation of the visual input, and unfold latent reasoning independently on each hypothesis. An orthogonality-based diversity regularizer discourages hypothesis collapse. Second, we formulate the latent reasoning process as a resource-constrained sequential decision problem and introduce a resource-aware reinforcement learning policy that adaptively allocates computation across hypotheses, dynamically deciding whether to expand a reasoning path, prune a hypothesis, merge reasoning paths, or produce the final answer, thereby substantially reducing memory footprint and improving rollout efficiency. Experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that DLWM outperforms existing methods by 2-5 points in accuracy while reducing memory usage by 24%.
  </details>

- **[GRTO: Bootstrapped Group Relative Tool Optimization for Referring Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23500)**  `arXiv:2605.23500`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mario Markov, Stefan Maria Ailuro, Mohammad Mahdi, Luc Van Gool, Danda Pani Paudel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring segmentation requires reasoning to connect language with visual evidence and identify the intended target in challenging scenarios. This makes GRPO a natural choice for training VLMs together with a segmentation tool. However, existing approaches optimize only the VLM, assuming that the tool is perfect and leaving it untouched. This assumption is particularly limiting when the tool faces domain shift, as segmentation errors can undermine even correct reasoning. We therefore argue that the segmentation tool should be optimized together with the reasoning policy. However, this is challenging because policy optimization and differentiable tool learning rely on fundamentally different objectives. In this work, we introduce group relative tool optimization (GRTO), a mathematically grounded two-stage framework for jointly optimizing a policy and its differentiable tool. The key idea is to first align the tool with the policy expected after joint training, rather than the initial policy. The prealigned tool then leads to a more robust reward signal and superior policy convergence in subsequent policy training. In the first stage, GRTO bootstraps the tool toward the theoretically optimal policy, capturing readily attainable gains without costly on-policy rollouts. This alignment bootstraps the second stage, where GRTO extends GRPO to jointly refine the policy and tool. Across three challenging referring segmentation benchmarks, GRTO achieves substantial improvements over plain GRPO and alternative tool training procedures, matching or surpassing domain-specific state-of-the-art methods. This demonstrates the value of unifying reinforcement learning with differentiable auxiliary objectives for reasoning-intensive segmentation.
  </details>

- **[DRIFT: Detachable Reasoning for Runtime-Flexible and Efficient End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24719)**  `arXiv:2611.24719`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Emad Aghajanzadeh, Bethany Anne Wong, Leonhard Kunze, Moritz Thoma, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Nael Fasfous, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The integration of vision-language models (VLMs) into end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has demonstrated strong potential by providing scene description and reasoning capabilities. However, VLMs are computationally expensive, and switching language reasoning on and off at runtime without hurting closed-loop performance has not been demonstrated, which hinders practical adoption. To address these issues, we propose DRIFT, an E2E autonomous driving framework that integrates a lightweight language model (LM) for visual question answering and action commentary. By efficiently projecting intermediate driving representations into the language embedding space, we demonstrate that a compact LM (<150M parameters) can achieve strong scene description and action commentary. Furthermore, we introduce a runtime-flexible planning module that is trained on surrogate reasoning features distilled from the LM and can be conditioned on either the LM or the surrogate reasoning features at inference. This design enables bypassing the LM entirely when required by deployment constraints, without sacrificing driving performance. With only 255M parameters, DRIFT reaches up to a Driving Score (DS) of 97.03 and a Success Rate of 89.39% (mean over three runs) on Bench2Drive by interleaving its LM and surrogate reasoning at a chosen reasoning rate. This is on par with the strongest non-language models while being far more compact than existing VLM-based models. DRIFT further achieves competitive results on the real-world NAVSIM and AlpaSim benchmarks at significantly lower computational cost than existing VLM-based approaches. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Sightline: Long-Window Speculative Decoding for Fast SVG Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23807)**  `arXiv:2611.23807`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yanchao Li, Ben Gao, Jiaqing Xie, Yusu Zhang, Beilun Wang, Yanbo Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) generation models write a graphic as code with one forward pass per token, so drawing is slow. Speculative decoding speeds them up losslessly, and block drafters guess many tokens per pass. On SVG, many of their rounds accept the whole draft and end only at the verification window. We find that their misses are rare and clumped, and that they stay accurate far deeper into a block than on natural language. A run-length identity and three propositions show that a longer window pays once the candidates are wide, up to the drafter's horizon. We propose Sightline, which pairs a window as long as the drafter's horizon with a draft tree wide enough to fill it. Experiments cover text-to-SVG and image-to-SVG with three models on six benchmarks from MMSVG, MMSVG-2M and SArena. In every setting, Sightline is the fastest of the compared lossless decoders. On SArena, it stays the fastest when every decoder shares one loop control. It also leads in almost every setting of six models from four other families, which write SVG code, compressed SVG tokens or Lottie JSON. Analyses trace the lead to rounds that shorter windows cut off, both in loop-free content and in loops. Speculative decoding also saves time for choosing samples by rendering, which beats choosing by likelihood. These results suggest that speculative decoding on vector graphics should verify as far as the drafter sees. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Quicksviewer: An LMM with Early Video Compression using Annealed Gumbel-Softmax](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15270)**  `arXiv:2504.15270`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Ji Qi, Yuan Yao, Yushi Bai, Bin Xu, Juanzi Li, Zhiyuan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) uniformly perceive video frames, creating computational inefficiency for videos with inherently varying temporal redundancy. This paper present **Quicksviewer**, an LMM with new perceiving paradigm that partitions a nonuniform redundant video into varying cubes using Gumbel Softmax, followed by a unified resampling for each cube to achieve efficient video understanding. We further propose a Gumbel-noise annealing strategy that enables stable end-to-end LMM training with the discrete cubing network. This simple and intuitive approach dynamically compress video online based on the momentum of frame differences, significantly reducing spatiotemporal complexity (overall 45$\times$ compression rate), while enabling efficient training with large receptive field. We train the model from a language backbone through three progressive stages, each incorporating lengthy videos on average of 420s/1fps thanks to the perceiving efficiency. With only 0.8M total video samples, our model outperforms contemporary LMMs with the same backbone and comparable parameters amount. An experiment in A/B pretraining with same backbone and training data shows that, w/ cubing achieves lower training loss and higher benchmark accuracy (+2.1) than the fixed partitioning baseline, strictly validating the benefits. Moreover, the front-loaded compression in encoding substantially reduces latency in the computationally expensive prefill and decoding, making it suited to mainstream EPD disaggregated inference deployment. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReactBench-AD: Do Driving VLAs and World-Action Models React to Imminent Hazards in Their Path?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20011)**  `arXiv:2611.20011`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Nikos Theodoridis, Reenu Mohandas, Sushil Sharma, Ciarán Hogan, Ganesh Sistu, Anthony Scanlan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) and World-Action Models (WAMs) have emerged in autonomous driving with the promise of strong generalization to long-tail scenarios. Although these models achieve state-of-the-art results on autonomous driving benchmarks, most existing benchmarks are dominated by nominal scenarios and therefore cannot test this promise. To test one such long-tail capability that is critical for safety, we introduce ReactBench-AD, a benchmark that measures how driving models react to objects that appear in the ego vehicle's path. Built on real NAVSIM and PhysicalAI-Autonomous-Vehicles scenes, ReactBench-AD composites a crossing pedestrian, a standing pedestrian, or a stationary car consistently across all camera views and history frames. Each edited scene thus forms an exact, pixel-level counterfactual pair with its original, which allows us to isolate the causal effect of the inserted object on the predicted trajectory. Evaluating four VLAs and two WAMs, we find that they react weakly, often barely at all. Finally, we test how easily one of the evaluated VLAs, ReCogDrive, can be taught to brake hard for imminent hazards: a short fine-tuning stage on edited scenes substantially improves its reactions, with the learned behavior generalizing to unseen objects and fully real scenes, at minimal cost to nominal driving. These results call into question the out-of-the-box long-tail capabilities of these models, but suggest that they can be a strong foundation for acquiring them with little additional training, highlighting the value of real or synthetic data that demonstrates the long-tail behaviors nominal datasets rarely contain.
  </details>

- **[OCTR2A: Real-Time RGB-to-BEV Trajectory Projection and Forecasting at Urban Intersections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24352)**  `arXiv:2611.24352`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Emanuele Iacobelli, Simone Teglia, Francesco Pro, Claudia Melis Tonti, Irene Amerini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trajectory forecasting at urban intersections is often studied assuming that reliable bird's-eye-view (BEV) motion histories are available, which in practice requires costly resources such as LiDAR, aerial views, HD maps, or calibrated infrastructure. We address this upstream perception-to-forecasting problem by reconstructing and predicting agents' BEV trajectories only from paired opposite-corner RGB cameras mounted on existing urban infrastructure at variable heights at inference time. We introduce the Opposite-Corner Trajectory Reconstruction and Anticipation Architecture (OCTR2A), a modular pipeline integrating per-view detection with class refinement, geometric cross-camera matching, transformer-based BEV projection, and egocentric GRU forecasting, trained in cascade and mainly supervised with a soft multi-scale spatial-overlap loss. Since no public benchmark provides this sensing configuration, we introduce R3ST++, a Blender-based synthetic benchmark built from SIND trajectories that provides synchronized opposite-corner views, cross-view correspondences, and BEV ground truth, while also serving as a deployment-support simulator for site-specific fine-tuning. Experiments on these datasets show that, with 12 observed and 12 predicted frames at 10\,Hz, OCTR2A achieves projection~-~forecasting ADE/FDE of 0.144/0.140~-~0.154/0.241~meters in real time on a single consumer GPU. Evaluated on ground-truth BEV histories, the forecasting module improves over the strongest state-of-the-art methods on SIND by 66.7\%/41.7\% ADE/FDE. Transfer experiments on TUMTraf-V2X show that R3ST++ pretraining supports adaptation to real-world roadside domains with different sensing configurations.
  </details>

- **[SHAMISA: SHAped Modeling of Implicit Structural Associations for Self-supervised No-Reference Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13669)**  `arXiv:2603.13669`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Mahdi Naseri, Zhou Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  No-Reference Image Quality Assessment (NR-IQA) aims to estimate perceptual quality without access to a pristine reference. Learning an NR-IQA model faces a fundamental bottleneck because it requires many costly human perceptual labels. We propose SHAMISA, a non-contrastive self-supervised framework that learns from unlabeled distorted images through explicitly structured relational supervision. Unlike prior methods that impose rigid binary similarity constraints, SHAMISA shapes soft, controllable implicit structural associations that are both distortion-aware and content-sensitive. A key innovation is its compositional distortion engine, which generates an uncountable family of degradations from continuous parameter spaces and groups the resulting images so that exactly one distortion factor varies at a time. This construction enables fine-grained control over representational similarity during training. Images with shared distortion patterns are pulled together in the embedding space, while severity variations produce structured shifts. Dual-source relation graphs integrate known degradation profiles with emergent structural affinities throughout pre-training. The learned encoder is then frozen and paired with a linear regressor. Extensive experiments on synthetic, authentic, and cross-dataset NR-IQA benchmarks demonstrate strong overall performance, improved transfer in the reported cross-dataset comparisons, and favorable qualitative robustness diagnostics. Self-supervised pre-training requires no IQA opinion-score annotations or contrastive loss.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Information Fusion for Chart Understanding: A Survey of MLLMs -- Evolution, Limitations, and Cognitive Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10138)**  `arXiv:2602.10138`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhihang Yi, Jian Zhao, Jiancheng Lv, Tao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chart understanding is a quintessential information fusion task, requiring the seamless integration of graphical and textual data to extract meaning. The advent of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has revolutionized this domain, yet the landscape of MLLM-based chart analysis remains fragmented and lacks systematic organization. This survey provides a comprehensive roadmap of this nascent frontier by structuring the domain's core components. We begin by analyzing the fundamental challenges of fusing visual and linguistic information in charts. We then categorize downstream tasks and datasets, introducing a novel taxonomy of canonical and non-canonical benchmarks to highlight the field's expanding scope. Subsequently, we present a comprehensive evolution of methodologies, tracing the progression from classic deep learning techniques to state-of-the-art MLLM paradigms that leverage sophisticated fusion strategies. By critically examining the limitations of current models, particularly their perceptual and reasoning deficits, we identify promising future directions, including advanced alignment techniques and reinforcement learning for cognitive enhancement. This survey aims to equip researchers and practitioners with a structured understanding of how MLLMs are transforming chart information fusion and to catalyze progress toward more robust and reliable systems.
  </details>

- **[BaseRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement of a Base Vision-Language Model via Training-Free Harness Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24578)**  `arXiv:2611.24578`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Yuanhao Dong, Qiang Huang, Rui Qian, Yanjin Li, Zhenyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is moving from the harness of prompts and tools around a base model toward the model itself. However, existing RSI still faces key challenges, including harness-bound gains that stay outside the base model, black-box supervision that reads only the model's outputs, and imprecise edits whose site and strength are fixed in advance. To address these challenges, we propose BaseRSI, a new paradigm of Recursive Harness Internalization: the base model improves its harness, then writes what the harness adds into its own weights through training-free edits, round after round on its own responses. To support this paradigm, we introduce ReGap, a mechanism that supervises every transformer block with the high-dimensional activation difference that the harness makes at that block alone, and updates the block's weights along this signal in a low-dimensional subspace. Building on this mechanism, we further propose ReKey, an algorithm that writes this signal by regression into a few key-value neurons of every block, and measures the site and strength of each edit on the model's own responses. Moreover, the base model fills every role of this recursion with forward passes alone, and its edits, written at different sites, compose in one checkpoint without refitting. Experimental results on ten visual reasoning benchmarks show that BaseRSI consistently improves a base vision-language model both in distribution and on held-out benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Let a Small Model Read First: Faster Prefill for Long Interleaved Image-Text Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24431)**  `arXiv:2611.24431`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaolin Lin, Kai-Chiang Wu, Bing Li, Grace Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about long interleaved inputs, such as documents of many pages and collections of hundreds of images, but they are slow to start: on inputs of about 100K tokens, the prefill takes most of the wait for the first output token. A common approach prunes the input by scoring its tokens with the target model itself or with a small model of the same family that first writes an answer. However, this scoring takes much of the time that pruning saves. We find that the evidence for a question lies in a few whole units (images or paragraphs), and that a small hybrid model finds them with one attention head of an early layer, even at half image resolution. Based on these findings, we propose SkimPrefill, which lets the small hybrid model read the whole input first, up to that early layer, with images at half resolution. Most layers of the small hybrid model (Qwen3.5-0.8B) run in linear time, so this pass is short. SkimPrefill then ranks every unit with that head and gives the target the top units as an ordinary shorter prompt. One untrained selector serves targets of different model families. Extensive experiments on MMLongBench at 128K tokens, with 19 datasets of five task types, show the effectiveness of SkimPrefill. With 10% of the input tokens kept, SkimPrefill speeds up the prefill of Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-12B and Qwen3-VL-32B by 3.7 times, 11.6 time and 8.2 times while maintaining their average accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Fuse to Recognize the Unseen: Class-Relative Infrared-Visible Fusion for Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24316)**  `arXiv:2611.24316`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zehao Gao, Jie Feng, Wenda Zhao, Junpeng Zhang, Ronghua Shang, Weisheng Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visible-infrared image fusion leverages complementary thermal cues to enhance visual perception, particularly under challenging illumination. However, such perceptual complementarity does not readily translate into semantic gains in open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS), where CLIP-based models are predominantly pretrained on RGB imagery. Directly introducing IR information may instead disrupt RGB-language alignment, turning promising thermal cues into semantic interference that undermines unseen-class recognition. Our pixel-level RGB-IR semantic analysis reveals that class-relevant thermal cues can correct certain RGB misclassifications while preserving reliable RGB predictions, motivating RGB-anchored, class-relative thermal cue selection. Based on this insight, we propose EmergeFuse, an infrared-visible fusion framework for OVSS that selectively converts complementary thermal evidence into unseen-class semantic corrections. Specifically, class-relative cue selection evaluates RGB-IR semantic responses and calibrated thermal evidence relative to RGB predictions, generating class-relative correction requests through competitive selection. Text-contrast image rectification then translates these selected requests into semantic rectification directions through text contrasts, guiding evidence-gated feature updates to realize complementary thermal evidence for unseen-class recognition. Experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate gains of up to 4.755% in unseen-class mIoU and 15.730% in strict complementary conversion rate over existing fusion methods, while maintaining competitive fusion quality.
  </details>

- **[Gaussian Primitive Fusion for Any-Resolution Multimodal Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24164)**  `arXiv:2611.24164`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Baoshuo Cai, Xinrui Jiang, Bing Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal image fusion integrates complementary observations from different modalities into a unified representation. Existing methods typically estimate modality contributions on a common discrete grid. When source modalities have different native resolutions, constructing such a grid generally involves resampling, which may introduce artifacts and affect subsequent fusion decisions. To address this problem, we propose Gaussian Primitive Fusion (GPF), which reformulates heterogeneous-resolution fusion in a shared continuous Gaussian space. Instead of first converting source images to a common resolution, GPF directly fits their native-resolution observations with shared Gaussian geometry and modality-specific content representations, and learns modality contributions within the resulting continuous representation. Once optimized, the fused representation is rendered at the finest native resolution without resampling the sources. With heterogeneous-resolution inputs, GPF produces clearer target contours and finer textures, with less blurring and blocking artifacts than resample-then-fuse pipelines. Quantitatively, it achieves the best results on multiple infrared-visible benchmarks against state-of-the-art fusion methods equipped with interpolation or super-resolution frontends.
  </details>

- **[Inherited Adapter Experts for Replay-Free Domain-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24145)**  `arXiv:2611.24145`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gyutae Oh, Hyunmin Kong, Jitae Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Replay-free domain-incremental learning trains a model on the same classes in successive domains without keeping earlier images. Per-domain learners freeze a pre-trained ViT and train an additional expert for each new domain: the small set of parameters and the classification head learned on that domain. Test images carry no domain label, so a router selects one expert for each image. Recent work trains this router. However, a training-free Mahalanobis router costs the analyzed learners under 2.7 points of final accuracy relative to always selecting each image's own-domain expert. The experts, not this router, limit accuracy. Our learner, Lamarck, therefore uses this router and strengthens the experts. Its adapters, small trainable layers that correct every image token after each deep ViT block, add 4.4 points of final accuracy on the deepfake benchmark CDDB-Hard. In addition, each expert is initialized from its predecessor at no extra cost: the domains share their classes, so an image misrouted to a later expert loses less. Against twelve frozen-backbone baselines under one protocol, Lamarck is first on CDDB-Hard by 5.2 points, second on DomainNet and OfficeHome, and highest in mean accuracy.
  </details>

- **[APS: Adaptive Partitioning-Based Frame Selection for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24079)**  `arXiv:2611.24079`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junxiang Wen, Yinxi Zhao, Jiawei Chen, Jingying Hu, Lijun Wang, Zhen Qin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) have significantly improved video understanding capabilities, yet processing long videos remains challenging due to the substantial computational cost of extensive visual inputs. Frame selection offers an efficient alternative, but many existing region-based methods rely on fixed or predefined temporal partitions, which may result in suboptimal temporal regions, compromising subsequent budget allocation and frame selection. In this work, we propose Adaptive Partitioning-Based Frame Selection (APS), a training-free framework that adapts temporal partitions to local variations in query-frame relevance. Specifically, APS formulates temporal partitioning as a complexity-regularized fitting problem, jointly optimizing partition boundaries and local models through dynamic programming. The resulting adaptive temporal partitions guide frame budget allocation based on query relevance and temporal extent, enabling informative frame selection under limited visual budgets. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench demonstrate that APS consistently improves performance across diverse LVLM backbones, achieving the highest average gains over uniform sampling among competing methods. Further analyses validate the effectiveness of adaptive partitioning and demonstrate its generalizability across model scales, similarity encoders, and advanced LVLMs.
  </details>

- **[ProtoBridge-CLIP: Observation-Masked Finding Prototypes for Medical Image--Report Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24002)**  `arXiv:2611.24002`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinrui Wu, Yun Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiology reports distinguish affirmed findings, explicit negations, and unmentioned concepts, but conventional global image--report contrastive learning often compresses these different evidence states into a single study-level target. We introduce \model{}, which combines symmetric global image--report alignment with observation-masked finding supervision and a soft relation objective over affirmed report concepts.
