Text-derived finding prototypes are aligned with local visual tokens, while uncertain and unmentioned concepts are excluded from direct finding labels. We evaluate the framework on three CT benchmarks and five chest-radiograph benchmarks, together with component ablations, paired and clinical retrieval, localization, perturbation robustness, calibration, and per-finding analyses. \model{} achieves macro-AUROC values of 73.6%, 66.5\%, and 68.8% on CT-RATE, RAD-ChestCT, and INSPECT, and 80.2%, 91.4%, 78.5%, 86.5%, and 90.8% on ChestXray14, CheXpert, PadChest, RSNA, and SIIM-ACR, respectively. Relative to the matched global-only controls, the findings and relation objectives yield consistent gains across the evaluated datasets. These results demonstrate the practical value of treating observed and unobserved report concepts differently during medical image--text representation learning.
  </details>

- **[TOMAS: Topology-Aware Operation Modeling and Adaptive Staging for Egocentric Hand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23970)**  `arXiv:2611.23970`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Guanzhong Sun, Junyi Ma, Yuxuan Wu, Erhang Zhang, Chenyang Xu, Yanzi Miao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting future hand movements from egocentric videos plays an important role in understanding human intentions and hand-object interactions. However, different manipulation intentions can lead to substantially different hand trajectories even when interacting with the same object. Existing methods have not sufficiently explored the intrinsic relationships between articulated hand movements and object semantics to capture such operation-dependent motion patterns. Therefore, we propose TOMAS, a topology-aware operation modeling and adaptive staging framework for egocentric hand forecasting. Specifically, we develop a topology-aware hand-object reasoning mechanism that associates hand skeletal structures and articulation dynamics with object semantics and spatial relationships to jointly infer potential interaction targets and latent manipulation operations. The resulting object-operation representations provide interaction conditions for future hand trajectory prediction, with supervision adapted to the annotations available in each dataset. Furthermore, we introduce an adaptive staging mechanism that organizes future hand movements into ordered latent stages with learnable temporal boundaries. Stage-specific geometric subgoals are then incorporated to guide trajectory refinement according to the underlying manipulation patterns. Experimental results on multiple publicly available benchmarks demonstrate that TOMAS achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-dimensional and multi-target hand motion forecasting.
  </details>

- **[SEE: Unlocking the Hidden Perception of Frozen VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23962)**  `arXiv:2611.23962`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Alin-Ionut Popa, Tuo Zhang, Dimitrios Dimitriadis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) grasp the overall content of an image but often miss the fine details a question hinges on, such as a small object in a large photo or a number in a dense infographic. Yet even when their answer is wrong, the region they point to often contains the evidence. We introduce Self-Elicited Evidence (SEE), a training-free method that lets a frozen VLM look again where it points. When the initial answer has low confidence, SEE prompts the model to propose regions where the details may appear, answers the question on these enlarged crops, and aggregates the answers with the original prediction through confidence-weighted voting. The model also answers with its own transcript, which helps on text-rich images. SEE needs no detector or OCR engine. Across five backbones, SEE improves every small-object benchmark by 2.3-13.1 points and outperforms five training-free baselines on small objects. With SEE, Qwen3.5-4B surpasses the direct answer of Qwen3.5-9B and matches the 6 times larger Qwen3.5-27B.
  </details>

- **[SixthView: A Benchmark for Hidden-View Reconstruction and 3D Assembly Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23891)**  `arXiv:2611.23891`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Pooya Khosravi, Jen-Feng Chang, Fnu Pramono_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inferring the unseen side of a 3D assembly requires reasoning about part placement and occlusion. Different assemblies can match the observed views while producing different hidden views, leaving the target ambiguous. We introduce SixthView, a benchmark in which models predict a sixth orthographic view from five observed views and information about the component shapes. The benchmark contains 602 synthetic polycube assemblies with 2--10 parts and requires a dense grid of part labels for the missing view, allowing both exact-view and cell-level evaluation. For each item, we exhaustively verify that all legal assemblies consistent with the exact component geometry and observed label grids yield the same target view. With these inputs, the task tests symbolic assembly and coordinate reasoning. The image-only variants additionally require shape recovery from rendered views, where occlusion can make different 3D shapes look identical. We evaluate three vision-language models under four input conditions. Across 552 items with complete evaluations, exact-grid accuracy with images plus exact text ranges from 33.2% to 85.4%. Adding exact text to separate images improves accuracy by 19.7--63.0 percentage points, whereas adding images to exact text changes it by only $-0.5$ to $+2.1$ points. Separate image panels outperform a merged sheet by 6.7--11.1 points. The results show that performance depends strongly on access to exact symbolic information, with little further improvement from images once the geometry is specified. GitHub:this https URL
  </details>

- **[NullTok: empirical-null token selection for training-free long video understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23881)**  `arXiv:2611.23881`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhang Ziyue, Juwei Jian, Baiyang Song, Yunshan Zhong, Jun Peng, Yiyi Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding remains a daunting challenge for existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), mainly due to the large number of visual tokens accumulated in long videos. Currently, frame selection and token selection are two main paradigms for addressing this issue, both aiming to select relevant visual information to form a compact input for MLLMs. A natural approach combines these two paradigms by selecting frames before pruning tokens, but noisy frame-level relevance estimates can cause irreversible evidence loss. In this paper, we study this problem from the perspective of assessing the spatiotemporal distribution of query-specific evidence and propose a novel and training-free approach termed NULLTOK. For every patch across all frames, we construct an empirical-null distribution from 64 group-matched irrelevant control queries, capturing the scores expected in the absence of target-specific evidence. Target affinities are calibrated against these same-position references into right-tail Monte Carlo p-values, which are discrete super-uniform under a conditional exchangeability assumption. Calibrated screening and a high-recall semantic guard produce candidate tokens for deterministic visual deduplication and joint spatiotemporal budget allocation. To validate NullTok, we apply it to two representative MLLMs and conduct extensive experiments on a set of video benchmarks. The experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness under a bounded visual token budget. For example, it achieves 65.81% and 68.85% on Video-MME using frozen LLaVA-Video-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B backbones, respectively, at a cap of 64 full-frame equivalents (FFE), without subtitles or audio. These results outperform all evaluated baselines on Video-MME under the same budget for both backbones. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Radiology Reports in 3D Chest CT: Results of the ReXGrounding Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23862)**  `arXiv:2611.23862`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Baharoon, Mahmoud Hussain Alabbad, Sung Eun Kim, Xiaoman Zhang, Lucas Bijnens, Zirong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Segmenting each reported finding in a radiology scan would allow findings to be measured and tracked over time and let AI systems generate reports that are verifiable, but so far AI models have performed poorly at this task. To advance this task, we organized the ReXGrounding Challenge at MICCAI 2026, the first challenge dedicated to it. Given a non-contrast chest CT scan and the set of findings from its report, participants trained models to produce the corresponding set of 3D segmentations. The challenge used 2,992 training and 200 validation scans from the ReXGroundingCT dataset, and evaluated methods on a held-out test set of 300 scans with 582 findings, exhaustively annotated by board-certified radiologists. In total, 48 teams from 15 countries made 760 submissions, and the best method reached a Dice of 0.375 and a hit rate of 0.77, matching agreement between two independent radiologist groups (Dice 0.32, hit rate 0.76, on a 50-scan subset). On the 100 scans of the pre-challenge benchmark, the winner reached a Dice of 0.390, against 0.285 for VoxTell, the best model before the challenge, which five of the six best teams fine-tuned. Non-focal findings remained far harder to segment than focal ones, instance F1 stayed below inter-reader agreement (0.34 vs. 0.47), and models sometimes segmented the right abnormality in the wrong location. Top methods thus match radiologist agreement in Dice, but non-focal findings, findings with multiple instances, and adherence to the anatomical location described in the text remain open problems.
  </details>

- **[TOGBench: Benchmarking and Improving Tiny-Object Generation Fidelity in Aerial Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23840)**  `arXiv:2611.23840`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chanhwi Jeong, Dongmin You, Seungwook Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in deep generative models now enable the synthesis of realistic imagery. However, aerial imagery is dominated by tiny objects, such as vehicles, ships, and aircraft, which occupy only a few pixels owing to the overhead, wide-area nature of aerial imaging. Existing generative models struggle substantially more with tiny objects than with larger ones. This limitation has been overlooked, as prior work on generative evaluation emphasizes prompt following or human preference rather than whether small objects are rendered faithfully. To address this gap, we introduce TOGBench, a quantitative, object-level benchmark for evaluating tiny-object generation fidelity in aerial imagery. We further propose two simple train-/inference-time techniques that improve the ability of layout-to-image generators to render small objects, raising their performance on TOGBench.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Sink Token: Sink-Guided Key Editing in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23516)**  `arXiv:2611.23516`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Xiangyu Zhou, Dongxiao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In vision-language models (VLMs), a few sink tokens attract a disproportionate share of attention. While previous studies have examined why these tokens attract attention, we investigate how the sink-key direction affects attention to image and question tokens. Across four probed VLMs, boosting the magnitude of positive sink-aligned components increases attention on average, whereas boosting negative components decreases it; these responses persist through later layers. This identifies the native sink key as a reference for controlling attention beyond its own position. Motivated by this finding, we propose SHRINK, a training-free method that partially reduces sink-aligned components across visible keys during generation. On nine benchmarks under sampled reasoning, it improves mean accuracy by 5.1 and 2.7 percentage points on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B, with smaller gains on the other two main models, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Gemma-3-4B. Beyond SHRINK, rescaling sink-aligned key components can replace the attention operations of two existing attention-steering methods, GIFT and ACG, with comparable accuracy on hallucination benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[MAVE: Reconstructing Editable 3D Blockouts from Film Shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464)**  `arXiv:2611.22464`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Chang, Jian Yang, Jiangnan Shao, Xiaofeng Wang, Zhaoxin Fan, Renjing Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VibePrevisBench: Evaluating Agentic Coding for 4D Scene Generation in Blender](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15789)**  `arXiv:2611.15789`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhe Chen, Muxi Diao, Lele Yang, Wenbin Xu, Xuyang Liu, Qile Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controllable real-world video generation models can condition on a rendered graybox proxy video, which fixes layout, motion and camera. Coding agents can build such videos by generating a 4D scene in Blender, yet no benchmark checks both these rendered proxy videos and the complex, multi-shot scenes behind them. We introduce VibePrevisBench, a benchmark for 4D scene generation: an agent turns a brief into an executable 4D scene and renders its proxy video. Its 242 tasks span four input kinds, including scripts, photos, continuations, and reference videos. Each delivery is scored along two evaluation dimensions. Rule-based Scene Check re-runs the code and compares the exported 4D scene with hidden ground truth facts in nine dimensions. VLM-based Screen Check asks a VLM judge whether facts that pass the rules are recognizable in the proxy video. We analyze the evaluation results and trajectories of 11 frontier coding agents and identify three key observations. First, multimodal understanding separates the agents, especially in understanding space and camera motion. Second, all agents seem to already master basic camera work and staging, and fail at challenging requests. Third, agents inspect their own renders but fail to correct their errors. Agents thus need better visual understanding of space and camera, explicit checks for challenging requests, and item-by-item verification of renders against the brief.
  </details>

- **[2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12805)**  `arXiv:2611.12805`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $\Phi$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.
  </details>

- **[MotionGraphicsBench: Evaluating Motion Graphics Generation with Programmatic Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10592)**  `arXiv:2611.10592`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaju Ma, Ashvini Kumar Jindal, Chuhua Wang, Sumithra Bhakthavatsalam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, outperforming frontier VLM judges by 4.4%-12.8% while achieving at least 90% recall for both human approvals and rejections. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 5.0% to 68.0%. Across 15 models at their highest reasoning effort, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We fine-tune Qwen3.8-27B with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) using our verification programs as rewards, improving pass@1 from 30.31% to 52.19% on held-out instances. Our project page is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhoneValue-Bench: A Dual-Ground-Truth Scorecard Benchmark for Smartphone Recommerce Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00791)**  `arXiv:2611.00791`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Junjie Huang, Xiaozhen Zhong, Du Tiantian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote smartphone recommerce is scaling quickly, and locking a repurchase offer from seller photos and diagnostic screens before the handset arrives has become a central operational task. Existing multimodal resources mainly target defect localization, catalog attributes, or screen reading. They overlook payout-facing scorecards with dual cosmetic and operational ground truth under a declared input contract. This gap stems from two challenges: (1) the lack of a large weakly labeled trade-in corpus kept separate from a compact audited ranking cut, and (2) architectures that either flatten unordered photo bags into one prompt or collapse evaluation into a single price scalar. To address these challenges, we propose PhoneValue-Bench, a dual-ground-truth recommerce framework with PhoneValue-Corpus for weak training on 21,550 orders and 278,748 images, and PV-Rank as the only leaderboard on 335 quality-gated orders. Systems are ranked by CardScore under LeadGate, with Cosmetic Fill and Operational Fill as diagnostics, while grade and price stay off the board. Notably, ValueAgent is a six-stage perception-fusion reference that canonicalizes views, detects regional defects, reads screenshots, and fuses evidence under warehouse-style rules, so bag-of-images prompting is not the only evaluation unit. On the same PV-Rank cut and frozen scorer, this full-system and input-contract comparison finds ValueAgent at 84.1 CardScore against the strongest Flat Vision-Language Model at 69.9, with the largest reported gap in CosFill, while OpsFill remains limited under the two declared contracts. CardScore measures field-fill completeness, not quote accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CAPTOR: a vision-language foundation model for Chest-Abdomen-Pelvis Oncologic CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14226)**  `arXiv:2610.14226`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Séléna Jean-Mactoux, Sophie Loizillon, Ghina Jardali, Amandine Crombé, Nathalie Lassau, Alexandre Bône_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objectives: Radiologists interpreting oncologic chest-abdomen-pelvis (CAP) CT must also detect non-oncologic conditions, yet existing CT foundation models are trained on single anatomical regions or on non-oncologic data. We developed and evaluated CAPTOR, a vision-language foundation model for oncologic CAP CT that uses an anatomically structured preprocessing strategy to learn from whole examinations.Materials & Methods: CAPTOR is a contrastive vision-language model pretrained on 25,512 predominantly oncologic CAP CT-report pairs (single or multiple series) from a retrospective multicenter dataset (North America, Europe, Asia). It was compared with five CT foundation models, a larger-scale variant (CAPTOR-100K) and an ablation without anatomical preprocessing (CAPTOR-NoPre) on classification and retrieval using held-out internal and public test sets, and on survival prediction using TumorImagingBench. Confidence intervals and pairwise comparisons used patient-level bootstrap and permutation tests.Results: On 10,794 test studies, CAPTOR achieved higher AUC than all five models on CAP (0.767 vs. 0.695 for the next-best model; p<0.001) and oncologic subsets (0.784 vs. 0.734; p<0.001), with superior retrieval on both, and ranked first on three of four oncologic survival benchmarks. Despite substantially fewer training examinations, it performed comparably to CAPTOR-100K. Removing the anatomically structured preprocessing reduced classification performance, including on the institutionally held-out test subset (macro-AUC 0.737 vs. 0.691 for CAPTOR-NoPre, p<0.01).Conclusion: Anatomically structured preprocessing enabled whole-examination CAP CT interpretation, including multiple-series acquisitions, and improved downstream performance. CAPTOR outperformed state-of-the-art CT foundation models on CAP and oncologic subsets while matching a substantially larger-scale variant.
  </details>

- **[SciFigQual-Bench: Scientific Figure Quality Assessment with Manuscript-Linked Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27084)**  `arXiv:2607.27084`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Huiqi Liang, Xiaozhen Zhong, Weidong Cai, Lequan Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figure quality is bound to the manuscript: a crop can be visually clear and still contradict its caption or the paragraph that cites it. Most image quality assessment and chart-understanding methods target a detached visual surface or a question-answering score, rather than alignment among the figure, its caption, and the citing text. A single end-to-end prompt that sees the image and the text together still mixes what is visible with what the caption and the citing paragraph claim, so fluent wording can raise a visual score and missing text can be treated as low quality. To address this gap, we introduce SciFigQual-Bench, a manuscript-linked benchmark for published CS-conference figures that binds each figure to its caption and to index-resolved citing paragraphs, and scores visual clarity, layout, caption consistency, context consistency, and misleading risk, leaving a dimension unevaluated when its evidence is absent. SFQ-Agent reads the image and the text in separate calls and records modality-specific evidence. A cross-modal judge scores caption and citing-text alignment from those reports, and a deterministic runner keeps the visual scores, sets misleading risk by a fixed rule, and applies the written caps, so each dimension follows the rubric instead of a single end-to-end prompt. Experiments comparing this staged judge with single-pass and OCR-sidecar protocols find the closest point-estimate fit to mean human ratings under staged judging, while the gap between protocols remains small. Caption consistency remains the main gap, and agreement with the rater mean answers a different question from agreement among human raters. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PrismRestore: A Compact Restoration Toolbox for Agentic Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24915)**  `arXiv:2611.24915`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaocheng Yu, Kui Jiang, Jing Cao, Yiang Chen, Xiaocheng Feng, Junjun Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. We propose PrismRestore, which constructs a compact bank of callable restoration actions from a single frozen all-in-one model. Our approach builds on task-dependent yet overlapping channel responses in the shared model. An offline task-prior search uses activation and gradient statistics to group channels with similar response profiles, then searches task-specific modulation coefficients over non-representative channels while leaving representative channels unmodulated. The resulting prior bank enables explicit action selection and composition without additional restoration backbones or task-specific adapter modules. A state-conditioned policy learns to compose these actions using Group Relative Policy Optimization. Its objective separately normalizes fidelity and perceptual feedback within groups of trajectories sampled for the same input, and combines terminal evaluation with return-to-go derived from stepwise quality changes. Experiments on synthetic composite-degradation and real-world multi-task benchmarks show improvements over the evaluated all-in-one baselines and restoration quality competitive with multi-model agents. PrismRestore achieves these results with a single restoration backbone and faster inference than the evaluated agentic baselines.
  </details>

- **[ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24904)**  `arXiv:2611.24904`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shagun Mittal, Raymond A. Yeh, Satish Ukkusuri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.
  </details>

- **[Ultimate3D: Teaching Multimodal LLMs 3D Camera-Object Relations by Large-Scale Visual Instruction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24795)**  `arXiv:2611.24795`  `cs.CV`  
  _Liu He, Yizhi Song, Ruiqi Xu, Baris Sarper Tezcan, Xiao Zeng, Albert Y.C. Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with accurately capturing camera-object relations, especially for object orientation, camera viewpoint, and camera shots. This stems from the fact that existing MLLMs are trained on images with limited diverse camera-object relations and corresponding textual descriptions. To address this, we propose a synthetic generation pipeline to create large-scale 3D visual instruction datasets. Our framework takes 3D assets as input and uses rendering and diffusion-based image generation models to create photorealistic images preserving precise camera-object relations. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) are used to generate text prompts for guiding visual instruction tuning and controlling image generation. We create Ultimate3D, a large-scale 3D-grounded 2D visual instruction dataset. Ultimate3D consists of 240K VQAs with precise camera-object annotations, and corresponding benchmark. MLLMs fine-tuned on our proposed dataset outperform commercial models by a large margin, achieving an average accuracy improvement of 29.7% on camera-object relation recognition tasks. Our code, dataset, and benchmark will contribute to broad MLLM applications.
  </details>

- **[A$^3$Bench: Compositional Reasoning over Aggressive Interactions in Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24762)**  `arXiv:2611.24762`  `cs.CV`  
  _Austin C Baker, Raiyaan Abdullah, Daniel Zeev Yoffe, Shruti Vyas, Yogesh S Rawat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on general video understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to reason about participant roles in aggressive interactions remains largely unexplored. Existing violence and crime datasets primarily evaluate whether aggression occurs, without testing whether models can identify who performed the action, who received it, and how participants are related within the scene. To address this gap, we introduce A$^3$Bench, a benchmark to study aggressive action and association in videos. The benchmark evaluates whether models can jointly recognize aggressive actions, distinguish aggressors from victims and bystanders, and correctly bind actions to participants under challenging distractor settings. We benchmark 17 multimodal models and find that they struggle substantially on fine-grained aggressive-scene understanding. Performance degrades consistently as reasoning complexity increases, with models frequently confusing aggressors with victims or bystanders, revealing a systematic failure in role-action association rather than coarse aggression recognition alone. We further observe that increasing model scale or generic reasoning models experience limited improvement. Finally, we explore a simple role-graph prompting strategy that encourages structured reasoning over participant interactions, leading to improvements in compositional reasoning. Our findings highlight that reliable aggressive-scene understanding remains a major challenge for current MLLMs and motivate future research on role-aware video reasoning beyond coarse violence detection. Code and dataset:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[HandWorldBench: A Unified Benchmark for Hand-Centric World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24731)**  `arXiv:2611.24731`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaolu Wang, Jiacheng Xu, Lihong Wang, Shuqing Wang, Jianjie Fang, Ziyou Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in world models are opening new possibilities for fine-grained embodied tasks, with hand-object interaction offering a particularly demanding setting. Modeling these interactions involves capturing how hand actions change objects and how the resulting states persist over time. Yet systematic evaluation tailored to these hand-centric capabilities remains underdeveloped. To address this gap, we introduce HandWorldBench, a unified benchmark that systematically evaluates hand-centric world models across human hands, simulated dexterous hands, and real dexterous hands along six dimensions: visual quality, hand fidelity, appearance consistency, instruction following, physical consistency, and temporal coherence. To construct the benchmark, we develop a data curation pipeline that extracts hand-object interaction clips and screens them for quality, yielding 20,500 high-quality egocentric clips from over 600 hours of footage. We then manually select 500 clips covering seven task families for evaluation. Benchmarking eleven models under a unified protocol reveals that stronger hand-pose control does not consistently translate into coherent object-state evolution and persistence or robust cross-embodiment performance, with several specialized hand-control models underperforming general-purpose text/image-to-video generators on dexterous-hand tasks.
  </details>

- **[Can Omni-modal LLMs Understand Spoken Intentions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24706)**  `arXiv:2611.24706`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zijie Xin, Tianyi Wang, Daoze Zhang, Jie Yang, Changli Tang, Qijie Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (omni-modal LLMs) can respond to spoken requests directly through voice and video calls. Such requests rely on the scene shared by the user and the assistant, so the same utterance may call for different help or for no response. Most audio-visual benchmarks provide questions as separate text input, and interactive benchmarks mostly score a response as a whole. It thus remains unclear whether a model recognizes when the user is asking for help, and whether its reply meets the user's need once the request is recognized. We propose O-IN-Bench to evaluate how well omni-modal LLMs understand spoken intentions. A model watches a complete video with native speech and outputs one text response, or [SILENCE] when no response is needed. O-IN-Bench contains 1,000 Chinese and English samples in five categories and 16 subcategories. Contrastive pairs combine the same request with different scene conditions to test whether the response changes with the context. A sample-specific rubric judges language, intent, and content separately, and O-IN-Engine verifies each rubric against the video. Across 30 omni-modal model configurations, the best overall score is 59.6, achieved by Gemini3.7-Flash. Gemini3.7-Flash passes 74.4% of the intent judgments but only 42.6% of the content judgments. When the speech is a conversation between peers rather than a request to the assistant, even strong models often keep responding. When a reply must satisfy several people, the best score is only 25.9. These results indicate that recognizing a request and meeting its requirements are separate abilities, and O-IN-Bench measures each of them.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Species Splits: A Challenging Fine-Grained Benchmark for Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24672)**  `arXiv:2611.24672`  `cs.CV`  
  _Christian Lange, Joakim Bruslund Haurum, Bingchen Zhao, Oisin Mac Aodha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Widely used category discovery benchmarks have become increasingly saturated and often fail to reflect real-world discovery settings, making long-term evaluation of new methods difficult. We introduce PS-SPLIT, a large-scale fine-grained benchmark for category discovery, motivated by taxonomic splitting: the real-world process by which what was once considered a single species is found to comprise multiple distinct ones. PS-SPLIT contains 79K globally distributed, high-quality, community-collected bird images spanning 771 species, derived from 257 recent real expert-verified taxonomic splits, with associated geographic metadata and full taxonomic hierarchy. The visual differences between categories arising from real taxonomic splits can be very subtle, often requiring significant human expertise to tell apart, making PS-SPLIT a challenging benchmark which we hope will assist in advancing fine-grained category discovery research. Based on taxonomic splitting, we introduce the task of discovery by category splitting, where the goal is to determine which known categories should be subdivided into new finer-grained subcategories. We adapt four generalized category discovery methods to this setting, evaluate existing methods, and show that there is substantial room for future progress.
  </details>

- **[AggCLIP: Aggregating CLIP for Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24643)**  `arXiv:2611.24643`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhi Li, Hanyu Wang, Fei Shen, Zhengtao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection (ZSMAD) aims to identify both appearance and geometric defects in novel categories without relying on target-specific training data, making it especially valuable in real-world scenarios constrained by data scarcity, privacy concerns, or high annotation costs. While recent approaches successfully adapt vision-language models like CLIP to achieve this open-vocabulary generalization, they still face significant challenges: (i) severe geometric information loss during 3D-to-2D point cloud rendering; (ii) spatially noisy 2D feature maps produced by raw CLIP patch embeddings, compounded by shallow late-stage modality fusion; and (iii) severe cross-modal semantic misalignment due to decoupled processing streams. To address these limitations, we propose AggCLIP, a novel framework that adapts frozen CLIP representations for ZSMAD by explicitly incorporating local geometric structure and visual spatial context. Specifically, to accurately localize fine-grained defects, we design a Geometry-Aware Spatial Aggregation (GSA) module that refines back-projected 3D point features via density-adaptive neighborhood weighting, alongside a Context-Aware Spatial Aggregation (CSA) module that smooths multi-scale 2D patch embeddings using a lightweight trainable adapter. Furthermore, to prevent semantic drift between modalities, we introduce a Dual-Stream Prompt Contrastive (DSPC) loss, which aligns the abstract concepts of normality and abnormality across the point cloud and RGB streams. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed AggCLIP framework achieves state-of-the-art performance on the MVTec 3D-AD and Eyecandies benchmarks under both one-vs-rest and cross-dataset zero-shot settings, notably surpassing the strongest competitive methods by up to 2.9% in pixel-level AUPRO.
  </details>

- **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597)**  `arXiv:2611.24597`  `cs.CV`  
  _Salman Mohammad, Furkan Kasım, Manuel Günther_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SCRiD: Spatial and Contextual References for Generalizable AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24524)**  `arXiv:2611.24524`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Tan, Zekai Zuo, Boyang Chen, Jinghui Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizable AI-generated video detection remains challenging because natural motion and content variations can resemble synthesis artifacts, making isolated video discrepancies unreliable indicators of authenticity. To address this challenge, we propose Spatial and Contextual Reference-informed Detection (SCRiD), a framework that interprets video variations through complementary spatial and contextual references. Specifically, we introduce Matched-Coordinate Residual Response (MCR) to characterize temporal residual associations under motion and coordinate references with matched subpixel sampling conditions. We further develop Real-Conditioned Score Adaptation (RCSA), which learns a content-conditioned denoising reference from authentic videos and probes its responses across multiple noise scales and quality views to guide feature adaptation. To integrate these complementary sources of evidence, we design Reversible Evidence Exchange (REE) to enable information exchange through reversible symplectic updates. We train SCRiD using synthetic videos from only 4 generators and evaluate it across 88 generator or task subsets spanning 6 benchmarks. Our method achieves average ACC and AP of 72.72% and 74.23%, respectively, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.24 and 0.81 percentagethis http URLand robustness experiments further validate the effectiveness of the proposed components and the stability of SCRiD under common post-processing operations.
  </details>

- **[Comparing Each Generation with Independent Siblings Gives Video World Model Post-Training a Proper Objective for Outcome Frequencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24512)**  `arXiv:2611.24512`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xin Li, Yiwen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models are used as simulators, so when a first frame leaves several physical outcomes open, repeated generations should produce each outcome as often as it occurs in the data. Physics post-training scores every generation against the single recorded future of its history, which makes single videos more faithful, but the expected score is linear in the model distribution and is maximized by always producing the most likely outcome. The log-likelihood of the recorded outcome is maximized by the data distribution, and its gradient weights agreement with the record by the inverse of how often the model produces the generated outcome, a frequency that other generations of the same history with independent noise, its siblings, can estimate. To train with this weight from a finite group, we propose SibPO, which performs Sibling-paired Policy Optimization: siblings estimate a truncated inverse-frequency weight for agreement with the record, and a second term subtracts the same weight times the agreement with the siblings, which makes the expected objective a strictly proper divergence over outcome cells at every group size. On PisaBench, SibPO lowers the KS distance to simulated landing times by 9.1-10.2% relative to the strongest method outside its reward family on three of four video models without degrading single videos, and it lowers the error of estimated robot policy success rates by 13%.
  </details>

- **[MLIE-CoT: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via Multi-Level Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24433)**  `arXiv:2611.24433`  `cs.CV`  
  _Baichuan Yang, Dongyuan Xu, Zhao Yang, Yong Liu, Bing Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has proven effective in improving the reasoning abilities of large language models by breaking complex tasks into step-by-step solutions. In recent years, researchers have attempted to extend CoT reasoning to visual generative models to improve their ability to handle complex tasks. However, existing CoT methods for text-to-image generation still rely mainly on textual reasoning and therefore cannot fully use and preserve the correct semantic information already present in an image, as even the most detailed language cannot capture all visual information. With advances in image generation, models such as FLUX and BAGEL can generate images conditioned on visual inputs. Recent work has attempted to carry images forward as context in the chain of thought, but this introduces another limitation: unsuitable earlier images can strongly interfere with the model, while repeated whole-image regeneration introduces new hallucinations at each round and causes them to accumulate. To address these issues, we propose MLIE-CoT, a visual chain-of-thought framework based on multi-level image editing. MLIE-CoT learns to select the repair granularity from three levels, preserving correct semantics whenever possible during reasoning while reducing the effect of incorrect content. To improve the ability of multimodal models to route errors of different severity to appropriate repair levels, we construct an image-repair dataset covering different error cases and use it to train a repair-level router. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MLIE-CoT significantly outperforms both its base models and methods using fixed repair strategies, producing clear improvements across multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[PhysResponse: Probing the Physics of Video Generation with Controlled Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24419)**  `arXiv:2611.24419`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuhan Li, Shuran Zhang, Haixin Tian, Baonuo Rao, Weipeng Li, Yujia Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models are increasingly viewed as world simulators, which requires not only plausible motion but dynamics that respond correctly to specified physical parameters. Existing models, datasets and benchmarks, however, largely target physical plausibility, leaving this physical parameter responsiveness insufficiently explored. We introduce PhysResponse, a benchmark that measures it at two levels: scene-conditioned simulator inversion recovers the effective gravity, friction and initial speed of generated motion, and event evaluation tests outcomes near and across critical transitions. Evaluating ten image-to-video models, we find that even the lowest parameter errors exceed those of physically correct videos by over an order of magnitude, that models rarely reproduce both outcomes of a transition pair, and that the two levels expose complementary failures that visual metrics such as PSNR miss. To supply the missing training signal, we construct 72,000 simulated videos of 20 multi-object interaction types with controlled parameters; fine-tuning on them lowers parameter errors, even on held-out interaction types, and transfers to real videos and an external physics benchmark. Continued training with a numerical encoder further lowers all three errors on held-out types. PhysResponse provides a foundation for world simulators that follow physical conditions, not just appearances.
  </details>

- **[StrideWorld: Benchmarking Scene Rigidity and Action Consistency in Interactive Video World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24408)**  `arXiv:2611.24408`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chuang Wang, Rui Nie, Jie Song, Junyuan Gao, Jiahe Song, Haitao Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive video world models turn user commands into the exploration of a generated scene, which requires the scene to keep its 3D structure as the camera moves and each action to keep a stable effect. Existing benchmarks mostly evaluate these properties against references fitted to the video under test, either a scene reconstructed from its own frames or a camera trajectory aligned to the command path with a free scale. Such references can absorb gradual scene deformation and changes in pace and route length, so these errors can pass undetected. We therefore introduce StrideWorld, a benchmark whose references are fixed before the motion they judge. It evaluates scene rigidity against the ground-truth geometry of the start view, and action consistency against the stride of each action, the camera motion of one command. The stride is measured on the model's first steps and then used to test the steps that follow, so each model is judged only against its own steps. Its measurements are validated on rendered videos with known camera motion, and its scores agree with human judgments. Across \nvideos{} videos generated by \nmodels{} open models from $140$ start views, all models but one deform their scenes as the camera moves, with offsets up to $7.3$ times those of a rigid rendering along the same camera path. Most models keep a steady pace when a single action is held. Along composed routes, several models often stall, and most completed routes end away from the positions predicted by each model's own strides. Only the two models that scale commands by the estimated scene depth bring most routes close to these positions. Fine-tuning two models on a few rendered clips removes most of their mid-route stalls. Code, data, and a leaderboard are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CanopyKV: Remember beyond the Crop for Fast Active Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24373)**  `arXiv:2611.24373`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lei Yang, Dayan Wu, Yiliang Zhu, Xinze Liu, Hengjie Zhu, Zihao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Active visual reasoning enables multimodal models to answer challenging visual questions by gathering fine-grained evidence through iterative image inspection and tool use. However, accumulating crops and reasoning tokens expands the key-value (KV) cache, making compression important for reducing memory and inference costs. Existing KV compression methods typically select historical tokens based on attention, recency, or redundancy. These criteria do not explicitly distinguish locally replaced evidence from complementary information needed for future exploration: each crop refreshes a local region, while earlier views may still support subsequent decisions. Discarding such evidence can impair accuracy or require additional search, while scoring and cache reorganization can offset the computational savings from compression. We introduce CanopyKV, a training-free KV compression framework that uses parent-child crop relationships to retain complementary evidence, keeps retained history stable, and protects the state needed for ongoing reasoning. Tool metadata provides retention signals without additional attention scoring, while compact KV storage reduces subsequent attention costs. Across VisualProbe, V*, HR-Bench, and MME-Lite, CanopyKV achieves state-of-the-art answer accuracy and end-to-end efficiency among the evaluated KV compression methods. On MME-Lite, it retains approximately one-quarter of Dense's total KV while improving accuracy by 10.5 percentage points and accelerating complete reasoning by $1.64\times$.
  </details>

- **[One Affinity, Two Axes: Bidirectional Slot Attention for Iterative Aggregation across the Structure--Detail Spectrum in Visual Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24371)**  `arXiv:2611.24371`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihang Wang, Wenkai Zhu, Lingdong Kong, Teng Yan, Alan Liang, Shuyuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aggregating dense patch tokens into compact and discriminative global descriptors is central to visual retrieval. Although foundation models provide rich local features, existing aggregation heads still rely on single-pass mappings and lack an explicit mechanism to coordinate structural and fine-grained cues. Therefore, we propose Bidirectional Slot Attention (BiSA), which reformulates aggregation as an iterative coupled system. Cross-Axis Consensus (CAC) progressively refines learnable slots by interpreting a single shared token--slot affinity along two complementary axes. Along the competition axis, slots vie for each token under a temperature shared across all of them, determining token ownership and inducing a non-redundant scene decomposition. Along the selection axis, a slot-specific temperature governs the breadth of each slot's response, spanning a spectrum from broad layouts to sparse fine-grained details. Their consensus couples non-redundant decomposition with multi-granular slot specialization. Furthermore, the two axes expose intrinsic token-reliability cues at no extra cost: a token's claim strength under competition and its salience under selection. Progressive Token Filtering (PTF) converts these cues into a retention probability that down-weights unreliable tokens before the next CAC iteration, without any segmentation branch or external prior, forming a self-reinforcing loop of competitive decomposition, selective focusing, and feedback purification. Across all three retrieval tasks, BiSA leads on five of six place recognition benchmarks (+4.2 R@1 on Baidu Mall), tops all 16 metrics of drone--satellite geo-localization (+8.4 R@1 at 150 m), and improves instance-level retrieval on ROxford-Hard by 4.5 mAP points. Notably, the selection axis adds only 20 learnable scalars, yet improves average R@1 by 2.8 points.
  </details>

- **[SCOUT: Risk-Guided Foundation-Model Escalation for Robust Tissue Detection in Whole-Slide Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24366)**  `arXiv:2611.24366`  `cs.CV`  
  _Cristian Tommasino, Antonio M. Rinaldi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable tissue detection is essential for whole-slide image analysis, yet high average segmentation accuracy can conceal failures involving pale tissue, small fragments, and heterogeneous staining. Pathology foundation models offer transferable representations, but exhaustive inference is computationally demanding and may introduce false-positive tissue predictions. We propose SCOUT (Selective Correction of Uncertain Tissue), a risk-guided cascade that selectively refines an initial thumbnail-based tissue mask. A lightweight audit network estimates the spatial distribution of segmentation errors and prioritizes candidate regions for processing by a frozen pathology foundation model. An asymmetric consensus rule integrates the resulting predictions with the initial mask to recover missed tissue while limiting false positives. A calibrated slide-level review score additionally controls the marginal rate of unflagged case-group failures under the calibration assumptions. Evaluation on a public multi-source, multi-stain benchmark shows that SCOUT reduces the proportion of case groups requiring mask correction from 38.1% for the best released detector to 18.6%, with particularly pronounced improvements in immunohistochemistry. Source-held-out experiments demonstrate better generalization than a segmentation network trained on the same annotations, while selective inference achieves an observed failure rate no higher than processing all candidate regions. Downstream, lymph-node classification with SCOUT masks is non-inferior to that obtained with two of the three released detectors evaluated. A variant built on an openly licensed foundation model is publicly released as an installable Python package with trained weights. These results support risk-guided foundation-model inference as an effective approach to tissue detection across heterogeneous histopathology data.
  </details>

- **[Pixel2Perspective: A Benchmark for Multi-Modal and Multi-View Generalization in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24327)**  `arXiv:2611.24327`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aditya Gandhamal, Aniruddh Sikdar, Arya Kishor, Suresh Sundaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied AI systems, such as drones and autonomous vehicles, improve navigation by enhancing perception in environments containing unseen objects. While Visual Language Models (VLMs) offer strong image understanding, their real-world application is underexplored. For effective deployment, open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) models, leveraging models like CLIP to assign pixel-level labels from text, must be robust to outdoor conditions, adverse weather, and varying viewpoints. Despite their potential, these models encounter significant challenges in cross-domain generalization, hindering their practical use in embodied AI applications. In this work, we present Pixel2Perspective, a benchmark for multi-angle object localization and open-vocabulary segmentation across both aerial and ground perspectives. This benchmark supports thorough evaluation across different viewpoints and sensor modalities. We assess foundational models like CLIP and advanced OVSS models using a curated dataset that spans varied outdoor conditions, sim-to-real scenarios, and multiple sensors, including infrared, to support the development of robust and generalizable perception systems. We also present a novel framework, Multi-Source RGB-IR Uncertainty Quantification (MSRIQ), which jointly models aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties across RGB and infrared imagery. This approach supports robust evaluation of open-vocabulary segmentation models across diverse environmental conditions. Our work sets a robustness-driven benchmark, providing key insights and laying the foundation for future research in open-world embodied AI perception. The benchmark and project page are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[AnchorUp: Anchoring Feature Upsampling on a Classical Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24296)**  `arXiv:2611.24296`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kotaro Horikawa, Keisuke Toida, Junya Komori, Kazuhiro Hotta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision foundation models produce semantically strong features on a coarse patch grid, and dense prediction needs them at pixel resolution. Existing learned upsamplers close that gap by regressing the high-resolution feature map from the low-resolution one, typically through cross-attention driven by the input image. We observe that most of what they regress needs no learning: plain bilinear interpolation already reproduces the smooth regions of the target, and recent benchmarks even report bilinear and bicubic on par with several learned upsamplers. The rest is mostly edges and fine details, which suggests that these learned upsamplers spend much of their capacity re-computing the smooth content that classical interpolation provides. We therefore introduce AnchorUp, which stops predicting the high-resolution feature map and predicts only its correction from the interpolation. It keeps a parameter-free classical upsampler as an anchor, obtains a second prediction from a small guidance network, and blends the two through a single learnable scalar gate. Because the anchor supplies the smooth content, the guidance network only has to correct what remains. An analysis of the learned correction confirms that it concentrates on edges and fine details, and so the network can be small: AnchorUp uses 167,425 parameters, a quarter of the budget of the strongest baseline, NAF, and runs at 64% of its latency. Trained once on DINOv3 with only the first of NAF's two training stages, and evaluated on that backbone plus six held-out ones, it is already on par with NAF trained through both stages on Pascal VOC and Cityscapes. Our method surpasses it on DAVIS-2017 video object segmentation; the gain holds on every backbone and is larger in contour accuracy F. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CROMPT: Adapting CLIP via Cross-Modal Prompting for Robust Zero-Shot Generalization under Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24284)**  `arXiv:2611.24284`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aniruddh Sikdar, Arya Kishor, Snehashis Dhibar, Suresh Sundaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain adaptation for semantic segmentation is challenging when target-domain images are unavailable during training. Zero-shot domain adaptation addresses this by adapting models using only textual descriptions of the target domain. However, existing prompt-learning methods are not well suited for ResNet-based CLIP vision backbones or pixel-level cross-modal feature alignment. We propose CROMPT, a prompt-learning framework for zero-shot domain adaptation in semantic segmentation. CROMPT introduces Cross-Modal Feature Augmentation (CROMA), combining Random Visual Prompting with Hybrid Textual Prompting (HTP) to jointly adapt visual and textual representations. We further propose CROMA-VL, which leverages vision-language models to generate domain-conditioned prompts, and a progressive easy-to-hard adaptation strategy for improved domain invariance. Experiments on two benchmark datasets demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines and state-of-the-art methods, achieving 1.63-2.63% mIoU gains while maintaining inference-time complexity. These results establish CROMPT as an effective and efficient framework for zero-shot multi-domain semantic segmentation. OpenReview:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Look Closer, Look Back, or Wait for Agentic Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24267)**  `arXiv:2611.24267`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Li, Zhitao Gao, Lingling Zhang, Wenxuan Huang, YiCheng Xiao, Chen Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video models must answer questions about a live video under strict memory and latency limits. Feeding only the latest few frames keeps present perception sharp, whereas adding past frames dilutes attention on the current scene and triggers hallucinations. Recent cascade methods retrieve past frames when uncertain, but they still fail when current frames are blurry or when decisive future clues have not appeared yet. We introduce ActiveForager, a streaming video agent that maintains a clean short working window and gathers extra evidence only on demand. For each query, the agent either answers directly, zooms into high-resolution recent frames, recalls past events from memory, or waits for upcoming frames. When recalling history, a shared-weight sub-agent verifies candidate clips in isolation and returns a compact evidence summary. A stream-decoupled attention mask then keeps historical evidence from interfering with present visual tokens. We train this policy using counterfactual information gain, which rewards a tool call only when the gathered evidence makes the true answer more likely and prevents tool collapse across all actions. Across Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B on OVO-Bench, ActiveForager improves real-time perception, historical recall, and proactive response at once, lifting overall accuracy on Qwen3-VL-8B from 47.6% to 65.9%. It also doubles zero-shot accuracy on the multi-hop Video-Holmes benchmark while adding minimal visual tokens per query.
  </details>

- **[Do Thinking VLMs Need to Keep Looking at Long Videos? Write It Down, Then Look Away](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24259)**  `arXiv:2611.24259`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Li, Zhitao Gao, Lingling Zhang, Heng Wang, Chengqian Ma, WenHao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) reason about long videos over hundreds to thousands of tokens. Every decoding step re-reads the key-value (KV) cache of the entire video. Must the model keep looking while it reasons? In LookAway, the model first writes timestamped notes on what the question needs and then puts the video away. Once the notes close, the inference engine hides the video's KV cache for the rest of the response. Across both hybrid and pure-attention VLMs, dependence on the video is concentrated early in the response. On Qwen3.6-27B, writing notes preserves accuracy better than free reasoning at the same withdrawal step. Replacing them with another video's notes harms accuracy, whereas keeping the video visible alongside the model's own notes adds no significant benefit. Without additional training, LookAway nearly matches full-video thinking accuracy at 0.12-0.13 times its modeled decode cost. To shorten this initial look, we fine-tune the model on its own correct LookAway responses. Reinforcement learning then rewards correct answers while penalizing KV reads. The trained model puts the video away after a median of 66-68 tokens. Across three held-out benchmarks, it writes more tokens than an identically trained full-video model yet reads only 27-35% as many KV positions at matched accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Target-Consistent Action Guidance via Semi-Unbalanced Optimal Transport for Drifting World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24222)**  `arXiv:2611.24222`  `cs.CV`  
  _Md Abtahi Majeed Chowdhury, Md Rifat Ur Rahman, Md Sakib Hossain Shovon, Jaesik Choi, Minhyuk Sung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action-conditioned world models let robots imagine the outcomes of their actions for planning and offline policy evaluation, but these uses need many rollouts, which multi-step diffusion makes expensive. Drifting models move the iteration from inference to training and generate samples in one network pass. DriftWorld brought this paradigm to action-conditioned world models; to strengthen action following, it repels predictions from the current frame, a construction borrowed from classifier-free guidance. We show that this accentuation has a target bias: the push persists even when a prediction matches the recorded future, in kernel, balanced, and unbalanced Wasserstein drifting fields alike. We introduce Mass-Share Accentuation (MSA) to remove this bias. MSA treats the current frame as a counterfactual destination competing with the recorded future in a semi-relaxed optimal transport problem; subtracting the closed-form share of the correct future makes the guidance vanish at the target, and the remainder is shared by all predictions. We prove that the recorded future is the unique fixed point along the motion direction for collapsed predictions, and that the guidance fades with prediction error. On Push-T, Robomimic, and BridgeV2, where trained models under-react rather than overshoot, MSA improves action ranking, response slope, and moving-region error over latent-space baselines at no inference cost, though these benchmarks do not isolate bias removal as the cause.
  </details>

- **[WM^MLLM: Turning MLLMs into World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24193)**  `arXiv:2611.24193`  `cs.CV`  
  _Baiang Li, Enze Xie, Jincheng Yu, Jin Wang, Haozhe Liu, Yitong Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models keep the world in the context of a generator, so a revisit is synthesized again and earlier edits can drift or disappear. We present WM^MLLM, the first interactive world model built on multimodal large language models (MLLMs), for persistent, editable world generation from a single image. WM^MLLM keeps the world state outside every generator, as a panorama with a revision history, and lets the MLLMs write it: they turn each request into operations on this state, select where a local edit is written, and check every candidate before it becomes the next state. Image generators only propose views, and video only presents them. Users can walk to new places and return, edit objects through text or a box, place subjects, and change lighting, weather or season. By construction, a revisit at the same revision reproduces the earlier view exactly, a local edit leaves every panorama pixel outside its region unchanged, and each request starts from the result of the previous one. Against six world model baselines on MBench-A, WM^MLLM has the highest mean on all four environment-recovery metrics (Epipolar 91.5 versus 0.1 to 74.0) and completes the most camera round trips (85.3% versus at most 50.9%). It has the best value in all 15 CompBench columns against 15 baselines. On 112 local edits judged blind, selecting the region to write after generation raises completion from 34.8% to 83.0%. On 258 WBench and MBench-A items, all 46 revisits recover their earlier views, and all 97 local edits that pass the check leave panorama pixels outside their region unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Monitoring Unplanned Construction Using Satellite Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24190)**  `arXiv:2611.24190`  `cs.CV`  
  _Harsh Kashyap, Taranum Wasu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cities need land for homes, shops, roads, and public services. New construction changes that land. It can harm natural areas and water bodies, add to waste, and strain public services. NITI Aayog's 2021 report stated that 65% of 7,933 urban settlements in India had no master plan. That number tells us about planning coverage at the time. It does not tell us whether any single building was authorised. Artificial intelligence, which lets computers learn patterns from data, can help find physical changes by comparing satellite images taken on different dates. We test simple methods on two public datasets from outside India. First, we turn each image into a set of numbers using a pretrained neural network, a model already trained to recognise patterns in images. We feed those numbers to standard machine-learning classifiers that predict land categories and physical changes. The methods reach about 95 percent accuracy at spotting built-up land and about 83 percent accuracy at saying whether a small pair of images shows change, where each pair gets one label. We also train a small neural network on a standard change-detection benchmark. Separately, we run a preliminary descriptive check on an Agra sample of 48 patches, 7 of them positive. The results show that satellite images carry information about physical change. They do not show whether construction was planned or permitted.
  </details>

- **[ART: Bridging the Embodiment Gap in 3D Human-to-Robot Affordance Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24182)**  `arXiv:2611.24182`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zichen Zhang, Wei Zhai, Xuji Zhang, Yang Cao, Yu Kang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D affordance acts as a critical link between perception and action, providing priors for robotic manipulation. While existing approaches predominantly leverage human priors as direct references, they often overlook that affordance is not an object-intrinsic property but is inherently coupled with the agent's embodiment. Due to the structural and morphological disparities between human hands and robot end-effectors, directly applying human priors causes severe incompatibility for robotic execution. Motivated by this, we introduce a novel task setting, 3D Human-to-Robot Affordance Transfer, which addresses the affordance misalignment arising from distinct embodiment structures. To support this task, we construct the Human-Robot Paired Affordance Dataset (HPAD), the first large-scale 3D affordance benchmark in which every object is annotated with both human-prior and simulation-validated robot labels. HPAD covers 2,137 objects across 80 categories and 13 atomic action primitives, yielding over 27,000 object-action pairs. On top of HPAD, we propose the 3D Affordance tRansfer neTwork (ART). ART first filters geometric false positives in the human prior via a reliability gating stream, then reconstructs the prior into a robot-actionable distribution through hierarchical reconstruction and a graph-diffusion refinement that resolves the topological mismatch between human and robot affordances. Experiments on HPAD demonstrate the reliability of the proposed task and the superiority of ART. We further validate ART on a 6-DoF arm with a parallel-jaw gripper in a 2,400-execution benchmark across 12 objects and 4 action categories; ART attains the best success rate (86.5% over its 480 trials), confirming the practicality of the transfer paradigm. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ViStep-RL: Stepwise Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Visual Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24170)**  `arXiv:2611.24170`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyang Chen, Yao Qin, Xiao Li, Ningchao Wang, Yiguo Qiao, Wenbin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon visual agents must do more than inspect images: they must turn observations into evidence that remains usable as a task unfolds. Existing active-perception methods teach agents where and how to look, while a final-answer reward gives little structure for deciding which observations satisfy dependent subgoals or should be retained for later use. We present ViStep-RL (Visual Stepwise Reinforcement Learning), which models execution as a dependency-linked graph of evidence-bearing subgoals. A verifier tracks which subgoals are supported by valid evidence, and changes in graph potential provide a net-progress learning signal; an active memory protocol preserves evidence across turns and links retrieval to downstream use. On ViInteract, ViStep-RL reaches 60.0% strict completion with Qwen2.5-VL-7B, compared with 10.0% for the base model and 46.5% after supervised fine-tuning. Controlled studies show that incremental task-state rewards outperform cumulative rewards by 3.2 points, while active evidence writing improves strict completion by 7.75 points over automatic writing. Under a common interaction framework, the task-trained ViStep-RL policy achieves 59.5% answer accuracy, 24.0 points above the strongest of six general-purpose reference models. Held-out episodes confirm the gain at both Qwen scales, and gains extend to three external visual reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AVTR: Adaptive Multimodal Reasoning with Visual Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24161)**  `arXiv:2611.24161`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoqian Shen, Yunyang Xiong, Changsheng Zhao, Saksham Suri, Chong Zhou, Zechun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models increasingly rely on textual reasoning to solve advanced multimodal tasks. Despite its effectiveness, this paradigm faces two fundamental limitations. First, relying on textual reasoning alone can limit perceptual ability in vision-centric questions, where accurate answers depend on interpreting task-relevant visual evidence. Second, applying textual reasoning uniformly across inputs overlooks question-dependent variation in both reasoning modality and depth, often producing verbose and unnecessary traces when little logical inference is required. To this end, we propose Adaptive Visual-Textual Reasoning (AVTR), which introduces visual imagination as a complementary reasoning modality to textual thinking, enabling the model to revisit its visual observations and distill the salient evidence through generative visual reflection. Furthermore, we formulate reasoning-modality selection as a learned routing problem, allowing a single policy to adaptively harness the complementary strengths of visual reflection and textual reasoning in response to the perceptual and inferential demands of the input. Across 16 benchmarks, AVTR improves the average score by 4.5 points over Qwen3.5 baseline, while reducing inference latency by 33\%. It also scales effectively into a smaller size backbone, outperforming the evaluated 2B-scale baselines.
  </details>

- **[Game2Player: Scaling Gameplay Experience for Visual Agents through Environment-Player Co-Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24154)**  `arXiv:2611.24154`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xirui Jiang, Yuxi Chen, Rui Yang, Feng Luo, Pengcheng Wang, Bingxuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Games are a rich testbed for visual agents, demanding perception, long-horizon planning, and precise control. However, collecting human gameplay is costly, and learning from gameplay videos requires inferring missing actions. Existing datasets also cover a limited range of games, often without explicit tasks or verifiable outcomes. To address these gaps, we introduce Game2Player, a framework that co-develops game environments and players to collect diverse and verifiable gameplay experience at scale. In the Game2Player loop, a Builder agent adapts existing web games, exposes their internal state, and defines tasks with state-based progress measures and success checks. A Coding Player and a VLM Player then attempt the tasks and report problems to the Builder for repair, so that play serves as both environment validation and training data collection. With Game2Player, we build 568 verified web games with 3,436 tasks, collect 15.7K trajectories (307 hours of gameplay), and hold out 50 games as the benchmark PlayerBench. To evaluate the collected experience as training data, we train VLM agents with progress-weighted SFT, assigning greater weight to steps that advance task progress. Game2Player-9B improves progress from 14.3% to 31.2% on PlayerBench and from 30.0% to 53.1% on the external GameWorld benchmark. More broadly, our work suggests a path for visual agents to scale their gameplay experience by building, verifying, and learning from existing game worlds.
  </details>

- **[SEER: Semantic Evidence for Executable Region Propagation in Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24152)**  `arXiv:2611.24152`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyang Chen, Xiao Li, Yiguo Qiao, Wenbin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
