  Training-free open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) has made strong progress by adapting CLIP-like representations to dense prediction, but its remaining errors are often local, structured, and tied to the active label space. A successful correction must be visually plausible, legal under the target vocabulary, and executable in the segmentation score field. We propose SEER, a training-free test-time repair layer for Semantic Evidence and Executable Region propagation. SEER casts post-prediction OVSS repair as evidence-conditioned label propagation. The OVSS score space proposes compact candidate labels, a frozen VLM verifies closed-set regional evidence, and an execution gate certifies whether the evidence is legally and semantically actionable. The accepted label is then propagated by an OVSS-side policy: coherent high-confidence regions allow broad propagation, while object- or boundary-risk cases use conservative score-grounded propagation. Across eight standard OVSS benchmarks, SEER reaches 58.0 average mIoU without updating OVSS weights. Matched-input controls isolate the repair mechanism, while resource measurements report the cost of the complete frozen system.
  </details>

- **[EVF-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for E-commerce Visual Effects in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24116)**  `arXiv:2611.24116`  `cs.CV`  
  _Run Ling, Ke Cao, Runze He, Jie Gao, Ao Ma, Wei Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  E-commerce visual effects (EVF) are dynamic, product-centered visual presentations in e-commerce videos, often accompanied by promotional text. They play an important role in attracting viewer attention and conveying product information. However, general measures of visual quality and text relevance alone do not establish whether generated videos preserve product appearance, maintain readable text, and produce coherent, contextually appropriate effects. To address this issue, we introduce EVF-Bench, a dedicated benchmark spanning six evaluation dimensions: visual quality and text relevance as general dimensions, and motion, product consistency, text stability, and creative rationality as EVF-specific dimensions. To complement the benchmark and support task-aligned research and model adaptation, we further construct EVF-32K, a dataset of over 32000 curated image-text-video triples tailored to e-commerce scenarios. Using this suite, we systematically evaluate representative video generation models and their domain-adapted variants. The results reveal complementary strengths, with no evaluated configuration leading all six dimensions. Together, EVF-Bench and EVF-32K provide a task-oriented evaluation framework and a supporting data resource for e-commerce video generation.
  </details>

- **[RoughCut-Bench: A Benchmark for Automatic Video Rough-Cut with Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24098)**  `arXiv:2611.24098`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruisi Zhao, You Li, Sihan Chen, Aosong Cheng, Chixiang Ma, Sen Pei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video rough-cut is a core stage in media production workflows, organizing raw media materials into an initial video cut. Despite recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) for video-related tasks, automatic rough-cut remains underexplored, without a comprehensive benchmark for evaluation or mature workflows for producing executable cut plans. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce RoughCut-Bench, which covers two practical settings: (1) a visually driven setting over video cases, covering highlight detection and selection, source-video ordering, and adherence to user requirements when provided; and (2) a speech-driven setting over automatic speech recognition (ASR) transcripts, covering filler removal, expression deduplication, and low-value content trimming. Across these settings, the benchmark contains 101 visually driven cases, including 831 raw videos with 1,476 highlight annotations. It also includes 84 speech-driven cases, each consisting of an ASR transcript with annotated cleanup spans. The evaluation compares cut plans with these annotations in both settings and additionally uses a human-aligned agentic evaluator to assess rendered-cut quality in the visual setting. To apply LVLMs to automatic rough-cut, we further propose agentic workflows that guide them to produce structured cut plans from input materials. Experiments show that current LVLMs remain limited in producing expert-aligned cut plans. Our benchmark, code, and prompts are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Reasoning Reliability,Prior_Evidence Contrast,and Spectral Discrimination in Remote-Sensing Geological Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24068)**  `arXiv:2611.24068`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jun Wang, Fengpeng Li, Wei Han, Yuxuan Li, Tianjin Huang, Jining Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable lithology interpretation requires accurate geological recognition and reasoning grounded in visual evidence. Although Multimodal Large Language Models show promise in remote sensing, their reliability in geological interpretation remains unclear. We introduce HyGeoBench, a diagnostic benchmark of 10,000 questions based on hyperspectral imagery across three dimensions: reasoning reliability, prior_evidence contrast, and spectral discrimination. It covers ten task types, with both multiple-choice and open-ended response formats. Integrating hyperspectral satellite imagery with geological annotations and verified evidence, HyGeoBench examines reasoning errors, conflicts between geological priors and visual evidence, and cross-band inconsistencies. Evaluations reveal limitations in fine-grained recognition and evidence-grounded interpretation. To address these challenges, we propose HyGeoAgent, a tool-augmented framework integrating region observation, text knowledge retrieval, same-band image retrieval, and native PCA analysis through adaptive tool use. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements over the corresponding base models, improving geological image interpretation across diagnostic dimensions.
  </details>

- **[R$^{3}$Sieve: Suppressing Unconditional Information from Attention to Answers for High-Resolution VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24060)**  `arXiv:2611.24060`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yifan Cao, Yawen Zhang, Xiaocui Yang, Faxian Wan, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution visual question answering (VQA) requires models to identify question-relevant small objects in high-resolution images and select the correct answer based on what they recognize, demanding fine-grained perception. Previous methods focus on obtaining better visual evidence (image crops), but obtaining it alone is insufficient, using it effectively is equally crucial. Visual evidence should therefore be utilized as effectively as possible throughout inference. We argue that small-target evidence is obscured by unconditional information that remains observable in subsequent processing even after the condition on which it should depend is removed. Such information hinders the effective use of small-target evidence. We propose R$^{3}$Sieve (Residual-Region-Response Sieve), a training-free framework. We divide inference into three stages---localization, cropping, and answering---and introduce distinct counterfactual references to suppress interference at each stage. During localization, the Shared-Subspace Canceller (SSC) uses a generic image description to construct a counterfactual reference and obtain question-relevant attention maps. During cropping, the Unique-Contribution Spotter (UCS) constructs counterfactual references from visual and non-visual features to identify distinctive, complementary crop regions. During answering, the Content-Aligned Voter (CAV) constructs an image-free branch to calibrate answer responses. Experiments on three high-resolution VQA benchmarks show that R$^{3}$Sieve achieves the highest overall accuracy among compared methods across diverse settings, improving base models by up to 21.5 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[Semantic-aware Contrasting with Explicitly Disentangled Representations for AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24038)**  `arXiv:2611.24038`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaquan Yu, Jingyi Xie, Zeyu Zhang, Yunhang Qian, Xiaobin Hu, Jiawei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of AI-generated videos (AIGVs) has raised critical concerns about content authenticity. Existing benchmarks suffer from a fundamental limitation: real and fake videos are often collected or generated from different semantic distributions. As a result, detectors may rely on spurious correlations involving objects, scenes, or actions rather than intrinsic artifacts introduced by generative models. To this end, we introduce SAVGE, a large-scale semantically aligned AIGV detection dataset comprising 2,369,822 videos, including 424,115 real videos and 1,945,707 generated videos. SAVGE organizes samples into real-fake semantic groups by using captions paired with real videos as prompts for text-to-video generation, thereby providing both group-level correspondence and textual semantic anchors. Based on SAVGE, we further propose Explicit Disentangling and Semantic-aware Contrasting (EDSC), a model-agnostic training framework for learning artifact-focused representations with reduced semantic interference. EDSC first extracts and aligns visual semantic features with textual representations, and then explicitly suppresses semantic information in the artifact branch while contrasting real and fake samples within each semantic group. By integrating caption-guided semantic supervision with intra-group contrastive learning in a two-phase optimization strategy, EDSC encourages detectors to capture generation-related cues under matched semantic conditions. Extensive experiments on SAVGE and GenVideo demonstrate that EDSC improves most evaluated backbone-metric combinations, enhances robustness under challenging evaluation settings, and strengthens cross-dataset generalization. These results highlight the importance of semantic alignment in AIGV detection and validate explicit semantic-artifact disentanglement as an effective strategy for robust detector learning.
  </details>

- **[STAR++: A Physical-Aware Benchmark and Framework for Astronomical Super-Resolution across Multiple Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24023)**  `arXiv:2611.24023`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guohang Zhuang, Jiaze Li, Kuo-cheng Wu, Hao Du, Jinyang Huang, Xiang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Astronomical super-resolution (ASR) aims to reconstruct high-resolution star fields from low-resolution telescope observations across multiple filters for astrophysical analysis. However, developing a physics-aware ASR framework faces two challenges: 1) Data view: no large-scale benchmark supports field-level ASR, especially on unseen filters. 2) Method view: Existing ASR methods often fail to preserve stellar flux across spatial resolutions. Moreover, ASR requires reconstructing high-frequency components, including accurate star locations and profiles, making it more ill-posed than natural-image SR. To tackle these problems, we introduce STAR++, a large-scale benchmark comprising approximately 1.7 million flux-consistent image pairs across 36 filters, derived from Hubble Space Telescope (HST) observations. STAR++ uses a flux-preserving data generation pipeline to ensure photometric consistency across resolutions and provides the Flux Error (FE) metric to evaluate ASR models from a physical perspective. To address method-level challenges, we propose a Flux-Invariant Super-Resolution Plus (FISR++) model that generalizes to unseen filters while accurately reconstructing flux-consistent high-resolution images. Beyond the flux-preserving modules, FISR++ introduces two novel components. Specifically, a Filter-Aware Adapter (FAA) incorporates filter metadata to adapt image representations to varying spectral and optical conditions. In addition, a Spatial Ambiguity Disentangle Loss (SADL) models spatial ambiguity as pixel-shift uncertainty, mitigating the smoothing of learned conditional mean reconstructions caused by this ambiguity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FISR++ consistently outperforms existing ASR methods in reconstruction quality and flux accuracy, while generalizing to unseen filters, highlighting its effectiveness for photometrically faithful ASR.
  </details>

- **[ReSynth: Constructing Synthetic Supervision for Multi-Image Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23996)**  `arXiv:2611.23996`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yilin Wang, Xin Yu, Xinyi Guo, Xiangxi Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Linjie Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  People often understand the world by comparing different views of it. Vision-language models (VLMs), however, receive these views as separate images and still struggle to relate them. One problem is data. Training on these tasks requires groups of images with a known relationship: the same object from different viewpoints, or the same scene before and after a change. Such examples are hard to collect at scale from existing photo collections. We introduce ReSynth, which turns single-image perception and controllable image generation into cross-image relational supervision. Localized editing produces before-and-after pairs, viewpoint generation produces multiple views of the same instance, and verified scene contents support comparisons of counts and attributes. ReSynth combines Qwen3-VL-8B's captioning, grounding, and counting capabilities with Qwen-Image-2.1's generation and editing capabilities. It verifies single-image facts such as object counts and attributes, then combines these facts to construct multi-image QA. Using GRPO on approximately 440K ReSynth questions, with no real images during training, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B improve by 17.73 and 6.10 average points on BLINK, MUIRBench, and VLM2-Bench, while single-image performance is broadly preserved on Qwen2.5-VL-7B and improves across all four evaluated benchmarks on Qwen3-VL-8B. Notably, this supervision enables the 8B perception model itself to outperform Qwen3-VL-235B on BLINK and MUIRBench.
  </details>

- **[WaveView: The Novel View Is in the Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23994)**  `arXiv:2611.23994`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaiwen Wang, Jan-Hendrik Pauls, Yinzhe Shen, Frank Bieder, Christoph Stiller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative novel view synthesis (NVS) employs a diffusion model to render a static or dynamic scene from a target camera view. Explicit warp methods complete a reprojection of the source with video diffusion models, and implicit methods encode the camera parameters or 3D cues. Both add inference overhead, and the generated layout often drifts from the ground truth. We present WaveView, which injects the warped source into the phase of the initial noise of a pretrained generator. The Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform (DT-CWPT) allows local and anisotropic phase injection. The generator is thus constrained less in disoccluded regions and along the epipolar line. WaveView keeps the architecture and adds negligible overhead. On the DL3DV benchmark, it outperforms all baselines in every overlap bin. In the hardest bin, it gains 0.84 dB PSNR on the covisible region over MetaView, which shares its generator, while running 2.1 times faster. For dynamic NVS, fine-tuning AnyView with the same noise raises its zero-shot PSNR on AnyViewBench from 11.58 to 12.35 dB.
  </details>

- **[SimplePro: A Simple Baseline for Proactive Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23954)**  `arXiv:2611.23954`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yilin Wang, Xiangxi Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Linjie Li, Zhengyuan Yang, Alex Jinpeng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive video understanding requires vision-language models (VLMs) to decide not only what to answer as a video unfolds, but also when newly arrived evidence warrants a response and when to remain silent. Although modern VLMs excel at offline long-video understanding, they struggle in proactive streaming settings, leading prior work to introduce task-specific reinforcement learning or auxiliary triggering modules. We find that explicit reporting rules and structured streaming context substantially improve a frozen VLM's proactive response performance. We exploit this observation to build SIMPLEPRO, a training-free baseline for proactive video understanding. At each decision step, the model combines recent high-resolution frames, bounded historical observations, and prior answer records to determine whether new information warrants a response and, when needed, generate a concise answer. The system then filters consecutive duplicate answers while retaining the raw outputs as context for subsequent decisions. Without any parameter updates or auxiliary triggers, SIMPLEPRO with a 4B backbone consistently outperforms specialized proactive systems across OVO-Bench, StreamingBench-PO, and ProactiveVideoQA. SIMPLEPRO shows overall performance gains as backbone size increases from 2B to 32B. We hope this simple yet effective method will serve as a strong baseline for proactive video understanding, and we will release our code and inference pipeline to support future research.
  </details>

- **[VOL-Agent: Programming Your Film via Video Orchestration Language Agentic System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23950)**  `arXiv:2611.23950`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongji Yang, Yucheng Zhou, Lingting Zhu, Shengju Qian, Xinye Li, Huan Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent video foundation models generate high-quality and limited-length clips, but orchestrating them into minute-level, story-coherent films remains an open problem. Existing video agents highly rely on natural-language planning or predefined workflows, which make their production hard to program, customize, and extend. Therefore, we introduce VOL-Agent, a programmable video agent built on a Video Orchestration Language (VOL). With static compilation and verification, followed by runtime dynamic loading, VOL-Agent decouples structural reliability from execution flexibility. Through the skill-driven executor, VOL-Agent dynamically resolves tools and skills from an open-world repository and supports user-defined skills in natural language. Moreover, VOL-Agent is capable of crafting new director skills from learned knowledge and improves itself through feedback to accumulate filmmaking knowledge. To overcome the disconnect between different segments, we apply a structural memory mechanism to record and recall world and entity states, sustaining entity and scene consistency across different shots. Critic feedback supports local revision, while reusable filmmaking knowledge and experiences can be retained for subsequent tasks. Experiments on three benchmarks show that VOL-Agent generates minute-scale videos with strong overall quality and limited cost.
  </details>

- **[SIGNTOK: A Text-Aligned Continuous Pose Tokenizer for Sign Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23944)**  `arXiv:2611.23944`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nabeela Khan, Gaurav Rai, Bowen Wu, Sihan Tan, Benjamin Yen, Takeshi Ashizawa, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-conditioned sign language generation (SLG) depends not only on the generator but also on the space in which signing is generated. Existing approaches generate directly in pose space, in quantized code spaces, or in learned latent spaces trained for reconstruction. We find that these spaces are organized largely by signer identity rather than linguistic content: on CSL-Daily, a test clip's nearest neighbor in raw pose space is the same sentence signed by another person in only 2% of cases. To address this, we propose SIGNTOK, a continuous pose tokenizer whose latent space is shaped by three training-only heads: a kinematics head that predicts motion dynamics, a sentence-level head that aligns signing with the overall text, and a word-level head that aligns short motion windows with content words. Together, these objectives raise the same-sentence cross-signer nearest-neighbor rate to 82%. On three sign language benchmarks, the same generator trained in the SIGNTOK latent instead of pose space raises back-translation BLEU-4 in five of six benchmark-scorer settings at comparable joint accuracy, and word-level alignment gives higher back-translation scores than sentence-level alignment on every benchmark.
  </details>

- **[TRoPE: Topology-aware Relative Positional Encoding for 4D Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23933)**  `arXiv:2611.23933`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chengzhuo Gui, Zhenglin Zhou, Fan Ma, Zongxin Yang, Yi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video-to-4D mesh generation has made substantial progress in recovering dynamic 3D geometry and motion from monocular videos. However, commonly used feed-forward approaches remain prone to sticking artifacts, where geodesically distant vertices move together incorrectly when they become spatially close. We attribute this failure to cross-part motion leakage: spatially nearby yet structurally distant vertices may retrieve similar motion information when motion decoding fails to distinguish spatial proximity from mesh connectivity. To address this issue, we introduce TRoPE, a topology-aware relative positional encoding that makes mesh connectivity explicit in the interaction between vertex queries and mesh-supported motion sources. TRoPE derives spectral features from the graph Laplacian and constructs pairwise topological relations from distances between multi-scale diffusion features, using these relations to modulate vertex--anchor attention through rotary phases. We further introduce a vertex-wise tail-risk objective that emphasizes persistently high-error vertices to explicitly suppress sticking artifacts. To better characterize and quantify these failures, we introduce MeshSticking-4D, a dedicated benchmark for this failure mode. Experiments on MeshSticking-4D show that TRoPE suppresses sticking artifacts while improving overall motion accuracy. Compared with the baseline, it reduces the worst-vertex error by 39.4% and lowers the separation failure rate from 49.35% to 34.90%. Additional evaluations on Motion-80 and Motion324-Subset show lower tail errors on diverse objects, while transfer to Mesh4D demonstrates applicability across backbones.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Additive Composition: Interventional Modeling of State-Dependent Cross-Degradation Responses for Composite Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23889)**  `arXiv:2611.23889`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zongda Tang, Jie Feng, Wenda Zhao, Mingtao Feng, Yuping Liang, Ronghua Shang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Composite image restoration aims to recover images corrupted by multiple coexisting degradations. Existing methods predominantly decompose composite corruptions into individual factors, relying on these isolated priors to guide restoration. However, coexisting degradations are inherently interdependent: the manifestation of one degradation dynamically shifts with the states of others, inducing non-additive interactions that factor-wise paradigms fail to capture. To bridge this gap, we formulate state-dependent cross-degradation responses and propose PROBE, an interventional framework that explicitly models how coexisting degradations influence one another. Specifically, we first construct unified degradation axes by aligning visual and textual degraded-clean offsets. Along these axes, a calibrated coordinate head estimates current degradation states, while bidirectional feature perturbations are injected to probe cross-coordinate responses under the current states. Contrasting these responses with corresponding intensity-matched single-degradation references quantifies intrinsic non-additive deviations, allowing the resulting response descriptors and state coordinates to jointly guide restoration. For comprehensive evaluation, we introduce SCD-15, a new benchmark covering all 15 nonempty combinations of four degradation factors at three severity levels. Experiments demonstrate that PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance on the CDD-11, AWMM-100K, and SCD-15 composite restoration datasets using a compact model with modest GPU memory requirements.
  </details>

- **[SWIFT: Slow World, Instant Freshness, and Timely Action for Asynchronous Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23813)**  `arXiv:2611.23813`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiazhe Wang, Tianyi Yang, Ruihang Li, Fan Lu, Xianming Zeng, Jianyun Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving scenes keep changing during model computation, making both observation freshness and action-delivery time central to world-action planning. To retain rich generative world priors in autonomous driving without stalling real-time control, we present SWIFT (Slow World, Instant Freshness, and Timely action), a deployment-oriented asynchronous world-action system that moves world updates off the observation-to-action critical path. On a background GPU, a slow Video DiT streams surround-view history into read-only predictive world context, trained with a predictive span that covers both the update interval and background world computation. On a foreground GPU, a lightweight freshness carrier encodes current surround-view images into a gated key/value bank at every step, letting a fast Action DiT combine live visual feedback with the latest completed world context without waiting. On open-loop NAVSIM, SWIFT reaches 94.2 PDMS at 198.4 ms per decision with a trajectory scorer; on closed-loop Bench2Drive, it achieves an 80.30 Driving Score at 109.9 ms foreground latency. SWIFT further transfers to the Waymo E2E hidden test (8.0050 Rater Feedback Score) and nuScenes (0.54 m mean endpoint error, 0.03% mean collision rate). Across all four benchmarks, SWIFT achieves the best results among compared world-action methods, establishing a favorable accuracy-latency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[PReMind: When Personal Memory Meets Visual Hazards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23808)**  `arXiv:2611.23808`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yeongtak Oh, Donghyeok Lee, Joonhee Oh, Jaewon Jeong, Sungroh Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized vision-language assistants use visual observations and personal memories to provide advice tailored to the user. When the observed scene contains a physical hazard, personal context, such as allergies, medical conditions, or mobility constraints, can change what help is appropriate. Responding appropriately therefore requires recognizing the visible hazard and applying the relevant person's information. We introduce PReMind-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that evaluates this combination across diverse hazards and personal-memory conditions. It separately measures memory access, grounding, abstention, and hazard response, and assesses joint success on interdependency cases. Existing models often satisfy one requirement while failing the other, and training only for safety or personalization leaves a gap in joint success. We therefore propose PReMind, a two-phase reinforcement learning framework that combines on-demand and forced-call interactions (memory always retrieved) to jointly train grounded memory use and hazard response. At 4B, PReMind raises joint success from 12.9% to 28.6% over its base on the interdependency set and improves hazard adequacy and correct abstention on the main test. Joint-success gains persist under forced-call evaluation and under a second LLM judge across three Phase II seeds; averaged over these seeds, joint success also improves on unseen hazard groups. These findings highlight the need to evaluate and train personal assistants to connect visual evidence with the relevant person's context.
  </details>

- **[Keep the Reasoning, Read the Image: On-Demand Visual KV for Multi-Turn Reasoning VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23769)**  `arXiv:2611.23769`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziyi Wang, Xiyue Zhang, Bing Hu, Guangmin Song, Chenyi Zhang, Li Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning vision-language models (VLMs) think for thousands of tokens before they answer, and retention policies keep the KV cache affordable by pruning it as the reasoning grows. We find that this pruning sacrifices the image in multi-turn conversations: the image and the reasoning share one budget, so the growing reasoning crowds the visual entries out. A chart question that loses 8 points to the full cache when asked first loses 24 points when asked after a round of reasoning. Protecting a small visual reserve from eviction recovers most of this loss, while adding the same slots to the shared budget recovers considerably less. The image therefore lacks protection, not capacity. Based on this finding, we propose ShelfKV, a training-free method that works on top of existing retention policies. It keeps every visual entry in CPU memory and, at each decoding step, swaps the visual entries that the current query scores highest into the attention slots of the text entries it scores lowest. The image is never evicted, yet it occupies attention slots only while it is read. We evaluate ShelfKV on four benchmarks, three of them conversational, with two retention policies and two reasoning VLMs. In conversations, it improves the strongest existing policy, DBTrimKV, by up to 20.3 points at matched GPU memory, and still leads by 8.7 points when DBTrimKV uses twice the memory. It also consistently outperforms a fixed visual reserve of matched GPU memory in these settings, without hurting accuracy on standalone questions. These results show that the image should be protected from the reasoning but read only on demand.
  </details>

- **[CoScan: Multi-Scale Content-Adaptive Space-Filling Scans for Causal State-Space Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23717)**  `arXiv:2611.23717`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wei Dong, Terry Ji, Yan Min, Shahram Shirani, Jun Chen, Han Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selective state-space models (SSMs) have recently shown strong potential for efficient image restoration. A central difficulty, however, is that a 2D feature map typically needs to be linearized into a 1D token sequence for causal state-space updates, making restoration performance highly sensitive to the imposed scan order. Existing deterministic scans follow space-filling principles but remain fixed and content-agnostic, and can yield fragmented or semantically inconsistent contexts under degradations. We propose \textbf{CoScan}, a \textbf{co}ntent-adaptive \textbf{scan}-order design for causal vision SSMs. CoScan learns image-dependent space-filling traversals via differentiable proxy supervision driven by \textbf{scan-quality objectives} that \textit{encourage short-range coherence and causal predictability, while preserving spatial adjacency}. To support hierarchical restoration backbones, we introduce an \textbf{efficient multi-scale scan construction by coarsening a fine-level minimum spanning tree}, producing coherent scans across feature resolutions. In addition, a \textbf{frequency-guided start selection strategy} is designed to \textit{place informative structures early in the sequence prefix to strengthen causal context formation}. Plugged into existing Mamba-based backbones, CoScan yields consistent improvements across several restoration benchmarks with minimal overhead and shows promising results on high-level vision tasks. Code will be released atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[A Hardware-Aware Comprehensive Study of Edge Vision Deployment with A Unified Hardware-Model-Runtime Mapping Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23705)**  `arXiv:2611.23705`  `cs.CV`  
  _Le Duc An, Le Duc Thanh, Vo Hoai Viet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid growth of deep learning has driven the deployment of computer vision directly on resource-constrained edge devices. However, the extreme heterogeneity of edge hardware architectures poses major challenges in selecting and designing suitable vision models. This survey bridges the gap between edge hardware constraints, model architectures, and deployment software toolchains. We establish a comprehensive taxonomy classifying edge hardware into three distinct tiers: mid-range devices (GPUs and dedicated NPUs or TPUs), dedicated embedded MPU or SoC processors, and microcontrollers (MCUs). We aggregate and analyze multi-source benchmark results for image classification and object detection across the computing spectrum, spanning from tens of watts down to milliwatt-scale bare-metal MCUs. Based on these empirical findings, we propose a two-tiered hardware-to-model mapping framework that provides concrete model selection matrices across performance priorities (Speed, Balanced, Accuracy) and actionable architectural design principles (peak SRAM budgeting, resolution-first scaling, and algorithm-compiler co-design). Finally, we highlight critical deployment challenges including toolchain fragmentation, operator incompatibility, and Vision Transformer feasibility, and outline promising future research directions in on-device adaptation and sustainable Edge AI.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Degrade: A Recoverability-Aware Curriculum for Reliable Smart-Glasses Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23674)**  `arXiv:2611.23674`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yaopeng Wang, Qingliang Wang, Yilun Jin, Xiujin Liu, Han Yue, Chen Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Degradations in smart-glasses images obscure small text and fine-grained structures needed by vision-language models (VLMs) for visual assistance. We introduce an high-resolution (HR)-referenced semantic evaluation protocol that compares VLM outputs on restored images with those on corresponding HR references. Our evaluation reveals two failure modes: generative SR can synthesize misleading details, whereas restoration-oriented SR can leave critical evidence blurred or ambiguous. These findings motivate improving faithful detail recovery in restoration-oriented SR, where a key training challenge is to provide sufficiently difficult inputs while retaining recoverable visual information. We propose ReDiC (Recoverability-Difficulty Curriculum), which combines a model-independent recoverability proxy with model-relative restoration difficulty to adapt training degradation severity. ReDiC increases severity when inputs are recoverable and restoration is easy, maintains challenging but recoverable conditions, and reduces severity when estimated recoverability is low. It modifies only training degradation generation, leaving the SR architecture and reconstruction objective unchanged. Across NAFNet, SwinIR, and MambaIR, ReDiC improves average PSNR by approximately 2.5 dB and HR-referenced semantic scores by about 3 points over the corresponding baselines on synthetic benchmarks, with further gains confirmed by LLM-based and human evaluations on 200 real smart-glasses captures.
  </details>

- **[ORGANIC3D: Grounding 3D CT Vision Language Models in Organ Level Image Text Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23529)**  `arXiv:2611.23529`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zelong Liu, Hamza Ahmed, Wynn Wingyi Lee, Mickael Torjman, Alexander Dimitrakakis, Jiahua Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in 3D computed tomography (CT) classification, but high accuracy does not reflect whether their predictions rely on the correct anatomical evidence. We introduce ORGANIC3D, a framework for evaluating and improving organ level image text alignment through controlled anatomical interventions. Our benchmark assesses 12 vision encoder checkpoints and 7 generative VLMs using matched patient comparisons, organ erasure, lesion replacement, and laterality perturbations, revealing substantial alignment failures even among top performing models. To learn organ level image text correspondence, ORGANIC3D aligns each finding sentence with the anatomy aware image features and trains on edited scans to anchor this alignment in the organ's image evidence. We further extend this framework to generative VLMs, promoting evidence dependent question answering and report generation. Trained exclusively on public data, ORGANIC3D achieves SOTA classification AUROC on CTRATE, Merlin, and the heldout RADChestCT dataset and reaches 77% laterality accuracy, compared with at most 66% for prior models. Its generative VLM matches or exceeds all evaluated publicly released VLMs on visual question answering, achieves the highest report accuracy, and retracts 96 to 99% of target organ findings after organ removal, versus a maximum of 69% for other models. Together, these results establish ORGANIC3D as a framework for testing whether 3D CT vision language models ground their predictions in anatomical evidence and for improving organ level alignment alongside predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Trust the Ray, Not the Depth: Ray-Lateral Agreement for Multi-View 3D Human Pose Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23513)**  `arXiv:2611.23513`  `cs.CV`  
  _Farnoosh Koleini, Ahmed Helmy, Pu Wang, Xiwu Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Put a monocular mesh estimator behind every camera of a calibrated rig, and the reconstructed bodies land a meter or more apart. The cause is geometric: each estimate sits at the depth implied by a focal length the network assumed, not the one the camera has. Its error therefore points almost entirely along its own viewing ray, and the calibration tells us that direction exactly. To address this, we introduce the Ray-Lateral Agreement (RLA), which follows one rule: trust the ray, not the depth. RLA first slides every estimate along its own rays, which removes the depth bias and leaves the shape untouched. It then fuses the views one point at a time, under a Gaussian error whose precision follows each viewing ray. The maximum-likelihood solution is closed-form, interpolates between averaging and midpoint triangulation, and stays well conditioned where triangulation is singular. Robustness is the harder half, because correct views also disagree along their rays. Any weight built on the full residual therefore punishes good views together with bad ones. Ray-lateral agreement instead scores every view, at every point, by its residual across that view's own ray alone. We evaluated RLA on multi-camera public benchmark datasets. Across two, three, and four cameras and three per-view pose estimators, RLA consistently achieves lower worst-case error than all triangulation baselines.
  </details>

- **[Autoregressive View Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23417)**  `arXiv:2611.23417`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jensen, Zhou, Samuel Rota Bulo, Denis Rozumny, Duncan Zauss, Peter Hedman, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Autoregressive View Model (AVM), which generates a scene as a stream of views without constructing an explicit 3D representation. Through a unified in-context interface, AVM conditions on images or videos to generate short clips along a requested camera path. Visual context grounds reconstruction and interpolation, while a learned prior enables extrapolation into unseen regions. AVM retrieves relevant views from inputs and generated history to form a bounded active context. We fine-tune a pretrained bidirectional video diffusion transformer with flow matching in an N-in-1-out formulation. We then tune a causal model on its own rollouts to improve photorealism, reduce denoising steps, and limit long-horizon drift. We scale training to approximately 8M scenes and video clips. We evaluate generation capacity and scene persistence across benchmarks for camera control, text control, visual quality, dynamics, sparse and 4D reconstruction, and long-horizon consistency. The results demonstrate that AVM adapts to diverse input and output settings through one in-context interface.
  </details>

- **[Any2Building: Unifying Point Clouds and Images for Building Reconstruction via Shared Spatial Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23187)**  `arXiv:2611.23187`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongye Hou, Zhuoxiao Li, Liu Xiangyu, Tengxi Wang, Jing Ou, Dongli Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively. This motivates a unified framework that aligns geometric and visual cues in 3D and reconstructs buildings from either or both modalities. Building on this connection, we present Any2Building, a unified model for Level of Detail 2 (LoD2) building reconstruction from partial point clouds, overhead images, or both, with optional coarse geometric priors. At its core is an occupancy-grounded shared spatial queries representation that organizes available observations within a common 3D grid. Supervised by complete building surface voxels, these queries connect observational evidence to target geometry, enabling a shared generator to learn structural priors across tasks and providing spatial guidance for recovering missing structure. A trained voxel conditional surface-generation and low-poly remeshing pipeline then converts the predicted structure into compact LoD2 building models. To support this framework, we introduce BuildingWorld-Image, comprising approximately one million paired samples of partial point clouds, overhead images, and LoD2 building models across 18 cities. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Any2Building outperforms existing methods in geometric reconstruction accuracy across unimodal and multimodal point-cloud completion, single-image building generation, and prior-assisted generation, while maintaining detailed and robust recovery when point cloud input modality is degraded.
  </details>

- **[M3OPD-Reasoner: An Open Recipe for Post-Training Multimodal Reasoning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23034)**  `arXiv:2611.23034`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nguyen Quang Trung, Hoang Phan, Shuo Sun, Longyin Zhang, AiTi Aw_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD) enables a single student to absorb the skills of several domain teachers by learning from its own generated responses. We present Multimodal Model Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation Reasoner (M3OPD-Reasoner), a fully open recipe for post-training vision-language models (VLMs) over three reasoning domains: image STEM, image perception, and video. We curate two datasets, M3OPD-SFT-1.35M for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and M3OPD-RL-193K for reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards. On these data, we compare post-training recipes that start from Qwen3.5-9B: training one model on the mixed data of all domains (MixSFT and MixRL), and training one teacher per domain with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), directly or after an SFT warm-up, and distilling the teachers into one student with MOPD. We evaluate on 21 image STEM, image perception, and video reasoning benchmarks after we decontaminate the training data against them. The comparison establishes MOPD of teachers trained with GSPO directly from Qwen3.5-9B as the best recipe: the student closely matches each teacher on its own domain and is on average above the mixed-training baselines on the same prompts. Since MOPD already merges the domain capabilities that MixRL would otherwise have to learn, its student is a stronger starting point for RL, and we ask whether the same data can carry the model further from there: we add MixRL on top of the MOPD student, on the same prompts and with the same settings, which, to our knowledge, no prior VLM recipe has studied. The same prompts still improve the MOPD student, with no collapse, and give our main model, M3OPD-Reasoner-9B. MOPD and MixRL can therefore work as consecutive stages that get more out of the same data. We release all data, the training code, and the checkpoints of every stage.
  </details>

- **[TANGO: Real-World Unregistered RGB-Guided Hyperspectral Super-Resolution with Two-Camera Acquisitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22699)**  `arXiv:2611.22699`  `cs.CV`  
  _Matteo Kolyszko, Marco Buzzelli, Simone Bianco, Raimondo Schettini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unregistered hyperspectral image super-resolution aims to recover a high-resolution HSI from a low-resolution hyperspectral observation and an unregistered high-resolution RGB reference. Although recent methods have begun to address the geometric misalignment between the two modalities, their evaluation is still constrained by the lack of a large-scale public benchmark based on real two-camera RGB-HSI acquisitions with a high-resolution HSI reference. To address this gap, we introduce DUET, the first large-scale public dataset for unregistered RGB-guided hyperspectral super-resolution, comprising 403 indoor and outdoor scenes captured with two separate RGB and hyperspectral cameras. We further propose TANGO (Two-camera AligNment with Gated Occlusions), which first compensates for the dominant cross-camera displacement through end-to-end global alignment and then refines the remaining inconsistencies with a coarse-to-fine local feature alignment. A learned gating mechanism further attenuates the RGB guidance where the two views cannot be consistently matched. Experimental results show that TANGO achieves state-of-the-art performance on both DUET and REAL across all evaluated scale factors, outperforming the strongest competitor on DUET by up to 2.29 dB in PSNR while requiring 2.5$\times$ fewer FLOPs. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Family Labels: A Taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Prior for Avian 3D Shape Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21102)**  `arXiv:2611.21102`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunchan Jeon, SooGon Kim, Seungwook Kim, CheolWon Lee, Jongmin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering 3D pose and shape of birds from single images is bottlenecked by the long tail of avian diversity: out of ~10,000 species, only a few dozen have 3D-labeled training data, and in-the-wild test sets routinely contain species never seen at training. Generalizing across clades is therefore a central problem. The current state of the art, AniMer+, mitigates it with a family-level contrastive loss that conveys only a binary same-family indicator per pair and discards the continuous structure of the species tree. We propose a taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck prior on the shape code of the AVES parametric bird mesh model: a multivariate Gaussian whose covariance decays exponentially with Linnaean rank distance, with a learnable selection strength and noise scale. The prior generalizes family contrastive into a continuous, generative form, exposing the graded cross- and within-family similarity that a binary indicator cannot, and brings continuous comparative phylogenetics in as a biology-grounded inductive bias for the long tail. Our method improves over AniMer+ on both metrics of the unseen-species CowBird benchmark and on CtrlAVES3D, while remaining comparable on seen-species CUB. A permutation ablation isolates taxonomic structure from generic Gaussian smoothing, and a rank-granularity ablation identifies cross-order distinctions (the structure beyond a family-vs-not indicator) as the dominant source of the unseen-species gain.
  </details>

- **[Pros4D: Modeling Evolving 4D Worlds in Latent Space for Dynamic Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19803)**  `arXiv:2611.19803`  `cs.CV`  
  _Rongxin Gao, Dongxuan Liu, Chu Li, Chengyang Ji, Jialing Huang, Runyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic spatial reasoning from monocular video enables vision-language models (VLMs) to understand how 3D environments evolve over time, supporting physical world understanding and embodied intelligence. Existing efforts primarily address observed-dynamics understanding, whereas agents in dynamic environments also require future-dynamics prediction beyond the observation boundary. Moreover, external geometric processing and textual descriptions can increase inference complexity or lose information when representing continuous dynamics. These limitations motivate a shared internal state that preserves observed dynamics and remains predictive beyond the input video. Accordingly, we present Pros4D, a framework for modeling evolving 4D worlds in latent space that unifies retrospective understanding of observed-dynamics with prospective reasoning for future-dynamics prediction. Specifically, Pros4D-Engine derives geometry-grounded supervision and constructs paired examples for both tasks, while Pros4D-Bench evaluates them across eight spatial task categories spanning dynamic cameras, objects, and scenes. At the model level, Pros4D compresses observed dynamics into latent observed states and autoregressively rolls them forward into latent future states. 4D Latent Alignment (4DLA) grounds these states through complementary visual-semantic and geometric supervision, while joint language supervision connects them to task reasoning. Experiments across benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong baselines in both capabilities.
  </details>

- **[AcrossPlace: Do Vision--Language Models Recognize Places Beyond Their Source Views? A Comprehensive Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15314)**  `arXiv:2611.15314`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenyu Li, Tianyi Shang, Shuhan Dong, Ruotong Xu, Qingyu Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-guided place recognition requires recovering geographic identity across visual observations beyond the image from which a description originates. However, aggregate retrieval scores often entangle observation transfer, gallery competition, and linguistic evidence, obscuring the underlying recognition capability. We introduce AcrossPlace, a provenance-aware benchmark establishing a systematic evaluation standard for bidirectional language--vision place recognition. Built upon GSV-Cities, AcrossPlace comprises 56,726 places across 23 cities, 156,800 automatically generated, rule-filtered descriptions, and 113,404 target images. Each description retains its source-image provenance, while retrieval operates on disjoint target observations. Text-to-image (T2I) and image-to-text (I2T) retrieval share a unified place-identity relevance criterion supporting multiple valid matches. Two geographic protocols evaluate unseen places within represented cities (P0) and generalization to held-out cities (P1). Paired observation comparisons, gallery-controlled evaluations, fixed-query scaling, and blinded annotation auditing systematically characterize retrieval performance. Across three training seeds, place-supervised SigLIP2 T2I Recall@1 decreases from 19.69% on source images to 12.31% on independent targets under P0. For fixed P1 queries, expanding the gallery from 500 to 8,425 places reduces Recall@1 from 14.67% to 2.87%. A 240-description audit further reveals discrepancies between automated acceptance and factual evidence. These findings establish AcrossPlace as a reproducible evaluation standard for assessing transferable place identity across visual observations and geographic environments.
  </details>

- **[Integrating Learned Priors through Prior-Space Reparameterization for Physically Based Inverse Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07573)**  `arXiv:2611.07573`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hun Hwangbo, Minjun Park, Yongsung Kim, Wooseok Song, Jungmin Ko, Junghyun Hwang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physically based inverse rendering is an ambiguous problem, allowing independently optimized material variables to absorb appearance variations caused by illumination. Learned material predictions provide useful cues about which surface points have similar materials. Existing approaches often incorporate these cues through auxiliary regularization losses. Instead, we propose a novel prior-guided reparameterization of the material optimization space. We fuse multi-view material predictions onto a mesh and construct a permutohedral lattice in the resulting prior feature space. Surface points with similar predictions share lattice variables, restricting the material field to a prior-defined subspace that is optimized jointly with geometry and illumination using a purely photometric objective. Experiments on three synthetic benchmarks demonstrate improved material decomposition and relighting, with results on par with the strongest baseline on Stanford-ORB. As a complete system, our method improves relighting PSNR by 1.4-1.5\,dB over the strongest evaluated mesh-based baseline on Synthetic4Relight and TensoIR-Synthetic. Analysis of the optimization shows that shared variables pool per-vertex photometric gradients into updates that are more consistent across sampled views. In a controlled comparison within the same pipeline, the restricted subspace, despite its reduced degrees of freedom, yields more accurate material decomposition than both unrestricted per-vertex optimization and a regularization loss built from the same prior.
  </details>

- **[VidDub: Replacement-Aware Supervision for Two-Step Video Dubbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03404)**  `arXiv:2611.03404`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yexin Liu, Yunlong Lin, Zehan Wang, Guangxin He, Xinli Xu, Jiajun Zha, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video dubbing aims to synthesize lip movements that match target speech while preserving the speaker identity, head pose, and background of a source video. Existing editors are predominantly trained via self-reconstruction under original paired audio, even though the source mouth becomes a conflicting target when driving speech is replaced. We introduce \emph{replacement-aware cross-audio supervision}: paired-audio samples use full-frame reconstruction, whereas unpaired replacement-audio samples mask out source-mouth reconstruction losses and supervise the mouth solely through audio--visual synchronization while preserving unmasked facial regions and background. Because offline dubbing provides the complete source video and target speech beforehand, we present \method, a full-context diffusion editor that uses matched trajectory regression to transfer CFG-guided teacher trajectories to a two-step student and distribution matching to recover visual quality after compression. We will release the curated training dataset, VidDub-Bench, fixed paired- and cross-audio evaluation manifests, preprocessing outputs, and unified evaluation code. Across three training seeds, masking out conflicting source-mouth reconstruction improves cross-audio Sync-C by 0.833. At two NFE, the final student provides the strongest cross-audio synchronization and visual-speech recognition among the evaluated methods, with competitive visual fidelity and source preservation.
  </details>

- **[TurboWAM: Learning What Matters for Fast World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33941)**  `arXiv:2610.33941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guile Wu, Shuangzhi Li, Xingxin Chen, Chengjie Huang, Sam Hosseini, Tongtong Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models have demonstrated promising performance in robot policy learning, yet their high inference latency remains a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. Recent approaches have sought to improve inference efficiency by eliminating test-time future-frame imagination or accelerating action prediction. However, aggregating representations from all video world model layers can introduce redundant computation, while standard backpropagation may distribute action-supervision gradients unevenly across layers, limiting the effectiveness of world representation learning. In this work, we propose TurboWAM, a fast world action model that learns to efficiently aggregate and optimize world representations for action prediction. Specifically, TurboWAM introduces an efficient world representation aggregation mechanism that restricts aggregation to a bounded range of relevant world model layers identified through layer relevance analysis. Within this bounded range, TurboWAM estimates aggregation weights for different world model layers based on the current action denoising state and combines them with learnable layer-wise weights to fuse world representations. Moreover, TurboWAM employs a world model gradient modulation mechanism that decomposes action-supervision gradients into a layer-shared component and layer-specific residual components, enabling more balanced gradient propagation across world model layers. This mechanism operates exclusively during training and introduces no additional inference computation. Together, these designs enable more efficient world representation learning and low-latency action prediction. Extensive experiments on multiple robot manipulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks show that TurboWAM achieves low inference latency while maintaining competitive task success rates compared with state-of-the-art methods.
  </details>

- **[Tempo-Zero: Recursive Self-Play in Executable Worlds for Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32433)**  `arXiv:2610.32433`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingmin Zhu, Yanyan Zou, Xiuyu Li, James Bailey, Dinh Phung, Tao Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video reasoning requires understanding how events unfold over time, yet videos with accurate temporal annotations are expensive to collect. Self-play offers an alternative by letting a model generate and solve its own tasks without manual annotation. However, existing video self-play methods train on fixed sets of recorded videos, so they cannot alter the underlying event dynamics and rely on noisy pseudo-labels derived from the model's own outputs, such as majority voting, that risk reinforcing shared model errors. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Tempo-Zero, a self-improvement framework that closes a loop of executable environments, execution-verified self-play, and environment evolution, where executing a program renders a video with the ground-truth option and interval of every question. A single policy plays both Questioner and Solver, proposing questions and answering them with timestamped evidence lines, and is trained on execution-grounded rewards instead of model consensus. The Solver's performance steers a curriculum over tasks and difficulty levels, and the policy selects rule changes that enrich the event dynamics of later rounds. Despite being trained solely on simple programs, without supervised fine-tuning or external video data which prior methods typically require, Tempo-Zero outperforms the base models and recent self-play baselines on average across twelve real-world video benchmarks at different model scales, with particularly strong gains on temporal grounding tasks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14094)**  `arXiv:2610.14094`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tongxi Wu, Yuyang Cheng, Jiacheng Xian, Hu Huang, Zhihao Wu, Menglin Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Most prior attacks rely on specially constructed visual inputs to carry or support harmful intent. In this paper, we uncover a different vulnerability: task-relevant scene semantics alone can shift an MLLM from refusal to compliance, even when the visual input introduces no additional attack instructions and the original task intent remains unchanged. We formalize this failure as a scene-grounded visual safety vulnerability and introduce SceneSafetyBench to investigate it across eight risk categories and nine white- and black-box MLLMs. Our analysis reveals that this failure is associated with how task-relevant visual evidence influences downstream safety representations, suggesting that visual grounding alone cannot fully explain the observed safety shift. Building on this finding, we propose Scene-Grounded Attacks (SGA), which preserve the original task while constructing validated scene-grounded queries and combining their independent responses. Across nine target MLLMs, SGA achieves an average attack success rate (ASR) of 86.17%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 8.55 percentage points and achieving the highest ASR on eight models. Our results identify scene semantics as an overlooked attack surface in MLLMs and highlight a growing tension between stronger visual grounding and robust multimodal safety alignment.
  </details>

- **[No Corners Cut: State-Grounded Transitions for Mid-Stream Prompt Switches in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38691)**  `arXiv:2609.38691`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zejing Rao, Ketong Ren, Xiaoqiang Liu, Yiping Meng, Guoxin Zhang, Fan Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video generators allow users to dynamically modulate video synthesis via mid-stream prompt switching. Existing streaming methods can respond to the updated instruction while still cutting corners, prematurely realizing goals or taking heuristic shortcuts that bypass necessary intermediate state changes needed for a plausible transition. In this study, we present SEGUE, a novel framework that makes this process explicit and trains the generator to execute these transitions faithfully. At each switch, a training-free planner parses the latest frame and prompts, writes a few segue prompts with roles and durations, and then hands control back to the user's prompt. Furthermore, to address the inherent difficulty of training causal models on short-lived temporal schedules without corrupting preparatory supervision, we introduce SpanDMD, which evaluates each active prompt using the full rollout as temporal context while retaining its DMD residual only within the prompt's assigned span. On OpenTrans-360, a benchmark of 1,800 switches that scores how the old state exits and the new one begins, SEGUE ranks first on all eight transition metrics and raises the overall score over the strongest baseline from 0.866 to 0.887. It leads on four of six StreamAV-Bench instruction-response metrics and achieves the highest CLIP score in all five post-switch segments of VBench-Long evaluation, while the planner transfers to frozen autoregressive generators without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Small Object Detection in Drone Aerial Imagery with LAF-YOLOv10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14560)**  `arXiv:2609.14560`  `cs.CV`  
  _Quratulain Nayeem, Fahmina Taranum, Mohammed Mudassir Uddin, Sara Rizwan, Samia Areej_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose object detectors lose accuracy on UAV footage, where targets span only a handful of pixels and onboard compute is limited. Prior work composes independently-validated architectural techniques into one detector, assuming gains reported in isolation transfer once combined. We stress-test that assumption directly. LAF-YOLOv10 integrates four techniques into YOLOv10n: a Partial Convolution C2f (PC-C2f) backbone block, an Attention-Guided Feature Pyramid Network (AG-FPN), a P2 detection head replacing the large-object P5 head, and Wise-IoU v3 regression, asking whether their combined effect matches what each contributes alone. We train LAF-YOLOv10 three times (seeds 42, 123, 256) on VisDrone-DET2019, benchmark against unmodified YOLOv10n, and use TIDE error decomposition, per-category breakdown, per-component ablation, attention/loss comparisons, zero-shot transfer to UAVDT, and held-out/test-dev evaluation to localize where the combination succeeds or fails. Composability does not hold here. LAF-YOLOv10 reaches 24.0+/-0.4% mAP@0.5 at 2.14M parameters, 7.8 points below YOLOv10n (31.8%), a deficit that transfers to UAVDT (-10.0 points) and is confirmed by held-out and test-dev evaluation (23.5%, 22.5%). Background false positives, localization error, and duplicate detections move in the direction AG-FPN and Wise-IoU were designed to push. Ablation traces the deficit to a specific source: the P2/-P5 head swap costs 2.5 points independently plus a 2.5-point interaction penalty when layered onto a backbone weakened by PC-C2f, whose own 2.0-point loss is consistent with a partial pretrained-weight transplant (73/150 backbone tensors transfer). The failure is attributable to a specific interaction, not the four components individually. Composability must be verified directly, not assumed. Code/checkpoints:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[RadMatch: Auditable Radiology Report Evaluation via Finding-Level Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01470)**  `arXiv:2609.01470`  `cs.CV`  
  _Charles Corbière, Léo Machado, Baptiste Callard, Aubin Charley, Korentin Le Floch, Martin J. Willemink, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI systems are increasingly used to draft radiology reports, reliably evaluating their clinical quality remains a critical challenge. We introduce RadMatch, a multi-stage LLM-based framework that turns report evaluation into a structured, auditable finding-level record. It extracts findings from a reference and a candidate report, matches them by clinical equivalence, grades each match over ten clinical attributes, and gives every match and unmatched finding a verdict and a clinical-significance tier. To validate metrics on CT, we build and release MetricEval-BodyCT, the first public metric-evaluation benchmark of chest and abdomen CT reports annotated by radiologists error by error. On it and three chest X-ray benchmarks, RadMatch with an open 31B judge on consumer GPUs agrees with radiologists more closely than previous published metrics, and even reaches inter-expert agreement on ReXVal and on the significant errors of MetricEval-BodyCT. Radiologists auditing its decisions on 60 studies confirm 98.5% of its matches and 93.6% of the errors it counts. When used to evaluate state-of-the-art radiology report generation models, RadMatch shows that most CT models, unlike several chest X-ray models, actually leave more significant errors than an image-blind normal template, as their added unsupported findings outnumber the reference findings they correctly report. We provide RadMatch's code, its dashboard, the MetricEval-BodyCT dataset and all per-report scores.
  </details>

- **[Visual Token Coding for Video Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28008)**  `arXiv:2608.28008`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenxin Fang, Tao Chen, JunChao You, Jun Peng, Yiyi Zhou, Rongrong Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a new token compression paradigm for video Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), termed Visual Token Coding ($VTC$). Inspired by classical video coding principles, e.g., HEVC, VTC performs structured compression by predicting the I/P frames of a video and measuring their frame-wise residuals to estimate token redundancy. Based on this base paradigm, we further extend VTC with a set of dynamic budgeting designs towards more cost-effective token coding, termed $VTC_{dy}$. To validate VTC and $VTC_{dy}$, we apply them to three MLLMs and conduct experiments on a set of video understanding benchmarks. The experimental results show that as a base paradigm, VTC can achieve an average performance retention of 98.4% on Qwen3-VL under a 50% token compression ratio, and the enhanced $VTC_{dy}$ can further improve the retention to 100.3%, outperforming a set of compared methods. Moreover, as a plug-and-play design, VTC requires no additional tuning of MLLMs for token coding. Our code is given in the supplementary materials.
  </details>

- **[Video-OPSD: Exploiting Privileged Visual Evidence for On-Policy Self-Distillation in Video Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27065)**  `arXiv:2608.27065`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziyue Wang, Shiqi Huang, Weiwen Xu, Bihan Wen, Xudong Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) has recently emerged as an effective post-training paradigm that provides dense token-level supervision from a privileged self-teacher, yet it remains underexplored for Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). Existing methods typically construct privileged teachers by augmenting their context with additional information while keeping the primary input unchanged for both teacher and student. In video reasoning, however, privileged information can also be found within the primary input itself: videos contain substantial temporal redundancy, and only a few frames carry the evidence needed to answer a question. Building on this observation, we present $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$, an OPSD framework that exploits privileged visual evidence for both self-teacher construction and knowledge transfer. First, our Evidence-Grounded Self-Teacher conditions the teacher exclusively on annotated evidence frames, while the student reasons over a broader set of frames that contains them. This focused visual context enables more targeted supervision without introducing visual content unavailable to the student. Second, our Evidence-Guided Token Optimization adaptively weights token-level distillation according to an estimate of each reasoning token's reliance on privileged visual evidence, thereby emphasizing perceptually grounded reasoning. Across five video benchmarks and three Qwen-VL backbones, $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$ consistently outperforms Standard OPSD by 1.8-2.3 percentage points on average, establishing an effective and efficient post-training approach for Video-LLMs.
  </details>

- **[MVC-Bench: Benchmarking Calibration of Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27004)**  `arXiv:2608.27004`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ashshak Sharifdeen, Shihab Aaqil Ahamed, Ufaq Khan, Muhammad Akhtar Munir, Sujair Ibrahim, Mohamed Rafeek Mareer Ahamed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable evaluation of vision-language models (VLMs) and medical vision-language models (Medical-VLMs) requires calibrated confidence, particularly under realistic clinical conditions. However, existing efforts mainly focused on improving accuracy, leaving calibration in the medical domain underexplored. To this end, we propose MVC-Bench, a calibration-centric benchmark for medical image classification with VLMs and Medical-VLMs. MVC-Bench assesses the calibration across three axes: (i) robustness to modality, backbone, and domain shift (ii) effectiveness of calibration strategies and prompt-tuning methods (iii) stability under prompt-template and random-seed variations. The benchmark covers eight different backbones, three medical modalities, including fundus imaging, histopathology, and chest X-ray under in-domain and domain shift settings. It compares post-hoc calibration, train-time calibration, and zero-shot inference methods, together with six prompt-tuning methods. Across more than 1638 controlled experiments, we report accuracy and Expected Calibration Error (ECE) as primary metrics, and further report results with complementary calibration measures, including Maximum Calibration Error (MCE) and Adaptive Calibration Error (ACE). We further investigate the underlying causes of miscalibration in VLMs and Medical-VLMs and propose a simple train-time calibration method, Multi-Class Margin (MCM) regularization, which achieves lowest ECE on 10 out of 12 settings in in-domain and remains competitive under domain shifts. Collectively, MVC-Bench provides a structured evaluation framework and actionable guidance for improving calibration in safety-critical medical workflows.
  </details>

- **[OmniEdit-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Instruction-based Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05049)**  `arXiv:2608.05049`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenxuan Miao, Yutong Feng, Yi Lu, Yunfeng Yan, Donglian Qi, Shiwei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-based video editing (IVE) offers diverse applications, yet evaluating editing models remains challenging. Existing benchmarks suffer from two key limitations: narrow task coverage inherited from image editing that overlooks video-specific dimensions, and metrics that inadequately capture instruction fidelity, allowing visually plausible but incorrect edits to receive high scores. To address these limitations, we introduce OmniEdit-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark covering spatial, temporal, audio, and reference-based editing. It distinguishes between explicit and implicit instructions and incorporates reasoning-based scenarios to reflect real-world editing requirements. We further propose an evaluation framework that assesses accuracy, preservation, realism, and consistency using both human judges and a state-of-the-art vision-language model (VLM). An accuracy-aware penalty mechanism conditions the other three scores on accuracy, prioritizing instruction fidelity and penalizing plausible but incorrect edits. Experiments on prominent open-source and commercial IVE models reveal substantial room for improvement. OmniEdit-Bench provides a structured testbed for identifying model limitations and advancing instruction-based video editing. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Localize, Don't Beautify: Client-Side Control of Image-Editing APIs for Cosmetic Surgery Previews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02841)**  `arXiv:2608.02841`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sukhrobbek Ilyosbekov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ask a commercial image editor to preview a cosmetic procedure and it will often change more of the face than the request names: a jawline edit can also smooth the forehead or alter the eyes. Existing methods for confining an edit to one region require access to the model's internals, which a public editing API does not expose. We ask how much control is possible from the client side alone. In a pilot benchmark, five current commercial editing configurations perform facelift-style jaw-neck and rhinoplasty edits on synthetic faces under two levels of client-side control: the prompt alone, and a masked composite that cuts the edited region out of the response and pastes it back onto the original photograph through a landmark-derived mask. Of 162 attempted edits, 151 could be scored. ArcFace cosine measures identity preservation; a CIELAB pixel-change ratio measures how much change lands inside the requested region rather than a protected facial zone. On 15 faces, the masked composite improved localization over the paired prompt-only output by a median of 0.315 (95% face-clustered bootstrap interval 0.279-0.366) while retaining most of the target-region change. Editors differed in edit strength and identity retention, and one returned near-copies for the facelift request. This is a study of control, not clinical accuracy: the faces are synthetic, no surgeons rated the outputs, and each condition was generated once. Within that scope, keeping a surgical preview inside its intended region needs no access to the model; a mask and composite on the client enforce it across every editor tested, at low provider cost.
  </details>

- **[CADENA: Stepwise CAD Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00799)**  `arXiv:2608.00799`  `cs.CV`  
  _Soslan Kabisov, Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Antonio Rodriguez, Daniil Ignatiev, Nikita Gavrilov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-Aided Design (CAD) underpins modern engineering, yet converting existing shapes into editable models still demands substantial expert effort. Most AI systems emit the entire CAD program in a single pass, never inspecting the intermediate geometry. In contrast, human engineers build a part feature by feature, checking after each operation what remains to be modeled. We introduce CADENA (Spanish for "chain"), a model that reconstructs a 3D mesh as a parametric CAD program, growing its sequence of operations one at a time and comparing the target with the currently predicted geometry at every step. We also address the lack of benchmarks for evaluating reverse-engineering methods on mechanical parts, introducing CADENA-Bench, a benchmark that measures performance across categories of mechanical parts. CADENA outperforms prior methods on CADENA-Bench and on the DeepCAD, Fusion 360, and MCB datasets. Code is available atthis https URL, model weights atthis https URL, and CADENA-Bench atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VETO: Towards Protecting Images From Frontier AI Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27292)**  `arXiv:2607.27292`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jonas Grebe, Hossein Shakibania, Tobias Braun, Marcus Rohrbach, Anna Rohrbach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern image editors blur the boundary between image editing and text-to-image synthesis, extracting people and objects from a single photo and inserting them into new scenes without subject-specific fine-tuning. Existing defenses struggle to disrupt such manipulations in these models without large, visibly damaging perturbations to the images. We introduce VETO, a white-box image-cloaking method for open-weight editors that maximizes the entropy of attention between reference image and generation tokens to disrupt selective retrieval of source details, while requiring neither the future editing instruction nor modifications to the model. We also introduce VetoBench, a benchmark of 300 benign and harmful tasks balanced between within-scene editing and recontextualization. Full Pareto evaluations on FLUX.2 and FIBO-Edit across three benchmarks and five perturbation budgets demonstrate overall better protection-fidelity trade-offs, particularly against recontextualization. Protected images remain effective on fine-tuned and distilled variants of the editor without reoptimization.
  </details>

- **[Do Egocentric Video-Language Models Capture Both Hand- and Object-Centric Cues?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08514)**  `arXiv:2607.08514`  `cs.CV`  
  _Masatoshi Tateno, Alexandros Stergiou, Risa Shinoda, Yoichi Sato, Dima Damen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing hand-object interactions (HOIs) requires capturing both hand dynamics and object transformations, which provide complementary information for distinguishing visually similar manipulations. However, Video-Language Models (VLMs) trained solely on egocentric videos tend to over-rely on hand appearance, struggling to distinguish interactions with similar visual appearances but different dynamics (e.g., taking vs. kneading dough). To better utilize complementary hand- and object-centric cues, we propose a learning paradigm with (i) hand-object masked training to encode both hand- and object-centric cues and learn their relationships through cross-cue reconstruction, and (ii) an HOI-dynamics-aware decoder that grounds each cue through auxiliary location and semantic predictions. To assess how effectively models leverage hand- and object-centric cues for action recognition, we introduce Cue-Isolated HOI (CI-HOI), which independently evaluates recognition performance based on each cue. To enable this evaluation, we curate the DEHOI testbed using video inpainting to selectively remove either hands or manipulated objects, allowing us to evaluate action recognition with each cue isolated.
