DEHOI reveals that existing VLMs trained only on egocentric video rely more on hand-centric cues than VLMs trained on more non-egocentric video data. We show that our training paradigm mitigates this hand-centric bias, outperforming VLMs trained on the same egocentric data across DEHOI, standard action recognition, and object state recognition benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SPIRAL-360: Stratified Panoramic Instruction Replay and Angular Learning for Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02479)**  `arXiv:2607.02479`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingtao Xu, Zizhuo Lin, Jianwen Sun, Yi Yang, Yawei Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Localizing targets in $360^\circ$ environments requires combining global context with fine-grained evidence despite projection distortion and horizontal wrap-around. Uneven directional coverage compounds these challenges, particularly for seam-adjacent and polar targets. We present SPIRAL-360, a geometry-aware framework with separately trained single-turn and multi-turn models for panoramic localization. Spherically Stratified Replay (SSR) broadens directional coverage through stratified sampling and consistent transformations of panoramas, target annotations, and tool trajectories. TapSphere incorporates spherical distance priors into panoramic visual attention, while Spherical Direction Regularization (SDR) complements coordinate-token prediction with auxiliary direction supervision during training. The single-turn model predicts directly from the panorama, while the multi-turn model inspects tool-generated perspective views. We construct SPIRAL-360 Search, a curated dataset of trajectories interleaving reasoning, projection actions, and visual observations. Both models undergo full-parameter supervised fine-tuning followed by policy optimization. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art localization on S360 and competitive cross-dataset transfer, including H$^*$Bench. Systematic ablations support the effectiveness of this geometric learning design.
  </details>

- **[V-Zero: Gating On-Policy Distillation with Contrastive Visual Evidence for Fine-Grained Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25319)**  `arXiv:2606.25319`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoxiang Sun, Zhihang Yi, Langxuan Deng, Yuhao Zhou, Peiqi Jia, Jian Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained visual reasoning requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to identify task-relevant visual evidence and ground their reasoning in local image regions. Existing methods typically rely on reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards or supervised fine-tuning on large-scale annotated reasoning traces, resulting in costly exploration, hand-designed verification rules, or heavy dependence on textual supervision. While On-Policy Distillation (OPD) offers a promising alternative via dense teacher supervision, its token-level objective inherently focuses on local token corrections, failing to assess global trajectory quality. In this work, we first reinterpret OPD as a stop-gradient alignment objective, revealing that its potential is limited by the absence of trajectory-level discrimination. Motivated by this observation, we propose V-Zero, which gates on-policy distillation with contrastive visual evidence. Specifically, V-Zero evaluates each trajectory under paired positive and negative visual views; the resulting likelihood contrast forms a trajectory-level gate that selectively amplifies updates from visually well-supported reasoning paths while suppressing ungrounded ones. Experiments on multiple visual reasoning and perception benchmarks show that V-Zero improves the Qwen3.5-4B backbone by 3.9 points on average while preserving strong generalization. Notably, V-Zero achieves more than 3$\times$ and 6$\times$ training speedups over representative supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning baselines, respectively. It requires neither annotated textual reasoning traces nor external reward signals. Code and dataset are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RPC-GS: Gaussian Splatting with native RPC Rendering for Satellite Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06690)**  `arXiv:2606.06690`  `cs.CV`  
  _Valentin Wagner, Sebastian Bullinger, Christoph Bodensteiner, Michael Arens_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present RPC-GS, the first Gaussian Splatting framework for satellite imagery that operates natively with Rational Polynomial Camera (RPC) models. The RPC model is the de facto standard for representing the complex imaging geometry of modern pushbroom satellite sensors. To simplify rendering, prior satellite Gaussian Splatting methods replace the RPC model with perspective or affine camera approximations, leading to geometric errors during reconstruction. RPC-GS avoids these approximations by projecting Gaussian means and covariances directly through the RPC model during the splatting process. We embed the RPC model in a chain of carefully selected geo-coordinate transformations representing a mapping from splatting-suitable scene coordinates to image coordinates. To map the Gaussian covariance matrices, we derive a numerically robust Jacobian-based covariance projection for the (partially nonlinear) coordinate transformations. Since RPCs lack an explicit notion of camera depth, we integrate a metric ray-based depth formulation. We benchmark RPC, perspective, and affine camera models in a unified framework, with our native RPC renderer achieving the lowest reconstruction error on leading satellite benchmark datasets, improving mean altitude error over perspective and affine approximations by 34.3% and 13.5% on DFC2019, and by 5.0% and 1.2% on IARPA2016. Our implementation is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EVIDENT: Routing MLLM Adaptation through Entity-Grounded Visual Evidence for Cross-Domain Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26104)**  `arXiv:2605.26104`  `cs.CV`  
  _Geo Ahn, Jiwook Han, Youngrae Kim, Joonseok Lee, Jinwoo Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning MLLMs for Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) often improves in-domain performance but degrades sharply under domain shift. In this work, we find that this failure is primarily driven not just by unseen query concepts, but by visual domain shift, which prevents the model from coupling its learned temporal localization knowledge with its inherent entity-attention capability. To address this, we introduce EVIDENT, a parameter-efficient adaptation framework that anchors temporal grounding in the inherent entity-attention of pre-trained MLLMs by routing VTG adaptation through explicit visual entity evidence. EVIDENT consists of three components: (i) an Entity Bottleneck Adapter that transforms dense visual tokens into compact entity-level slots, (ii) an Entity-Binding Distillation loss that instills objectness priors into the semantically unstructured MLLM visual space, guiding each slot to bind to a coherent entity, and (iii) an Entity-to-eVidence gating mechanism that leverages the captured entities as evidence, steering the model to localize moments containing query-relevant entities. Together, these components enable VTG fine-tuning to rely on entity-grounded evidence rather than brittle dataset shortcuts. Experiments on cross-domain VTG benchmarks show that EVIDENT consistently improves out-of-domain robustness while preserving competitive in-domain performance with modest parameter overhead. These results suggest that entity-level grounding is an effective inductive bias for generalizable temporal localization.
  </details>

- **[CURVAS-PDACVI: A Multi-Rater CT Dataset and Challenge for Pancreatic Cancer Segmentation and Vascular Involvement Assessme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27582)**  `arXiv:2604.27582`  `cs.CV`  
  _M. Riera-Marín, O. K. Sikha, J. Rodríguez-Comas, M. S. May, T. Kirscher, X. Coubez, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical resection remains the only potentially curative treatment for pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC), and eligibility depends on accurate assessment of vascular invasion (VI), i.e., tumor extension into adjacent critical vessels. Despite its importance for preoperative staging and surgical planning, computational VI assessment remains underexplored. Two major challenges are the lack of public datasets and the diagnostic ambiguity at the tumor-vessel interface, which leads to substantial inter-rater variability even among expert radiologists. To address these limitations, we introduce the CURVAS-PDACVI Dataset and Challenge, an open benchmark for uncertainty-aware AI in PDAC staging based on a densely annotated dataset with five independent expert annotations per scan. We also propose a multi-metric evaluation framework that extends beyond spatial overlap to include probabilistic calibration and VI assessment. Evaluation of six state-of-the-art methods shows that strong global volumetric overlap does not necessarily translate into reliable performance at clinically critical tumor-vessel interfaces. In particular, methods optimized for binary segmentation perform competitively on average overlap metrics, but often degrade in high-complexity cases with low expert consensus, either collapsing in volume or overextending at uncertain boundaries. In contrast, methods that model inter-rater disagreement produce better calibrated probabilistic maps and show greater robustness in these ambiguous cases. The benchmark highlights the limitations of volumetric accuracy as a proxy for localized surgical utility, motivating uncertainty-aware probabilistic models for preoperative decision-making.
  </details>

- **[Graph-augmented Segmentation for Enhanced In-Situ Geometry Reconstruction of Complex Shapes in Laser Powder Bed Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24234)**  `arXiv:2604.24234`  `cs.CV`  
  _Stefano Raimondo, Matteo Bugatti, Marco Grasso_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In situ geometric inspection in Laser Powder Bed Fusion of Metals (PBF-LB/M) opens new possibilities for anticipated defect detection and more efficient qualification. It relies on image segmentation to reconstruct layer-wise geometries and identify manufacturing deviations as the part is being built. However, existing approaches face a critical yet largely overlooked challenge: photometric out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, namely the ability to maintain reconstruction accuracy when image appearance differs from the conditions represented during model training. This study represents the first exploration of a graph-augmented U-Net architecture for in situ geometric inspection in PBF-LB/M. The hybrid network integrates a Graph Neural Network (GNN) within the bottleneck to enhance reconstruction robustness under photometric distribution shifts. It is evaluated against conventional U-Net and active contour segmentation methods using two experimental case studies involving different lattice geometries, materials, and manufacturing setups, with X-ray computed tomography providing reference geometries. Generalization is assessed across different training and inference conditions and under controlled image perturbations. Results show that the UNet-GNN achieves higher reconstruction accuracy and consistency than the benchmark methods, with more pronounced improvements when training and inference conditions differ. Its advantages are confirmed across both case studies and under controlled photometric perturbations, while preliminary anomaly detection experiments indicate that improved robustness does not necessarily compromise sensitivity to geometric deviations. These findings highlight graph augmentation as a promising strategy for more reliable in situ geometric inspection under heterogeneous manufacturing conditions.
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Skeleton-Based Action Segmentation via Hierarchical Spatiotemporal Vector Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15196)**  `arXiv:2604.15196`  `cs.CV`  
  _Umer Ahmed, Syed Ahmed Mahmood, Fawad Javed Fateh, M. Shaheer Luqman, M. Zeeshan Zia, Quoc-Huy Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a novel hierarchical spatiotemporal vector quantization framework for unsupervised skeleton-based temporal action segmentation. We first introduce a hierarchical approach, which includes two consecutive levels of vector quantization. Specifically, the lower level associates skeletons with fine-grained subactions, while the higher level further aggregates subactions into action-level representations. Our hierarchical approach outperforms the non-hierarchical baseline, while primarily exploiting spatial cues by reconstructing input skeletons. Next, we extend our approach by leveraging both spatial and temporal information, yielding a hierarchical spatiotemporal vector quantization scheme. In particular, our hierarchical spatiotemporal approach performs multi-level clustering, while simultaneously recovering input skeletons and their corresponding timestamps. Lastly, extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks, including HuGaDB, LARa, and BABEL, demonstrate that our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art performance and reduces segment length bias in unsupervised skeleton-based temporal action segmentation.
  </details>

- **[CausalSpatial: A Benchmark for Object-Centric Causal Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13304)**  `arXiv:2601.13304`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenxin Ma, Chenlong Wang, Ruisheng Yuan, Hao Chen, Nanru Dai, S. Kevin Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans can look at a static scene and instantly predict what happens next -- will moving this object cause a collision? We call this ability Causal Spatial Reasoning. However, current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) cannot do this, as they remain largely restricted to static spatial perception, struggling to answer "what-if" questions in a 3D scene. We introduce CausalSpatial, a diagnostic benchmark evaluating whether models can anticipate consequences of object motions across four tasks: Collision, Compatibility, Occlusion, and Trajectory. Results expose a severe gap: humans score 84% while GPT-5 achieves only 54%. Why do MLLMs fail? Our analysis uncovers a fundamental deficiency: models over-rely on textual chain-of-thought reasoning that drifts from visual evidence, producing fluent but spatially ungrounded hallucinations. To address this, we propose the Causal Object World model (COW), a framework that externalizes the simulation process by generating videos of hypothetical dynamics. With explicit visual cues of causality, COW enables models to ground their reasoning in physical reality rather than linguistic priors. We make the dataset and code publicly available here:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Retinexformer: One-stage Retinex-based Transformer for Low-light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06705)**  `arXiv:2303.06705`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Hao Bian, Jing Lin, Haoqian Wang, Radu Timofte, Yulun Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When enhancing low-light images, many deep learning algorithms are based on the Retinex theory. However, the Retinex model does not consider the corruptions hidden in the dark or introduced by the light-up process. Besides, these methods usually require a tedious multi-stage training pipeline and rely on convolutional neural networks, showing limitations in capturing long-range dependencies. In this paper, we formulate a simple yet principled One-stage Retinex-based Framework (ORF). ORF first estimates the illumination information to light up the low-light image and then restores the corruption to produce the enhanced image. We design an Illumination-Guided Transformer (IGT) that utilizes illumination representations to direct the modeling of non-local interactions of regions with different lighting conditions. By plugging IGT into ORF, we obtain our algorithm, Retinexformer. Comprehensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that our Retinexformer significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on thirteen benchmarks. The user study and application on low-light object detection also reveal the latent practical values of our method. Code, models, and results are available atthis https URL. An online Gradio demo is available on Hugging Face atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs Understand What They Predict? Evaluating Latent Causal Structure Inference in Financial Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23533)**  `arXiv:2611.23533`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixiang Zheng, Yuechen Jiang, Zhiwei Liu, Jimin Huang, Yuyan Wang, Sophia Ananiadou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated through their ability to predict future outcomes, implicitly assuming that predictive success reflects an understanding of the underlying system. However, accurate predictions can often be achieved without recovering the latent mechanisms that generate observable outcomes. To examine this assumption, we introduce LUCID, a benchmark that evaluates whether models can infer latent market structures from event descriptions and historical financial observations. Models generate event-induced causal graphs representing inferred market structure and are evaluated through a reconstruction-based framework. Our evaluation of 6 open-weight and proprietary LLMs shows that adding their generated graphs generally reduces reconstruction quality compared with using historical observations alone, although a few metrics improve. These findings suggest that inferring informative, event-specific market structures from observable evidence remains challenging for the evaluated LLMs.
  </details>

- **[FlashREINFORCE: Critic-Free Single-Rollout Asynchronous RL for Agentic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18627)**  `arXiv:2611.18627`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Hu, Yifan Zhang, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Shaokun Zhang, Hongqing Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asynchronous reinforcement learning suits long-horizon agents with irregular rollout times, but group-relative updates require repeated rollouts of each prompt. However, group-based sampling limits prompt coverage at a fixed rollout budget and introduces group synchronization barriers. One rollout per prompt avoids these constraints but requires learning from stale, variable-length trajectories without a sibling baseline. We present FlashREINFORCE, to our knowledge the first critic-free single-rollout framework that scales to long asynchronous training and large models. It combines One-Batch REINFORCE, a Sequence Trust Region, and Sample-Mean Optimization, which provide prompt coverage and batch centering while controlling policy drift and length-amplified negative updates. Each component is easy to implement, and the result is their combination: a batch-mean baseline is stable under sample-mean loss, and each common alternative degrades or collapses training. FlashREINFORCE remains stable through 6,000 updates on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B at policy lag four, and through 1,450 on the 30B MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B at lag eight. Under identical loss, data, and lag it matches a strengthened group-relative baseline within 0.5 points, and with half the rollouts exceeds published synchronous baselines on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B (38.0 versus 36.3 five-benchmark mean). On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct it maintains tool use where GRPO stops calling tools and achieves 98.3%/96.5% seen/unseen ALFWorld success.
  </details>

- **[DV-Lens: Revealing the Functional Organization of Language Model Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04489)**  `arXiv:2610.04489`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Chenhang Cui, Jian Yu, Jiani Luo, Shuyi Miao, Dingrui Liu, Rui Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding parameter functions helps elucidate the internal mechanisms of large language models (LLMs). However, how to connect parameters from different modules to verifiable output effects and further characterize the relationship between their functional organization and model capability remains to be explored. To this end, we introduce the downstream vocabulary lens (DV-Lens), a parameter-level interpretability framework that links native parameter directions to their downstream vocabulary responses. Specifically, we first estimate module-specific downstream Jacobians over a reference prompt set for attention query, key, value, and output (Q/K/V/O) projections and feed-forward networks (FFNs). Second, we use these mappings to project native parameter columns into the final vocabulary space, obtaining signed readouts that characterize their average local output responses. Third, we group parameter columns by their vocabulary readouts and introduce downstream vocabulary complexity (DV-Complexity), which quantifies within-group structural variation using normalized reconstruction residuals of the original weights. At the parameter level, randomized controls and finite-difference tests show that DV-Lens readouts capture non-random vocabulary structure and predict local logit changes with 98.0% coordinate-orientation agreement across 720 cases from nine models. These readouts further guide parameter ablation, steering, and swapping across 21 models, shifting target-token probabilities in the predicted directions under controlled conditions. At the model level, the joint-parameter score of DV-Complexity achieves a Spearman correlation of 0.904 with benchmark-based capability rankings across 48 language models. Together, these results provide intervention-based evidence for DV-Lens interpretations and reveal an association between DV-Complexity and model capability.
  </details>

- **[Ceiling-Clipped Acceptance Histograms Indicate Stranded Speed-up in Block-Diffusion Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30427)**  `arXiv:2608.30427`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ephrem Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding speeds up generation using an efficient draft model (drafter) to propose tokens that a target model verifies in one pass, preserving the target's output distribution. High-acceptance block-diffusion drafters, such as DFlash and DFlare, fill an entire block in one parallel pass. In many cycles, the target model accepts all draft tokens in the block, so the drafter exhausts its trained horizon before verification ever fails. We call this unrealized acceptance stranded speed-up. Evaluating a drafter by its per-prompt or per-cycle mean committed length alone hides this phenomenon, whereas the acceptance histogram exposes this bottleneck as a spike in the ceiling bin, the fraction of cycles that accept the entire block. We recommend reviewing the acceptance histogram before spending more training compute. Naively widening the block at inference could reduce acceptance for drafters with bidirectional attention, since, once the block outgrows its training size, the drafter's bidirectional attention shifts its distribution even at early positions, which empirically degrades acceptance there. We therefore post-train the drafter to a longer block with a short curriculum that emphasizes the newly exposed positions, a method we call DBloom. We continue training the pretrained DFlash and DFlare drafters at the original block size of 16, before expanding them to 24 (B16 to B24) on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B. Compared to the original B16 drafters, this technique raises per-prompt committed length by up to +1.7 tokens and decode speed-up by a median of +10.2% versus +5.7% without the continuation. On high-ceiling benchmarks, the speed-up rises to +11.9%. On Gemma-4-12B-IT and GPT-OSS-20B, expansion also raises committed length on every benchmark. Against JetSpec, a tree drafter, DBloom has higher committed length by point estimate on every benchmark at tree budgets up to 64 nodes.
  </details>

- **[Clover-35B-A3B-Preview Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24636)**  `arXiv:2611.24636`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _DiffuSpace Technical Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Clover-35B-A3B-Preview (Clover), a mixture-of-experts diffusion language model with 35 billion total parameters and 3 billion activated parameters, supporting thinking-mode generation. Clover adapts Gated Delta Networks to block diffusion by combining recurrent memory of previously generated context with bidirectional softmax attention inside the current block. Historical memory readout competes with current-block tokens in a shared softmax, with learnable block-size calibration and a separate, zero-initialized right-context convolution branch. Clover leads the seven evaluated agent metrics, including 73.6 on SWE-bench Verified and 51.7 on Terminal-Bench 2.1. On internal agent workloads, it reaches a reported generation speed of 680 tokens/s using one NVIDIA GB300 GPU at BF16 precision and batch size one. We organize the evaluation around model quality, drafting and verification costs, and deployment under constrained memory. We also describe a decision-model variant that generates answers to related questions in one denoising pass. On an internal set of 500 groups, joint prediction raises question accuracy from 90.37% to 93.88% and all-correct group accuracy from 65.4% to 77.0%.
  </details>

- **[Piccolo: Co-Evolving Decision Models and Data through Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24651)**  `arXiv:2611.24651`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenqing Ling, Qirui Jiao, Daoyuan Chen, Cheng Chen, Xinji Mai, Haowen Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.
  </details>

- **[Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18397)**  `arXiv:2611.18397`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.
  </details>

- **[Once Upon a Bias: Gender Stereotyping in LLM-Generated Children's Stories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24156)**  `arXiv:2611.24156`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sayumi Muthukumarana, Roshan Godaliyadda, Parakrama Ekanayake, Roshan Ragel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to create stories for children. Since bedtime stories can shape children's perceptions of social roles, this raises concerns about whether LLMs adapt narrative portrayals according to the intended recipient's gender. We conducted a large-scale study to examine gender stereotyping in LLM-generated bedtime stories. Using matched daughter/son prompts and a five-generation design, we analyze 62,873 retained stories from 16 LLMs across 35 country settings and 12 narrator roles. We introduce a multi-level evaluation framework combining context-adjusted lexical rate contrasts, relative stereotype alignment, narrator-recipient interaction analysis, human evaluation, and representation-level interventions to examine both the consistency and interpretability of these differences. Our results reveal systematic differences in narrative framing: son-directed stories emphasize higher agency and lower communion compared to daughter-directed stories. While narrator gender expresses limited moderation on these patterns, high sociability exhibits a more pronounced interaction. Human evaluations demonstrate consistent agreement, although correspondence with lexical measures varies across narrative dimensions. Additionally, hidden-state analyses reveal decodable recipient-gender information. These findings demonstrate persistent yet context-dependent representational disparities in LLM-generated children's stories and motivate narrative-specific bias evaluation beyond short-form benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Replacement: Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23750)**  `arXiv:2611.23750`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuaidi Wang, Zhuocheng Gong, Xuejie Liu, Jianguo Li, Yu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer a flexible alternative to autoregressive models, enabling any-order infilling. While most existing decoding methods treat decoded tokens as fixed, some recent models allow previously decoded tokens to be revised only by replacing them with alternative tokens, a paradigm we refer to as replacement-only editing (ROE). Although ROE can correct individual tokens, it is inefficient for sequence shifts caused by missing or extra tokens and struggles when errors occur across many positions. To address these limitations, we propose LED (Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models), a post-training framework that extends ROE with insertion and deletion, enabling Levenshtein-style refinement of generated sequences. It uses two training designs. First, it reconstructs edit supervision on the fly during supervised fine-tuning using longest-common-subsequence alignment, enabling insertion and deletion learning without manually annotated labels. Second, it adopts multi-stage training, where the state from one stage becomes the input to the next, exposing the model to its earlier errors and enabling it to correct them. When applied to the 16B-parameter LLaDA2.1-Mini Base, LED raises the average score across twelve benchmarks from 54.86 to 57.10 under confidence-guided decoding while increasing tokens per forward from 6.14 to 7.05, whereas under confidence-free decoding it raises the average score from 27.52 to 47.19. We further show that LED achieves higher pass@k at large k than thresholded decoding without editing, demonstrating improved generation diversity and revealing the diversity potential of DLMs without confidence guidance. LED also improves robustness to large block sizes, raising the average score from 36.97 to 51.34 at block size 128. Together, these results suggest that LED supports more robust self-correction in DLMs.
  </details>

- **[FutureBench: A Renewable Framework for Evaluating Knowledge-Context Conflict in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23601)**  `arXiv:2611.23601`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Cheng Lai, Hen-Hsen Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asking a model to check retrieved text can also tell it which source to trust. We introduce FutureBench, a renewable framework for evaluating knowledge-context conflict using timestamped questions, controlled passage triplets, and no-context probes. Across 3,629 questions and six models, Verify (a prompt combining checking with passage priority) raises false-answer adoption in all five models tested on pre-cutoff events, mainly by displacing answers that were correct under Baseline, which allows both passage evidence and model knowledge. On post-cutoff events, only o4-mini increases adoption (+13.7 percentage points). A four-prompt comparison on 902 post-cutoff questions finds that passage priority without checking raises adoption, while moving to Verify lowers it in all five models. Adversarial-Aware, which warns of misleading passages and prioritizes model knowledge, restores 82.5-98.9% of displaced pre-cutoff answers with under one point of change in true-passage accuracy. FutureBench supports repeated evaluation on fixed cohorts and extension to new events as models change.
  </details>

- **[AStockArena: Beyond Returns, Can We Entrust Real Capital to the Decisions of Financial Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00515)**  `arXiv:2611.00515`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyu Liu, Sing Kwong Lai, Xuanzhi Feng, HaoYang Shang, Yang Li, Zihao Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in dynamic financial trading, yet real-time trading benchmarks mainly evaluate agents by cumulative returns and risk-adjusted performance. Such outcome metrics cannot show whether trades are faithfully reported, supported by available evidence, and consistent with account states. We introduce AStockArena, a real-time decision-auditing benchmark for continuous trading in China's A-share market. Under shared market conditions and execution constraints, we evaluate 13 open- and closed-source models over 3,198 sequential decisions. AStockArena reconstructsthe actions actually posted to accounts by aligning reports, trade requests, execution responses, and account states, and audits them with eight checks spanning execution traceability (O), evidence sufficiency (RK), and financial meta-awareness (B), combining record verification with consensus expert annotation. Of 1,070 trading points, 72.99% exhibit at least one audit gap; model-level rates range from52.2% to 91.2%, with evidence gaps most prevalent. Historical trade-filtering replay further shows thatportfolio outcomes are sensitive to these flagged trades. Decision-process auditing thus offers a verifiable complement to return-based evaluation of financial agents.
  </details>

- **[RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23466)**  `arXiv:2609.23466`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Improving Cross-Lingual Token Representations by Adding a Pinch of SALT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09953)**  `arXiv:2609.09953`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Guillem Ramírez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-lingual sentence encoders enable scalable transfer across hundreds of languages, powering applications such as translation mining and zero-shot learning in low-resource settings. Although trained for sentence-level alignment, they are increasingly also applied to token-level tasks such as hallucination detection and sequence tagging, exposing a mismatch between training and usage. We propose SALT, a lightweight post-training method that improves token representations by injecting span-level supervision into existing sentence encoders. Across five multilingual token-level benchmarks, SALT achieves the best overall results on four of them, outperforming alternative fine-tuning strategies and competitive encoders. It also improves sentence-level performance on cross-lingual retrieval and classification tasks. These results demonstrate that span-level supervision is an effective signal for improving both token and sentence representations.
  </details>

- **[MedExAgent: Training LLM Agents to Ask, Examine, and Diagnose in Noisy Clinical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07058)**  `arXiv:2605.07058`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yicheng Gao, Xiaolin Zhou, Yahan Li, Yue Zhao, Ruishan Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world clinical diagnosis is a complex process in which the doctor is required to obtain information from both interaction with the patient and conducting medical exams. Additionally, the doctor needs to adapt to different patient personas, as well as noisy and incomplete information that can happen at any time during the process. However, existing benchmarks for medical LLMs and methods for automatic diagnosis largely simplify this process by reducing it to single-turn question answering, noise-free conversations, or sequential exam making, etc., ignoring the interactive and uncertain nature of clinical diagnosis. In this paper, we aim to address this gap by formalizing clinical diagnosis as a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) with three action types: questioning the patient, ordering medical exams as tool calls, and issuing a diagnosis. We also introduce a systematic noise model comprising seven patient noise types and three exam noise types. Using our proposed environment, we train an effective diagnosis agent, \textbf{MedExAgent}, through a two-stage pipeline that first performs supervised finetuning on synthetic conversations structured after the Calgary-Cambridge model for clinical interviews, and then applies DAPO to optimize a composite reward capturing diagnostic accuracy, tool call quality, and exam cost including financial cost and patient discomfort. Through extensive experiments and ablation studies, we demonstrate that MedExAgent achieves diagnostic performance comparable to larger models while maintaining cost-efficient examination strategies.
  </details>

- **[HorizonBench: Long-Horizon Personalization with Evolving Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17283)**  `arXiv:2604.17283`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyue Stella Li, Bhargavi Paranjape, Kerem Oktar, Zhongyao Ma, Gelin Zhou, Lin Guan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personal assistants now talk with the same user for months, and users' preferences change as their lives change; an assistant that keeps serving a preference the user has outgrown fails that user. Real conversations do not record when or why a preference changed, and existing benchmarks either hold preferences fixed or have the user state the change, so they cannot tell whether a model forgot what the user said or failed to update its belief about the user. We introduce HorizonBench and its data generator, which samples how a user's preferences change and generates six months of conversation from that sequence; each change follows a life event that the user mentions without stating the new preference. A human longitudinal preference dataset that we collect and release, 83,024 ratings from 160 people over 13 weeks, sets how often and how much life events change preferences. HorizonBench contains 4,245 questions from 360 users, and when a preference has changed, its earlier value is one of the answer options. All 25 frontier models we evaluate choose the outdated preference more often than evenly spread errors would predict, and the best reaches 52.8% accuracy. Two of the newest models score no higher when given only the relevant conversations than with the full history, yet one added sentence stating the change raises their accuracy by 11 to 22 points, and retrieval and memory methods do not recover the lost accuracy. Remembering what a user said and knowing what the user prefers now are therefore separate capabilities, and HorizonBench, its generator, and the human dataset support research on both.
  </details>

- **[Jailbreak Detection on Large Language Models via Contrastive Prototype Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24936)**  `arXiv:2611.24936`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Qiu, Yuanyuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jailbreak attempts and harmful prompts remain a challenge for the safe deployment of large language models. We study whether contrastively learned representations can support safety detection without training an additional downstream classifier. We propose Jailbreak Detection with Prototype Inference (JDPI), which uses binary safety labels to construct harmful and benign prototypes and makes predictions from relative prototype distances. JDPI uses only binary safety labels for encoder adaptation, prototype construction, and validation. In our setup, it uses fewer labeled examples than the classifier baseline (Method A) by avoiding the separate split used to train the downstream classifier. Across four benchmarks covering different input formats, contrastive fine-tuning improves JDPI's detection accuracy by 9.1-18.0 percentage points. Using the same encoder, JDPI achieves slightly higher ROC-AUC on one benchmark and stays within 0.017 of the baseline across all four. With dataset-specific adaptation, JDPI exceeds the released Llama Guard 3-8B baseline by 0.173-0.310 in ROC-AUC across the four benchmarks. In multi-turn replay on TomGibbs, JDPI uses accumulated user history as the conversation unfolds, achieving higher conversation-level accuracy than when it uses the current turn alone. Across 12 directed transfers, JDPI outperforms the classifier by an average of 1.59 percentage points in accuracy, demonstrating stronger cross-dataset robustness.
  </details>

- **[Ground, Iterate, Trace: Decomposing AI Research Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24887)**  `arXiv:2611.24887`  `cs.CL`  
  _Samson Yu, Qiran Zou, Jing Yu Lim, Rushi Shah, Anirudh Goyal, Dianbo Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have been widely used to generate plausible research ideas, yet it remains unclear what makes automated ideation effective and how generated novel proposals arise from prior scientific work. We introduce the Creative AI Researcher (CAR), an agentic framework combining literature grounding, iterative development and explicit conceptual operators. CAR constructs a semantic knowledge graph from retrieved literature and equips an ideation agent with graph-retrieval tools and four operators: bisociation, analogy, exploration and elaboration. Each operator call is checked against structural acceptance conditions and recorded, producing a trace of intermediate transformations and declared literature sources. We evaluate CAR with two language-model backbones across 40 LiveIdeaBench research topics and two execution benchmarks. CAR achieves significantly higher methodological novelty (MN) than single-shot prompting and The AI Scientist. Its methodological novelty does not differ significantly from the Research Ideation Agent, while its feasibility scores are higher on both backbones. With a shared experiment executor, CAR achieves the highest pairwise win rate among the evaluated AI methods on FML-bench-Lite and BioXArena. In a ten-condition ablation study, MN point estimates favor CAR over one-step proposal on both backbones, but no MN contrast survives correction for multiple comparisons. The results do not establish a consistent quality benefit from explicit operators beyond free-form iteration. CAR makes the sequence of operations and declared literature sources inspectable, separating judged idea quality from procedural traceability.
  </details>

- **[On-Policy Morphing into Hybrid Attention Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24809)**  `arXiv:2611.24809`  `cs.CL`  
  _Disen Lan, Xuanda Wang, Jianbin Zheng, Huafeng Kuang, Wei Tang, Yu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid attention models combine full and linear attention to achieve a better quality--efficiency trade-off. Converting pretrained Transformers into such models requires identifying which layers should retain full attention under a prescribed budget. We identify two off-policy mismatches in FlashMorph's layer selection: (i) continuous branch interpolation and teacher-forced hidden states differ from discrete hybrid computation; and (ii) frozen model weights fail to account for parameter adaptation during subsequent recovery. We introduce FlashMorph2, an on-policy layer-selection method that (I) evaluates sampled discrete hybrid architectures end to end using their own hidden states, and (II) jointly optimizes layer-selection scores and model weights under the recovery objective to reflect post-adaptation performance. Experiments across Qwen3 model scales and linear-attention variants show that FlashMorph2 consistently outperforms existing layer-selection methods, improving average scores over FlashMorph by $+2.42$ points on RULER, $+1.08$ on LongBench, and $+0.76$ on recall-intensive tasks, while completing layer selection in only $0.72$--$3.57$ GPU hours across 0.6B--8B models, demonstrating high efficiency and scalability.
  </details>

- **[LipSpell: A Language-Independent Phonetic Scoring Framework for Spelling Correction Combining Distinctive-Feature Edit Distance, Keyboard Geometry, and Corpus Frequency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24285)**  `arXiv:2611.24285`  `cs.CL`  
  _Leonardo Araujo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic spelling correction must handle both keyboard typographical slips and cognitive or phonetic misspellings where writers approximate a word's pronunciation. Conventional spell checkers rely either on orthographic edit distance and keyboard proximity--which struggle when phonetic errors diverge by multiple characters--or on English-centric phonetic hashes such as Soundex and Metaphone. We present LipSpell, a unified, language-independent phonetic scoring engine paired with swappable language-specific G2P frontends, keyboard maps, and lexicons integrating: (1) a precomputed |P|x|P| phone-distance matrix from universal IPA distinctive features, (2) physical keyboard geometry, and (3) corpus unigram frequency priors, accelerated by a two-stage symmetric-delete and seven-class phonetic-code index. Across six English benchmarks (including 1,000 L2 errors from TOEFL-Spell), LipSpell outperforms Norvig and Hunspell on every dataset and beats Aspell on Norvig1 (78.52% vs. 75.56%), Holbrook (32.00% vs. 26.67%), and TOEFL-Spell (77.30% vs. 72.80%). Evaluated across six languages spanning five branches (Germanic, Romance, Slavic, Semitic, Turkic), three families (Indo-European, Afro-Asiatic, Turkic), and three scripts, LipSpell achieves strong fixed-weight transfer on Brazilian Portuguese (73.00% Top-1 on n=500 Wikipedia without NFD tolerance and 75.80% with NFD vs. 66.40% Norvig, 64.20% Hunspell, 64.00% Aspell) and Russian Cyrillic (85.67% vs. 78.50% Norvig). On 50/50 clean non-word splits with lexicon cleaning and weak-character indels, tuning yields strong gains over Norvig in Portuguese (86.97% vs. 76.75%), Turkish (81.67% vs. 79.05%), Hebrew Abjad (74.59% vs. 62.30%), and French (74.53% vs. 67.92%), while fixed weights remain optimal for English (80.37%) and Russian (85.67%).
  </details>

- **[MIRAGE: A Framework for Multi-hop Reasoning under Dynamic Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24058)**  `arXiv:2611.24058`  `cs.CL`  
  _Heyang Xu, HanWen Hao, Haifeng Wang, Haoyi Zhou, Jianxin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop Question Answering (MQA) requires large language models (LLMs) to retrieve and synthesize multiple pieces of evidence into a coherent reasoning path, which is essential for complex tasks. However, prior work on knowledge updating for MQA has largely focused on simple fact edits, overlooking a more challenging and realistic setting---\emph{Knowledge Injection}---in which the model must simultaneously accommodate both conflicting updates and entirely novel information. Under this setting, a hop-level answer is often wrong simply because the model trusts the wrong knowledge source: it clings to outdated parametric memory when the injected knowledge conflicts with it (K2), and it cannot answer when the required fact is entirely new (K3).To investigate this, we introduce NEXUS, the first benchmark tailored for multi-hop reasoning under Knowledge Injection, which explicitly covers both conflicting (K2) and incremental (K3) knowledge. We further propose MIRAGE, an efficient, training-free framework whose core component, the Correlation-aware Dual-path Reasoner (CDR), resolves such per-hop source-trust errors via P(True)-based confidence routing, complemented by a lightweight template-guided corrector for residual structural deviations. Extensive experiments across four LLM families and two benchmarks demonstrate that MIRAGE consistently improves both final-answer accuracy and hop-level reasoning accuracy, establishing a new state of the art in this challenging setting.
  </details>

- **[Localizing Text Anonymization for Trustworthy AI: Extending RAT-Bench to Malaysian Microdata and PII](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23704)**  `arXiv:2611.23704`  `cs.CL`  
  _David Hong Liang Chew, Zexi Yao, Nataša Krčo, Matthieu Meeus, Waqas Khalid Obeidy, Yves-Alexandre de Montjoye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As governments and organizations adopt foundation models for public services and workplace productivity, sensitive citizen, employee, and administrative text may enter LLM workflows through inference, retrieval, fine-tuning, or local model development. We examine whether text anonymizers transfer to new deployment settings by extending RAT-Bench to Malaysia using local microdata, Malaysian PII formats, and culturally grounded transcripts in Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia. We evaluate NER- and LLM-based anonymizers using an LLM attacker that infers attributes from anonymized text, measuring both re-identification success and text utility. Across Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia, the two LLM anonymizers provide the strongest explicit privacy-utility trade-offs, reducing Easy/Hard re-identification risk to 23-29% while preserving BLEU scores of 0.77-0.94. Non-LLM tools show sharper failure modes, either preserving utility while leaving high residual risk or reducing risk through severe over-redaction, especially in Bahasa Malaysia. These findings suggest that anonymizers should be evaluated against deployment-relevant languages, identifier formats, and cultural contexts before being relied on in public-sector, workplace, or local AI pipelines.
  </details>

- **[Jetriever: Shifting Agent Memory Retrieval from Similarity to Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21478)**  `arXiv:2611.21478`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruizhe Li, Licheng Zhang, Mingxuan Du, Zhendong Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a stored memory helps answer a user's question often depends on world knowledge rather than surface similarity. Jetriever replaces similarity search with full-pool utility scoring: given a query, it uses Jev, an LLM-derived System One decision model, to score every stored memory for the probability that it is useful to the answer, ranking the memory pool directly by those probabilities. On InMind's 125 indirect-query tasks, Jetriever places the gold exchange first in 84.8% of cases (versus 1.6% for BM25 and 0.0% for MiniLM with user-turn indexing) and reaches 76.0% end-to-end Application, compared with at most 16.0% across published retrieval and memory systems. A controlled 20-question comparison disentangles recognizing implicit evidence from ranking it properly: Jev and Qwen3.5-9B both recover 90% of gold exchanges within the top 50 (versus 30% for the BERT-based decision model Laya), yet at rank 1 Jev reaches 85% while Qwen3.5-9B drops to 40%. On a dimension-proportional random sample of 60 MemoryAgentBench questions, Jetriever raises the four-dimension average from 44.3% (ReFind) to 55.4%, at a mean retrieval cost of USD 0.00834 per InMind query and USD 0.03103 per MAB query. Together, these results show that effective utility retrieval hinges on combining broad world knowledge, calibrated ranking probabilities, and affordable full-pool evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Twists and Turns: A Graph-Based Benchmark and Framework for Long-Term Memory in Exploration Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06414)**  `arXiv:2611.06414`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chongyuan Dai, Yaling Shen, Zirui Song, Junfeng Liao, Jinpeng Hu, Xiuying Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term problem-solving processes, such as scientific research and software development, proceed along an evolving trial-and-error trajectory toward a central goal. We term these \textit{exploration tasks}, which require models to remember not only facts but also how solutions are tested against intermediate outcomes and early attempts are continually revised or revived. However, existing long-term memory research focuses on factual recall and updates, overlooking the interconnected nature of exploration. We observe that exploration processes naturally form graphs, with branching approaches linked by revisions and reconsiderations. Motivated by this, we propose \textsc{LExMem}, a graph-guided benchmark designed to evaluate \underline{\textsc{Mem}}ory in \underline{\textsc{L}}ong-term \underline{\textsc{Ex}}ploration tasks. We model exploration processes as graphs and introduce an evolutionary mechanism to increase their structural complexity, yielding 224 dialogues and 2,018 question-answer instances derived from graph structures. Building on the same insight, we propose \textsc{LExGraph}, a graph-based memory framework that incrementally organizes dialogues into interconnected memory nodes and retrieves evidence through hybrid retrieval and neighbor expansion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that \textsc{LExMem} poses a significant challenge to existing retrieval- and memory-based systems, whereas \textsc{LExGraph} achieves superior performance and generalizes effectively to external benchmarks. Codes and datasets are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38366)**  `arXiv:2610.38366`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinxun Zhang, Yuxiang Wang, Shanshan Feng, Jun Zhang, Tieyun Qian, Binhang Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred growing interest in temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). Most existing methods rely on semantic retrieval augmented generation (RAG), which suffers from fundamental limitations in complex temporal reasoning. On the one hand, semantic-based RAG methods struggle to capture the multi-hop causal knowledge required for complex temporal reasoning. On the other hand, these methods introduce excessive redundant knowledge, trapping LLMs in spurious reasoning chains that potentially result in erroneous answers. To address these problems, we propose a Temporal Causal Chain-guided RAG (TCC-RAG) to retrieve a concise yet sufficient set of causal knowledge to support complex temporal reasoning. Specifically, we reformulate the temporal knowledge graph into a multi-view causal cue graph, with causal cues inducing connections that explicitly encode temporal evolution and causal dynamics. Based on the causal cue graph, we construct a query-specific temporal causal chain to guide the retrieval process, enabling the model to focus on a concise set of causally relevant knowledge. Finally, we introduce a causal evidence filter to refine the retrieved knowledge, ensuring its causal consistency with the underlying temporal logic of the query. Experiments show that TCC-RAG achieves substantial performance gains of 10.72% and 8.40% on two TKGQA benchmarks, and delivers a remarkable 62.97% improvement for multi-step temporal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Meta-Learning Preferences for Multilingual LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13315)**  `arXiv:2607.13315`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaying Lin, Seongho Son, Nam Phuong Tran, Long Tran-thanh, Ilija Bogunovic, Debmalya Mandal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unequal availability of human preference data across languages poses a significant challenge for aligning large language models in multilingual settings. To address the lack of sufficient data in low-resource language alignment, we propose a meta-learning framework for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback and Direct Preference Optimization. By leveraging preference data from other languages, our framework learns a transferable initialization that enables effective adaptation to a target language with minimal data. We provide theoretical guarantees for both the meta-reward modeling and meta-policy optimization settings, and empirically demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on multilingual benchmarks. In an extremely low-resource setting with only 100 target-language preference samples, our approach achieves up to $33.4\%$ win-rate improvements over baseline methods, and consistently outperforms baselines across multiple target languages and model scales. Our approaches retain these advantages across different combinations of meta-training languages and varying linguistic distances from the target languages.
  </details>

- **[EasyOPD: An Easy-to-use On-Policy Distillation Framework for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11012)**  `arXiv:2607.11012`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jie Sun, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Qiyong Zhong, Gengsheng Li, Zhepei Hong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional language-model distillation often relies on fixed teacher-generated data, which may not cover the states encountered by an evolving student policy. On-policy distillation (OPD) instead collects teacher or evaluator supervision on student-generated rollouts. However, existing OPD methods differ substantially in supervision form, tokenizer compatibility, teacher access, and supervision granularity, leading to fragmented implementations that are difficult to reproduce and extend. We present \textsc{EasyOPD}, an on-policy distillation framework built on verl, a distributed reinforcement-learning framework for large language models. \textsc{EasyOPD} separates user-side configuration, method-specific supervision logic, and verl-based execution. Its method modules connect to the shared backend through extension boundaries for loss construction, rollout metadata, reward processing, tokenizer alignment, and teacher-side computation. We instantiate representative methods for three OPD settings -- cross-tokenizer OPD, on-policy self-distillation, and step-wise OPD. Experiments on reasoning, code-generation, scientific-knowledge, and tool-use benchmarks show that these implementations can be executed through the same verl-based backend while retaining their method-specific objectives and task-dependent performance profiles. We release \textsc{EasyOPD} with runnable YAML configurations, documentation, and an installable demonstration package and video.
  </details>

- **[CRAM: Centroid-Routing and Adaptive MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02502)**  `arXiv:2606.02502`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jun-Tao Tang, Zhen-Hao Xie, Yu-Cheng Shi, Da-Wei Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) unify heterogeneous vision-language tasks under a shared generative framework via instruction tuning, yet real-world deployment demands continuous capability expansion, making Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) essential. Existing methods either update all tasks with a shared parameter set or allocate dedicated modules for each new task. Shared updates force heterogeneous tasks to compete, causing forgetting of learned capabilities. Conversely, isolated expansion prevents interference but severely limits parameter efficiency over long task streams. To address this dilemma, we propose CRAM (Centroid-Routing and Adaptive MoE). Specifically, by isolating task-specific patterns into independent modules, CRAM mitigates catastrophic forgetting across tasks. To further boost parameter efficiency, we utilize adaptive-rank instantiation to identify the capability gap between existing expert capability and new task demands, and dynamically allocate only the necessary parameters. To ensure stable reuse among tasks, centroid-guided routing recognizes and activates existing experts' capabilities, while an orthogonality penalty confines new updates to task-specific directions, preventing re-learning general capability. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate its superiority over existing methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[S-GRADES -- Studying Generalization of Student Response Assessments in Diverse Evaluative Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10233)**  `arXiv:2603.10233`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tasfia Seuti, Sagnik Ray Choudhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating student responses, from long essays to short factual answers, is a key challenge in educational NLP. Automated Essay Scoring (AES) focuses on holistic writing qualities such as coherence and argumentation, while Automatic Short Answer Grading (ASAG) emphasizes factual correctness and conceptual understanding. Despite their shared goal, these paradigms have progressed in isolation with fragmented datasets, inconsistent metrics, and separate communities. We introduce S-GRADES (Studying Generalization of Student Response Assessments in Diverse Evaluative Settings), a web-based benchmark that consolidates 14 diverse grading datasets under a unified interface with standardized access and reproducible evaluation protocols. The benchmark is fully open-source and designed for extensibility, enabling continuous integration of new datasets and evaluation settings. To demonstrate the utility of S-GRADES, we evaluate three state-of-the-art large language models across the benchmark using multiple reasoning strategies in prompting. We further examine the effects of exemplar selection and cross-dataset exemplar transfer. Our analyses illustrate how benchmark-driven evaluation reveals reliability and generalization gaps across essay and short-answer grading tasks, highlighting the importance of standardized, cross-paradigm assessment.
  </details>

- **[Challenging Quadratic Attention$\unicode{x2013}$A Holistic View on the Rise of Alternative Language Model Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05364)**  `arXiv:2510.05364`  `cs.CL`  
  _Alexander M. Fichtl, Jeremias Bohn, Josefin Kelber, Edoardo Mosca, Georg Groh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers have dominated sequence processing tasks for the past seven years$\unicode{x2013}$most notably language modeling. However, the inherent quadratic complexity of their attention mechanism remains a significant bottleneck as context length increases. We review and distill the recent efforts to overcome this bottleneck, including advances in (sub-quadratic) attention variants, recurrent neural networks, state space models, and hybrid architectures. We critically analyze approaches regarding compute and memory complexity, benchmark results, and fundamental limitations to assess whether the dominance of pure-attention transformers may soon be challenged, which we consider possible, particularly in domain-specific and edge-device applications.
  </details>

- **[ThinkSum: Probabilistic reasoning over sets using large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01293)**  `arXiv:2210.01293`  `cs.CL`  
  _Batu Ozturkler, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Zhen Wang, Nebojsa Jojic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have a substantial capacity for high-level analogical reasoning: reproducing patterns in linear text that occur in their training data (zero-shot evaluation) or in the provided context (few-shot in-context learning). However, recent studies show that even the more advanced LLMs fail in scenarios that require reasoning over multiple objects or facts and making sequences of logical deductions. We propose a two-stage probabilistic inference paradigm, ThinkSum, which reasons over sets of objects or facts in a structured manner. In the first stage (Think - retrieval of associations), a LLM is queried in parallel over a set of phrases extracted from the prompt or an auxiliary model call. In the second stage (Sum - probabilistic inference or reasoning), the results of these queries are aggregated to make the final prediction. We demonstrate the possibilities and advantages of ThinkSum on the BIG-bench suite of LLM evaluation tasks, achieving improvements over the state of the art using GPT-family models on thirteen difficult tasks, often with far smaller model variants. We also compare and contrast ThinkSum with other proposed modifications to direct prompting of LLMs, such as variants of chain-of-thought prompting. Our results suggest that because the probabilistic inference in ThinkSum is performed outside of calls to the LLM, ThinkSum is less sensitive to prompt design, yields more interpretable predictions, and can be flexibly combined with latent variable models to extract structured knowledge from LLMs. Overall, our proposed paradigm represents a promising approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[CoNav-UAV: Cooperative Dual-Altitude Aerial Navigation via Stackelberg Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01802)**  `arXiv:2608.01802`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Junru Song, Wenhao Zhang, Yang Yang, Xuekai Qiu, Feifei Wang, Weien Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial search missions often require an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) to locate a target from only a brief description of its appearance and surroundings, a task known as target-oriented aerial vision-and-language navigation (VLN). The task demands city-scale exploration and grounding as well as collision-free close-range approach, two interleaved processes that are difficult to reconcile within a single agent. Most existing methods adapt the ground VLN paradigm to a single UAV and rely on external assistance to compensate for its inefficiency. A recent attempt deploys two UAVs at complementary altitudes but still depends on privileged sensing and trains the two agents independently, precluding any mutual adaptation. We propose CoNav-UAV, which models the task as a Stackelberg game between a high-altitude leader and a low-altitude follower to capture their sequential decisions and asymmetric observations and utilities, with both agents using only onboard sensing. We introduce Iterative Stackelberg Learning, which tailors optimization to each agent's role: the leader's high-level vision-language reasoning builds on pretrained capabilities and is refined through memory-based in-context learning, while the follower's precise motion control is fine-tuned through DAgger-style expert distillation. Alternating the two updates lets each agent continually adapt to the other's evolving behavior. CoNav-UAV outperforms single- and dual-agent baselines across three high-fidelity urban scenes from the AerialVLN benchmark. It improves success rate by up to 30.8 points on the learning scene and by up to 9.0 points under cross-scene transfer, where the leader reuses its learned memory and the follower is adapted with 2.6-3.7 times less target-scene data. Extensive ablations confirm the contributions of key components and the benefit of mutual adaptation, with consistent gains across VLM backbones.
  </details>

- **[ARISE: Practical Self-Evolving Memory for Accurate and Cost-Efficient Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23588)**  `arXiv:2611.23588`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jiangyun Zhang, Zhou Tang, Kristen Surrao, Zhiyu Chen, Albert Guan, Tor Nitayanont, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise LLM agents operate in dynamic environments where foundation models evolve rapidly, workflows update frequently, and deployments need to satisfy accuracy and cost requirements. We propose ARISE (Agent Refinement through Iterative Self-Evaluation), a practical memory architecture in which the agent's memory evolves from its own failures without updating model weights, tools, or workflow logic. ARISE uses the model itself or a teacher model to attribute the failures and propose corrections. Then, ARISE keeps the corrections as retrievable micro memory, consolidates them into macro rules, and accepts a rule update only if held-out accuracy rises. Across 14 enterprise-workflow domains from SOP-Bench and $\tau^2$-bench using 5 models, ARISE-Teacher, which uses Claude Opus 4.6 for attribution and consolidation, gains 10.9 pp over the baseline on SOP-Bench and 5.8 pp on $\tau^2$-bench. ARISE-Teacher also outperforms GEPA by 4.5 pp on $\tau^2$-bench and 1.4 pp on SOP-Bench. With the deployment model as its own attributor, ARISE-Self still gains 10.0 pp on SOP-Bench. ARISE-Teacher uses 4.0% fewer model calls and 5.2% fewer output tokens at comparable per-task cost (-1.0%).
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Memory Inconsistency: Where Factual Conflicts Disappear in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22245)**  `arXiv:2611.22245`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Hejia Zhang, Zhongming Yu, Yimin Ding, Wei-Ting Chen, Hengjia Yu, Wentao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent delegation lets LLM systems distribute evidence collection across workers with separate contexts. This creates a consistency challenge: facts retrieved by workers may be missing from the evidence available to the lead for a joint conclusion. We introduce a benchmark and evidence-flow profiler for studying multi-agent memory inconsistency through controlled factual conflicts in Harvey LAB document-review tasks. Pooled across three models with comparable generation spending, requiring delegation increases retrieval of both conflicting claims from 50.2% to 68.7% relative to single-agent execution, yet conflict detection falls from 16.2% to 13.2%. Within this setting, workers report both claims for only 35.2% of retrieved pairs, and pooled matched comparisons show higher detection than single-agent execution when one worker reads both claims but lower detection when the claims are split across workers. Worker-to-lead handoffs are a central target for improving conflict detection in multi-agent LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Disagreement-Guided Agentic Data Curation for Multimodal Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14256)**  `arXiv:2607.14256`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Genglin Liu, Muye Zhang, Krishnamurthy Viswanathan, Nichole J. Hansen, Blaž Bratanič, Nathan L Clement, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Written safety policies in multimodal settings define categories whose visual boundaries span many contexts. We study disagreement as a signal for identifying useful synthetic boundary cases. Our pipeline generates policy-conditioned images, selects cases with target--rater or inter-rater disagreement, conditionally escalates unresolved cases, and retrieves the resulting rater-labeled examples as in-context demonstrations. On a failure-enriched multimodal safety benchmark, policy grounding reduces false negative rate (FNR) from 0.875 to 0.413 and raises F1 from 0.220 to 0.731; retrieval from 50 disagreement-selected examples further reduces FNR to 0.245, compared with 0.297 from a 350-example Regular pool. The selected memory also improves a different target-model family, with a smaller gain on a proprietary benchmark with a refined policy. In a blinded human study, experts disagree on 24% of disagreement-guided images and 17% of baseline-curated images. These findings support traceable, disagreement-guided curation for multimodal safety evaluation and example selection.
  </details>

- **[GALA: Gallery-Level Alignment for Real-Life Vision Decoding from EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24206)**  `arXiv:2611.24206`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Philippe Forest, Giulia Lioi, Mai Pham Quyen, Alix Lamouroux, Javen Shi, Adrien Merlini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural decoding of visual experience from electroencephalography (EEG) has achieved strong performance in intra-subject settings, but current methods fail to generalize across individuals: on the standard 200-way benchmark THINGS-EEG-2, top-1 accuracy drops from above 80% within subjects to about 32% for an unseen subject. Adapting a pre-trained encoder to new subjects and sessions typically requires labeled data, a bottleneck whose collection time severely limits practical device deployment. In a practical Brain-Computer Interface (BCI), however, the possible outcomes are known in advance and form a gallery of candidates, and the unlabeled EEG of the user accumulates as soon as the device is used. We propose a new setting, incremental transductive calibration, in which the decoder is calibrated regularly on all the unlabeled EEG received so far and on the known gallery, without any labels. This setting is closer to the conditions of a deployed BCI than the zero-shot and batch evaluations in current use. We evaluate it with a protocol in which queries arrive one at a time, with unknown and possibly unequal frequencies, and each query is decoded before it is used for calibration. In this setting, we introduce GALA : GAllery-Level Alignment, a label-free calibration method which registers the unlabeled EEG embeddings onto the gallery through shrinkage whitening, hubness correction and a Sinkhorn--Procrustes alignment with query frequencies estimated from the buffer, updated after every query. On THINGS-EEG-2, with a gallery of 200 images, it raises the top-1 accuracy on a new subject from 28% to 67% over 53 minutes of use. Across 60 evaluation settings that vary the gallery size, the query frequencies and the number of repetitions averaged per query, GALA is the most accurate label-free method in every setting, 6.6 points above the best competing method on average at the end of the stream.
  </details>

- **[Continual Tool-Space Extension Learning for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23810)**  `arXiv:2611.23810`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinbae Seo, Jungin Park, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jiyoung Lee, Kwanghoon Sohn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents largely rely on generic tools such as cropping, search, and code execution, leaving specialist vision models outside their tool space. However, bringing such a model into an already trained agent is not straightforward. The challenge is to learn each new tool efficiently while retaining the ability to use existing ones. This work proposes COTEX, continual tool-space extension learning, that adds new tools at efficient adapter-level cost. COTEX locates two sets of parameter directions; one triggers a tool call and thereby opens a multi-turn reasoning trajectory, while the other determines which tool is chosen. The adapter of a new tool is restricted to the calling directions that leave earlier tool choices unaffected by orthogonal constraint. We further introduce a protocol that converts specialist models' existing task-specific annotations into verified tool-use trajectories. Experiments on two representative agents and three specialist tools show gains on ten vision-language benchmarks, with at most a 0.3-point drop on existing tools.
  </details>

- **[GUARD: Stabilizing Recursive Self-Improvement with Regret Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23476)**  `arXiv:2611.23476`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Minghe Zhang, Xuetao Kang, Yifei Ming, Yanfei Chen, Han Yu, Yufan Zhuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous AI agents pair a frozen base model with an editable harness of prompts, skills, tool rules, and memory. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) adapts this harness from experience without weight updates, yet self-editing without external graders often backfires: reflection and scalar scores conflate what an edit newly broke with what remains unsolved, causing local regressions, skill forgetting, and memory bloat.
