We introduce GUARD (Grader-free Unsupervised Environment Design [UED] with Adaptive Rule Distillation), a closed-loop framework for reference-free continual harness evolution. Our key insight is to decompose each task into pre-execution self-checks and guide harness updates via directional regret. Each round grades trajectories against these checks, pinning mastered rules while prioritizing newly broken behaviors, and selects among parallel candidate edits by critic-free group-relative fitness. A Soft-HURT gate then promotes an edit only when gains outweigh regressions, scoping specialized fixes to prevent over-triggering and guaranteeing non-regression on its gated buffer. At test time, one frozen harness runs in a single pass. Across benchmarks on memory, skill evolution, continual task streams, and domain reasoning, GUARD consistently improves performance without catastrophic forgetting or memory pollution while substantially cutting deployment token usage.
  </details>

- **[CADFather: Autonomous CAD Reconstruction through Coordinated Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09127)**  `arXiv:2610.09127`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Nikita Gavrilov, Albert Garifullin, Oleg Pavlov, Soslan Kabisov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing an editable CAD model from a 3D shape remains a challenging engineering task. Existing methods can propose CAD operations, but no single source of proposals works equally well across different part geometries and stages of reconstruction. We introduce CADFather, an autonomous agentic system that coordinates complementary tools to recover parametric CAD programs from 3D meshes. A vision-language assistant inspects renders of the target and intermediate reconstructions, then decides which candidate CAD programs to extend, which tools to invoke, how many proposals to generate, and when to finish. Learned and algorithmic tools propose CAD operations, while numerical optimization refines the parameters of existing programs. Proposed or refined programs are executed and evaluated to provide feedback for subsequent decisions. The agent maintains alternative candidate programs for each target part and preserves the best valid result throughout reconstruction. CADFather uses pretrained generation and assistant models without additional training. We evaluate reconstruction quality and execution validity on the full DeepCAD, Fusion360, and MCB test sets, as well as on CADENA-Bench, CADBench, and BenchCAD. We additionally analyze computational cost and the trade-off between cost and reconstruction quality.
  </details>

- **[Bringing Back Rule Induction to Fluid Intelligence Research? An Initial Validation of the ARC-AGI Benchmark in Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11263)**  `arXiv:2607.11263`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jasmin Thelen, Oliver Wilhelm_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two competing perspectives on fluid intelligence (gf) measures propose that performance is primarily constrained either by working memory capacity or by the ability to induce novel relations. The first perspective is currently dominant in measurement, as evident from the use of a limited set of recurring rules, whereas the second perspective is reflected in many definitions but rarely present in measurement. The ARC-AGI benchmark predominantly requires rule induction and was proposed as a measure of gf for both humans and artificial systems. However, its psychometric properties have not yet been examined in human samples. We therefore investigated the psychometric characteristics and nomological network of ARC-AGI in a first study with 100 participants. A compilation of ARC-AGI items showed good psychometric properties and correlated substantially with figural fluid intelligence as measured by a figural reasoning test ($\rho$ = .63). Associations with figural originality were weak. These findings provide initial support for the validity of ARC-AGI as a measure of human fluid intelligence. Future research should include more rule induction tasks as well as additional multivariate covariates. This study is unusual by studying a task in humans that was initially designed for machines. We suggest systematically embedding AI benchmarks into the nomological network of human cognitive abilities to enable more systematic evaluation and interdisciplinary cooperation.
  </details>

- **[RoboBuilder: Agentic Robot Co-Design with Real-world Video Feedback in the Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23855)**  `arXiv:2611.23855`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Ghadi Nehme, Dahyun Nam, Akshay Govind Srinivasan, Faez Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can AI agents design robots that work in the real world? We introduce RoboBuilder, a benchmark in which coding agents co-design a robot's hardware and program from standard parts, motors and a custom 3D-printed part, and every design that passes in simulation is built and tested on a physical course. Simulation alone is not enough: under public part data, none of 15 initial passing designs crossed the real course, so an agent learns the simulator from measurements it requests and videos of real runs. Across four frontier coding agents, ten obstacles at three difficulty levels, 16,427 simulations of 12,774 distinct robots and 135 physical robots built in an estimated 240 hours, agents solve nearly all easy obstacles and invent mechanisms our search baselines do not reach, but falter at harder levels, when one robot must cross every obstacle, and in the real world, where 66% of simulated passes hold across all levels. Closing the loop helps: shown a video of its real failure, an agent redesigns the robot, sometimes correcting its simulator, and removes 72% of level-1 and level-2 failures. RoboBuilder measures progress toward agents that engineer real machines. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RFBench: A Simulator-Grounded Multimodal Benchmark for Passive RF Layout Design and Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23410)**  `arXiv:2611.23410`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiarui Cui, Boxun Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal language models can interpret diagrams and generate structured outputs, their capacity to connect passive radio-frequency (RF) design intent, layout geometry, and electromagnetic behavior remains largely untested. To address this gap, we introduce RFBench, a simulator-grounded dataset and benchmark for passive RF layout design and understanding. The dataset contains 10,000 two-port planar layouts simulated using ADS Momentum, with each layout represented by editable geometric primitives, a raster image, a 16x16 occupancy grid, complex S-parameters at 201 frequency points, a structural category, and an RF response label. Building on these aligned representations, we define seven tasks spanning specification-conditioned layout generation, representation translation, response prediction, simulator-based validation, design diagnosis and improvement, structural understanding, and response classification. Extensive baselines across seven multimodal language models reveal systematic weaknesses in generating structurally valid layouts and predicting electromagnetically faithful responses. We show that RF-specific fine-tuning increases T1a structural validity from 0.0% to 83.2% and the T3 primitive-input within-2-dB rate from 17.7% to 62.8%, demonstrating the value of domain adaptation while leaving considerable room for further progress. RFBench establishes a common testbed for evaluating multimodal RF capabilities and guiding the development of models that connect design intent and layout representations with physical behavior.
  </details>

- **[CADMAGEDDON: High-Fidelity CAD Reconstruction with Local Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22563)**  `arXiv:2611.22563`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Oleg Pavlov, Nikita Gavrilov, Soslan Kabisov, Gennadii Savrasov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Anton Konushin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CAD practitioners often work with proprietary parts that they are unwilling to share with external services. For individuals and small companies, bringing this work in-house also requires computational resources they can afford. We introduce CADMAGEDDON, an open-source agent that reconstructs editable CAD from meshes and can run locally on single-GPU systems and current-generation laptops. The method decomposes this complex task into a chain of simple visual decisions about what to construct and what to correct. Geometric tools support precise construction, allowing the agent to focus on visual reasoning. Using the mid-sized Qwen3.8-27B model, CADMAGEDDON outperforms prior approaches, including systems based on frontier models, in reconstruction quality on all the main benchmarks. CADMAGEDDON integrates with FreeCAD, bringing its reconstructions into practitioners' existing CAD workflows. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Thinking Fast, Anywhere: One System-1 for the Decision Points of Any Agent Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23751)**  `arXiv:2611.23751`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Rui Li, Liyang He, Zheng Zhang, Zhenya Huang, Qi Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous LLM agents incur substantial cost and latency by invoking a large model (System-2) at every step, even when many intermediate actions choose among a small set of known candidates. Existing dual-system agents offload such decisions to a lightweight System-1 model, but rely on workflow-specific hooks authored per environment. We present AnyS1, a framework that turns human-authored procedural skills, whose instructions naturally specify where choices arise and which options apply, into a shared, domain-general source of decision points. Offline, AnyS1 mines grounded enumerable decisions across over 1,000 public skills, gathers execution-grounded trajectory states inside isolated containers to construct Any-DP, and trains a single calibrated 4B pointer System-1 with label smoothing. At run time, compiled workflows resolve routine decisions in one forward pass and fall back to System-2 when confidence falls below a calibrated threshold. Across three unseen benchmarks and distinct System-2 model families, AnyS1 preserves task reliability while cutting episode cost by 75-91% and model-call time by 63-92% relative to an uncompiled System-2 agent, and maintains performance under zero-shot threshold transfer.
  </details>

- **[When Does Learning to Stop Help? Configuring Risk-Controlled Early Exit for Reasoning-Model Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30852)**  `arXiv:2606.30852`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhe Dong, Fang Qin, Manish Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early exit lowers the cost of serving a reasoning model by stopping its reasoning once a provisional answer looks final. An operator must choose between a learned stopping rule and one static token budget, and savings against full reasoning do not settle the choice. We present a procedure that decides on calibration data from the workload. It certifies both families with Learn-then-Test to one bound on the fraction of all questions that full reasoning answers correctly but early exit does not, prices provisional answers as the serving stack does, and deploys the cheapest certified candidate. Across four models and five benchmarks neither family is better throughout. At a 5% risk target the learned rule saves 10.4-17.0 percentage points more tokens than the static budget on free-form mathematics with Qwen3, with accuracy 2.4-2.9 points below full reasoning, and less on MMLU-Pro on average. Which family costs less also changes with the risk target, in 8 of 15 settings, and with the price of a provisional answer. Across three prices, committing to one family in advance gives up as much as 27.4 points on some workload, whereas selecting on calibration data falls at most 11.6 points short of the better family, on a task with only 99 calibration questions. In a vLLM engine with Qwen3-8B, mean response time fell in proportion to generated tokens. Policies served on a task other than their calibration task exceeded the risk target in 18 of 44 task pairs; recalibration on the new task restored risk control.
  </details>

- **[SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23483)**  `arXiv:2611.23483`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Xi Ding, Jiawei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.
  </details>

- **[BOS Summary: The First Token Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23508)**  `arXiv:2611.23508`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Deng Pan, Arun Ross, Dongxiao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) and language models (LLMs) often exhibit attention sinks, whereby particular positions, such as the beginning-of-sequence (BOS) token, receive a disproportionate share of attention. Under standard causal masking, however, the BOS query attends only to itself, so the representation that later tokens repeatedly access carries no information about the input. We introduce BOS Summary, a training-free inference-time method that repurposes this persistent pathway to carry input context. At a single transformer layer during prefill, BOS Summary relaxes the causal constraint for the BOS query row alone, letting BOS aggregate all visual and textual input tokens into a self-derived context representation while every other token retains standard causal attention. The enriched representation then remains accessible to the model throughout decoding, with no additional parameters, auxiliary modules, or decoding-time overhead. Across multiple VLM and LLM families and eight multimodal and text-only benchmarks, BOS Summary improves over direct and Chain-of-Thought prompting and outperforms recent sink-intervention methods on multi-step reasoning tasks. A mechanistic analysis shows that making BOS input-specific also weakens its sink dominance and redistributes attention toward content-bearing prompt tokens, indicating that changing what a sink carries reshapes how later layers allocate attention.
  </details>

- **[Measuring and Attributing Evidence Sensitivity in Medical Vision-Language Models via Symmetric Counterfactual Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13006)**  `arXiv:2611.13006`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Bhaskar Gurram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision-language models (VLMs) asked closed clinical questions ("Is there a polyp?") can score well by relying on the question rather than the image, and current hallucination benchmarks cannot separate true visual discrimination from editing shortcuts or shifts in the answer threshold. We introduce a Counterfactual Evidence Framework that pairs each lesion erasure with a shape- and tool-matched control edit elsewhere in the image, adds same-site ring controls, restored and partial erasures, degraded views and natural negatives, tests on held-out inpainting tools, separates discrimination (the Evidence Sensitivity Gap, ESG, and AUROC) from the answer threshold, and checks the labels with blinded physician readers and hidden-state probes. Across 507 main-test cases (1,115 views), nine frontier VLMs claim 41.1% to 61.6% of erased lesions (ESG 0.269 to 0.470), whereas MedScope-4B, a Qwen3.5-4B agent trained on automatically built counterfactual trajectories, claims 9.6% (ESG 0.758; p <= 0.001 in all 27 primary comparisons). Physician readers support automated erasure in colonoscopy, where frontier models still claim 45% of erased lesions against 1% for MedScope-4B, but call a finding present on 54.7% of pooled erased reads across all sources, which bounds what unverified erasure can certify. Across 12 pre-registered training or system arms and 270 decision policies screened under a rule fixed in advance, every apparent utility gain comes from shifting the threshold rather than improving held-out discrimination, and probes point to perception as the remaining limit. We also report a 0/48 pneumothorax failure on an external cohort; retraining with pneumothorax patients in every stage finds 53% of pneumothoraxes on new patients without changing the main-test results.
  </details>

- **[MARBLE: An Agentic Benchmark for Spatial Reasoning and Visual Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22992)**  `arXiv:2506.22992`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yulun Jiang, Naël Ouerghemi, Yekun Chai, Maria Brbić, Michael Moor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A central challenge for multimodal language models (MLLMs) in real-world tasks is to abstract visual observations into structured spatial states and reason over how those states change under actions over long horizons. Existing multimodal benchmarks often focus on static question answering, where answers can be directly extracted from images, or assume symbolic states are available and bypass the visual-to-spatial abstraction process. We present MARBLE, a diagnostic benchmark for spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning across three agent environments: CUBE, MAZE, and BLOX. MARBLE spans 2D and 3D spatial puzzles under geometric constraints, dynamic layouts, and spatial transformations, with difficulty systematically controlled along two axes: observation format and planning horizon. Extensive evaluations show that frontier MLLMs struggle on MARBLE, with the strongest model GPT-5.4 achieving only a 7.3% average success rate across the three environments in the hardest setting. Pairwise ablations on the open-weight models identify visual-to-spatial abstraction as the major bottleneck: when models must infer spatial states from images instead of receiving human-annotated states, success drops from 50.7% to 3.7% for Gemma-4-31B and from 87.9% to 6.5% for Kimi-K2.6. The results indicate that models often fail to construct precise spatial states from visual observations before planning, a task that is straightforward for humans. Overall, MARBLE reveals the gap in reliable visual-to-spatial abstraction for frontier MLLMs and provides a controlled testbed for measuring progress in spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning.
  </details>

- **[NaviGATE: Noise-Aware Dual-View Generation for Multimodal Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24552)**  `arXiv:2611.24552`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Peng, Yongxue Shan, Yongfu Zha, Cui Miao, Xiaodong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal knowledge graph reasoning (MKGR) aims to infer missing facts by jointly exploiting relational structures, textual descriptions, and visual evidence. Existing embedding-based methods effectively capture graph topology, but their fixed multimodal representations are less effective for query-specific fine-grained semantic reasoning, while generation-based methods are vulnerable to noisy retrieval and may produce invalid entities. To address these challenges, we propose NaviGATE, a noise-aware generative framework for MKGR. NaviGATE first aligns graph-derived structural representations with textual semantics, then performs structure-aware internal knowledge activation by fine-tuning the multimodal language model with multimodal entity summaries and injected structural tokens. In the noise-aware dual-view instruction tuning stage, NaviGATE jointly optimizes retrieval-free and retrieval-aware views: reliable retrieved candidates are reinforced through contrastive logit sharpening, while misleading candidates are suppressed through parametric consistency distillation. In inference, structure-constrained decoding restricts generation to valid entities in the target knowledge graph. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that NaviGATE consistently outperforms strong unimodal, multimodal, and generation-based baselines, and remains robust under noisy retrieval scenarios.
  </details>

- **[ABSClaw: From Invented Worlds to Reality-Grounded Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24517)**  `arXiv:2611.24517`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shaobin He, Yan Gao, Quan Zheng, Zhenyang Lin, Zhen Liu, Jiyan He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based artificial societies exhibit rich behavior but often lack scientific grounding: personas and environments are assumed, interaction networks are synthetic, and simulators are generated as free-form code, making results difficult to tie to real populations and sensitive to the backbone model. We argue that simulated worlds should be sampled from observed data, while simulation logic should be implemented by composing operations from a shared, task-independent vocabulary. In this paper, we introduce ABSClaw, a zero-code agent that compiles natural-language requests into executable, reality-grounded social simulations. ABSClaw grounds who acts, where they act, and with whom they interact in survey and census data, geospatial sources, and real networks. It expresses state transitions through 26 atomic operations. To support an open and extensible ecosystem, ABSClaw further allows capabilities to be added as lifecycle-scoped skills without modifying its core. Furthermore, we propose ABSBench, a benchmark of 193 real-world social-simulation tasks. On ABSBench, ABSClaw achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated social simulators. Three real-world case studies show that grounding personas, environments, and interaction networks each improves agreement with observed outcomes. With one runtime skill, ABSClaw executes 13 population-scale simulations, collectively involving 25 million agents. Together, these results demonstrate ABSClaw's real-world fidelity, extensibility, and scalability.
  </details>

- **[Risk-Triggered Search: Behavior-Preserving Safe Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24409)**  `arXiv:2611.24409`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Deng, Bohao Lan, Kunpeng Li, Mingkai Yuan, Weihang Pan, Binbin Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained visual world models generate multi-step action plans from current and goal observations, yet terminal-proximity objectives fail to prevent forbidden-zone traversal or rigid-body collision sliding. Purely visual interfaces may lack analytical dynamics, structured states, or explicit collision geometry, requiring internal prediction of action-conditioned process risk. We propose Risk-Triggered Search (RTS) for frozen Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models. Rather than relying on static state features that cannot distinguish candidate plans sharing an observation, RTS unrolls action-conditioned future latent prefixes in the frozen world model and trains a lightweight risk head with process-violation supervision. To intervene selectively while preserving safe nominal behavior, RTS uses the same predicted prefix-risk criterion as both the search trigger gate and hard feasibility gate: safe nominal plans execute directly, whereas alarms trigger local Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) search to minimize nominal deviation within the feasible set. To prevent isotropic Euclidean minimal modification from shrinking obstacle-directed actions into zero-amplitude stalling, we introduce an amplitude-preserving metric $d_{\mathrm{AP}}$ that retains tangential progression while maintaining a unique zero at the nominal plan. Under unified gating and this unique-zero condition, bypassing safe-state search yields closed-loop trajectories strictly identical to every-step same-kernel search. Evaluated under a process-safety benchmark and Safe Arrival Rate (SAR) metric across five tasks, RTS improves SAR over Direct by 12.67--41.67 percentage points on navigation and articulated control and +2.08/+4.17 pp on Cube/PushT boundary cohorts, maintaining 100% action identity with every-step search while cutting controller time by 17.71%--70.41% across 1,968 paired trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Context Handoff with On-Demand Evidence for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24357)**  `arXiv:2611.24357`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guolong Zheng, Chen Hou, Xu Yang, Jianshan Zhang, Tao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional long-horizon LLM agents often hand off context through full-history prompting, retaining all previous action--observation interactions. As execution proceeds, this history keeps growing, increasing inference cost and potentially degrading accuracy. Existing methods control this growth by preserving compact conclusions while removing earlier observations, but doing so can discard evidence that later becomes relevant. We introduce Semantic Context Handoff (SCH), whose central abstraction is state--evidence separation: a compact semantic state carries the agent's current interpretation, while supporting action--observation evidence remains separately addressable and can re-enter context when later execution makes it relevant, without separate summarization or retrieval-model calls.Empirically, SCH improves long-horizon agent performance while substantially reducing context cost. In a controlled 150-task SWE-bench study, SCH's evidence recovery improves Pass@1 from $80.0\%$ to $86.9\%$ at essentially unchanged token use and execution time. In the complete-system comparison, SCH achieves 86.9% Pass@1 while using 43% fewer tokens and 41% less wall-clock time than the strongest baseline by task performance. Across models and benchmarks, SCH consistently reduces token use relative to full-history prompting while remaining competitive with or improving upon the evaluated baselines. Trajectory analysis confirms that SCH recovers evidence from substantially earlier parts of the interaction history rather than merely preserving a recent context window.
  </details>

- **[When Figures Disagree with Text: Evaluating Autonomous Visual Evidence Verification in Scientific Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24353)**  `arXiv:2611.24353`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Jiajun Zha, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Huihao Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figures carry evidence for the claims that cite them, so reviewing a paper means checking that a figure agrees with the text, and not only describing what the figure shows. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Deciding what to verify is thus a large part of the difficulty, and the selection rule we tested does not resolve it. Multimodal document understanding should be evaluated on whether a model can propose what to verify, not only answer questions about a figure it is handed.
  </details>

- **[KnowledgeClaw: Towards Agentic Knowledge Engineering via Omnimodal Hypergraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24294)**  `arXiv:2611.24294`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Luo, Jiapu Wang, Linhao Luo, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge engineering (KE) turns raw files into a reasoning-ready knowledge base, a task now within reach of LLM agents. KE has progressed from chunk and graph retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to memory agents, agentic KE and multimodal KE. However, a live knowledge base needs omnimodality, a closed-loop lifecycle and multi-granular application at once, yet existing systems meet at most two and no benchmark measures all three. We formalize Omnimodal Knowledge Engineering (OmniKE) and release OmniKE-Bench, six tasks with 1,829 model-free items over 3,960 real files in five modalities. We further propose KnowledgeClaw, an agentic system that builds an omnimodal typed hypergraph, keeps it current with a closed-loop lifecycle agent and routes each query to the granularity it needs. Against 30 baselines, it ranks first on all six tasks with an overall OKE of 55.9, +14.6 over the strongest baseline. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Coordinate: LLM-Guided Workflow Evolution for Token-Efficient Red Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24221)**  `arXiv:2611.24221`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Xing, Wenyu Dai, Xiaoliang Gong, Asoke K Nandi, Anthony Cohn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated red teaming is essential for identifying safety failures in large language models (LLMs), but its practical scalability depends on the resources required to discover those failures. Multi-agent systems can combine complementary capabilities, yet repeated planning, redundant exchanges, and ineffective information routing can make their coordination expensive. Moreover, a target model's response indicates the outcome of a test without identifying which collaboration decision should change. We present Learning to Coordinate (L2C), an LLM-guided framework that evolves an executable multi-agent workflow for each red-teaming case. L2C represents agent responsibilities, execution dependencies, and input bindings in a versioned graph, and associates each graph with records of the information actually delivered and produced. A controller trained through supervised fine-tuning integrates these records with target responses, evaluator verdicts, and remaining budget to retain the workflow, refine instructions, restructure collaboration, or lock further editing. This design connects feedback to explicit changes in subsequent execution while allowing a useful organization to continue without repeated controller intervention. The evaluation protocol covers HarmBench with three execution backbones and two target models, together with component ablations, dataset transfer, and evaluator checks. Across the six executor-target configurations, L2C improves ASR over EvoSynth by 1.4 to 4.5 percentage points while reducing mean online tokens by 35.6% to 37.3%. L2C connects adaptive collaboration to the resource requirements of automated security evaluation.
  </details>

- **[3D GameDev Arena: Benchmarking Coding Agents That Plan, Build, and Playtest extreme Long-Horizon Browser Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24073)**  `arXiv:2611.24073`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bizhe Bai, Jiliang Jian, Yuqing Yan, Tao Chen, Ying Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents now resolve repository issues and generate compact playable games, but whether they can build a large 3D game from scratch remains open. Such games tightly couple mechanics, scenes, assets, and interaction logic, and their quality rests on player enjoyment, which existing benchmarks rarely assess. Thus, we introduce 3D GameDev Arena, a first extreme long-horizon benchmark of 108 from-scratch game-development tasks across 14 genres. Given only a text specification and UI/UX reference images, a fully automated plan--implement--test--repair pipeline builds each task into a self-contained browser game. Runs average $\sim$1,000 LLM calls ; the longest uses 14,407 calls, 2.13B tokens, and 608 assets. Evaluation spans (1) objective code-level functional acceptance against the game specification, (2) agent-based QA and playtesting of 3D presentation and playability, and (3) blind pairwise Bradley-Terry human preference in a public arena. The preliminary findings indicate that long-horizon 3D game development remains far from solved, and code-level correctness alone does not dictate player preference. The public arena is hosted online.
  </details>

- **[Space-X: Self-Evolving Spatial Agents Weaving Clues into Evidence Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24004)**  `arXiv:2611.24004`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zijian Zhang, Wenqing Tian, Qiang Liu, Liang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning connects visual observations, geometric estimates, and linguistic decisions into a coherent derivation. Yet tool-augmented agents often acquire evidence without clear targets, combine outputs under inconsistent object or reference-frame semantics, and misjudge evidence sufficiency. Thus, more tools or calls do not reliably improve reasoning. We introduce Space-X, a spatial-reasoning framework that self-evolves toward complete, continuous evidence-chain construction. An Execution Agent acquires, associates, and judges evidence per problem using role-specific memory. A Control Agent diagnoses evidence-chain breaks from execution traces and jointly revises tools, interfaces, skills, memory organization, and decision rules. This loop co-evolves capability and evidence use: evidence needs guide capability development; usable results guide actions. Across multiple benchmarks, Space-X yields gains with four base models. Relative to baselines, GPT-6-sol improves answer accuracy on ViewSpatial and MMSI by 50.39% and 23.07%, respectively; Gemini-3.8-Flash gains 47.50% and 25.22% on these benchmarks. This provides a unified route from fragmented spatial clues to traceable, derivable answers.
  </details>

- **[MLE Agents: Autonomous ML Engineering in Large-scale Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23910)**  `arXiv:2611.23910`  `cs.AI`  
  _Matthew Li, Albert Liang, Dilip Raj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The typical workflow for improving large-scale ranking and recommendation models is slow and tedious: an engineer forms a hypothesis, implements it as a code change, trains a model, and evaluates it both offline and online, often relying on their own intuition and experience along the way. Autonomous LLM agents promise to speed this up by running the loop on their own, but it is still unclear whether they can do this in industry-scale systems rather than on benchmarks or small proxy tasks. We build human-on-the-loop machine learning engineer (MLE) agents that propose, implement, train, and evaluate model changes on large-scale search rankers. Engineers retain control over objective design, search direction, and final-model review. We identify three obstacles that make this industry setting challenging: feedback takes hours per experiment, offline metrics do not always predict online impact, and improvements saturate after the early wins. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Its best model increases search volume by 0.23\% in an A/B test of large scale. Several improvements were architectural changes the team had never tried, drawn from the broader industry and literature, suggesting that MLE agents can explore regions of the design space that human intuition overlooks. We find that the design of the harness around the model matters: intuition suggests that a stronger underlying model is enough, but in our experience, the design of the loop around it is just as critical. More broadly, our work shows that autonomous agents can do real machine-learning engineering in industry-scale systems.
  </details>

- **[EVA-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement of Video Evaluators for Judging Temporal Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23890)**  `arXiv:2611.23890`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Yuan, Ruizhou Ding, Shaoheng Fang, Stanley H. Chan, Zhuo Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video evaluators play an important role in video generation, yet they miss motion defects, limiting the quality of generated videos. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) has made strong progress in mathematics and coding, where explicit verifiers provide reliable feedback on a single goal. Video evaluation poses two difficulties for RSI: it has no verifier, and it must judge many dimensions at once, such as physics, smoothness and dynamics, where a gain on one can cost another. We introduce EVA-RSI, a multi-agent framework for exploring RSI under these two constraints. We build a fixed proxy exam with construction-defined targets by applying controlled changes to videos, such as temporal reversal and frame dropping. These edits supply supervision without human annotation. A small set of human-labeled real videos anchors the exam to human judgment. Each dimension has an LLM agent that proposes improvements. A leader agent coordinates the proposals, and a Nash bargaining rule selects updates that balance progress across dimensions. Humans design the exam, review candidate changes and revise the curriculum between stages. Starting from Qwen3-VL-8B, EVA-RSI improves motion evaluation, with the largest gain on smoothness, and the gains carry over to defect types never seen during the run. On the dynamics subsets of public benchmarks, our evaluator achieves the highest observed scores among 21 tested models. As an RL reward, it increases generated motion and earns human preference. A data-selection pipeline incorporating it also increases motion.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-Based Credit Assignment for Search-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23824)**  `arXiv:2611.23824`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wen Wang, Jindong Wang, Jiahua Bao, Tu Yongsiqi, Yihao Liu, Junwei He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from terminal answer rewards enables search-augmented language models to interleave reasoning and retrieval without step-level annotations, yet outcome supervision assigns identical credit to every search in a trajectory regardless of whether it retrieved useful information. This limitation is especially severe when all sampled rollouts for a question fail, collapsing group-centered outcome advantages to zero even when trajectories gather markedly different evidence. We present Evidence-based Credit Assignment (ECA), which augments terminal outcome rewards with a training-only auxiliary signal measuring how retrieved documents improve the likelihood of a reference answer. Rather than scoring the full dialogue prefix, ECA queries a frozen snapshot of the current policy under an evidence-only prompt containing solely the question and the deduplicated documents observed so far, isolating the utility of retrieved passages from surface variations in generated thoughts or search queries. Within this framework, we compare two return formulations, ECA-Step (which accumulates step-clipped potential increments) and ECA-Horizon (which preserves the telescoping horizon gain in potential), paired with cross-fold group normalization, novelty gating, and search-local token assignment. Across seven Search QA benchmarks, ECA-Horizon improves strict macro exact match over an outcome-only GRPO baseline by 1.03 and 4.80 percentage points on Qwen2.5-3B- and 7B-Instruct, respectively, outperforming both RLOO and GiGPO. Zero-shot evaluation on MultiHop-RAG further demonstrates strong transfer to multi-hop inference queries.
  </details>

- **[Metrics-in-the-Loop: Complexity Feedback Reshapes Agent-Written Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23817)**  `arXiv:2611.23817`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nicolas Chapados, Jean-François Gagné, Nouha Dziri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents now write whole codebases in long autonomous runs, guided mainly by whether their tests pass. Yet passing tests alone does not reveal accumulating complexity or gaps in test coverage, which then persist as agents extend their own code. We introduce metrics-in-the-loop, an inference-time intervention that gives agents in-flight feedback: cheap, automatically computed code metrics injected into the agent's context during the trajectory. After each code-changing agent turn, we insert short feedback messages naming changed functions whose cyclomatic complexity exceeds a limit (complexity feedback) or changed lines that are skipped by tests (coverage feedback), giving the agent the opportunity to refactor code or strengthen tests before continuing. We run extensive experiments and ablations comparing several interventions across three long-horizon coding benchmarks and four strong models. We find that complexity feedback significantly reduces erosion, a measure of complexity concentration of code, without affecting held-out correctness of the code. Broadly, our experiments investigate a practical form of agent "re-railing", where timely, actionable measurements redirect agents before weaknesses become the foundation they keep building on. As agents scale to codebases with millions of lines, such interventions become critical to sustaining effective development and long-term code maintainability.
  </details>

- **[HealthCLBench:Evaluating Context Learning in Health Conversations through Controlled Case Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22362)**  `arXiv:2611.22362`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ting Yang, Zijing Hong, Chengjie Wang, Peng Wei, Chunxiao Guo, Yun Yue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex health conversations require answers grounded in individual information and currently valid evidence. We evaluate health context learning: acquiring, integrating, and applying relevant context during inference with fixed model parameters to answer health questions and respond appropriately to changes in facts, evidence, or task goals. To our knowledge, Chinese-language medical benchmarks have yet to systematically combine explicit contextual difficulties, controlled case variations, and member-specific medical criteria for open-ended answers. We introduce HealthCLBench, comprising 328 controlled clusters, 1,103 cases, and 4,931 rubric criteria across medical consultation and everyday health management. Members share backgrounds while evidence, patient states, or task goals change; each has its own medical scoring requirements. A 70-person medical team annotates and reviews the simulated cases and their rubrics. Human review of 700 full-context responses from the original evaluation yields 90.5% pooled agreement with the original automated judgments at the rubric-item level. We report full-context and query-only results for seven general-purpose models and Ant Afu. Across the eight systems, scores with full context are 11.83 to 31.76 case points and 10.87 to 38.97 geometric cluster points higher than those with the current question alone. GPT-6 Astra leads both full-context metrics at 75.03 and 60.42, followed by Claude Opus 5.5 and Ant Afu. Cross-condition differences describe score changes under the reported evaluation settings. Response comparisons reveal failures in checking historical information, expressing uncertainty when warranted, and ensuring recommendations respect required conditions. The resource supports model comparison and targeted follow-up evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Regularizing Treatment Leakage for LLM-based Uplift Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20794)**  `arXiv:2611.20794`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bojing Feng, Jinqiu Deng, Zihan Hong, Yuan Xue, Wenqi Xiang, Shuhan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) provide semantic features for uplift estimation, but treatment-revealing text can induce prediction shortcuts that do not transfer to deployment. We propose LLM-based Uplift Leakage Regularization (LULR) to address this risk. With the LLM encoder frozen, LULR penalizes dependence between the causal head's trainable shared representation and treatment while retaining separate outcome heads. The penalty uses a variational approximation to the contrastive log-ratio upper bound (CLUB) of mutual information, targeting treatment dependence rather than generic parameter shrinkage. Controlled proxy experiments provide exploratory evidence of improved ranking scores under injected treatment information relative to no regularization and the tested weight-decay setting. Structured-only benchmarks show modest, dataset-dependent effects. On an industrial pricing randomized controlled trial, mean offline lift@0.3 is 0.258 for LULR, compared with 0.106 for the same-backbone baseline. A separate one-week online A/B test of the complete deployed pricing policy records 5.45% higher sales volume and 1.91% higher gross merchandise value than the control arm. These short-term policy-level gains do not isolate the regularizer's effect.
  </details>

- **[Agentic On-Policy Distillation Implicitly Reshapes LLMs' ReAct Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19244)**  `arXiv:2611.19244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Deyang Kong, Zhaoyi Li, Sijian Ren, Ying Wei, Defu Lian, Shikun Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers specialized capabilities to language-model agents, but its cross-domain effects on interaction remain unclear. We investigate how OPD reshapes ReAct interaction patterns across mathematics, ALFWorld, and $\tau^2$-bench, focusing on reasoning, actions, and observation acquisition. General-reasoning supervision reduces mathematical tool attempts from 70.4% to 14.4% while increasing accuracy from 16.7% to 38.0%; code-interpreter supervision improves household actions and retail queries. In contrast, ALFWorld supervision reduces $\tau^2$-bench accuracy from 41.5% to 6.6%, accompanied by frequent tool calls that violate user-information dependencies. To address negative transfer using only source-domain training trajectories, we explore selective OPD guided by teacher preference changes before and after domain-specific RL. Retaining the top 95% of positions raises $\tau^2$ accuracy from 11.5% to 29.9% with a source-learning trade-off.
  </details>

- **[GenPFN: An In-Context Tabular Foundation Model Approach for Augmenting Small Datasets in Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14804)**  `arXiv:2611.14804`  `cs.AI`  
  _Noah J. Bagazinski, Cyril Picard, Faez Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Costly simulations and experiments limit the data available for engineering design. This paper proposes GenPFN, a training-free candidate generator guided by a pretrained tabular Prior-Data Fitted Network (TabPFN) classifier with fixed weights. The context contains evaluated designs labeled desired (Class 1) or undesired (Class 0). GenPFN interpolates between desired designs and refines candidates using gated classifier gradients and Gaussian noise. Each candidate is evaluated using the problem's constraints and objective functions. Evaluated desired designs and their objective values augment the context for regression training.Evaluation spans fourteen selected problems, including a two-parameter benchmark and a 44-parameter hull problem. In paired comparisons, GenPFN with TabPFN v1 achieves higher class satisfaction and lower realism distance than the tested adversarial, variational-autoencoder and diffusion generators. The variational-autoencoder generator provides greater coverage and diversity. Relative to the synthetic minority over-sampling technique (SMOTE), GenPFN provides lower coverage distance and greater diversity. Among observed paired regression fits, augmentation reduces estimated AutoGluon validation mean squared error by 62.1% relative to context-only training. A hull-stern case study yields 99.3% propeller-compatible candidates versus 1.4% among randomly sampled context designs. Generation followed by evaluation produces sample sets that augment limited datasets and support training more accurate predictive models.
  </details>

- **[Learning When to Think: Dual-Reference Offline Optimization for Adaptive VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09154)**  `arXiv:2611.09154`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongbin Lin, Sizhe Zou, Juangui Xu, Xinyue Xu, Zhuoli Ouyang, Stanislav Kolchin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning traces improve vision-language models on complex multimodal tasks, but forcing every input to follow a long reasoning process leads to overthinking and unnecessary inference cost. We study adaptive fast/slow thinking for VLMs, where one model answers directly for simple inputs and reasons step by step when needed. Fast answers and slow traces often come from distinct behavior policies, making a single-reference offline objective misweight one mode. We propose DUAL-REFERENCE BOLT (DR-BOLT), which distinguishes the desired fast/slow mixture reference from the sampling distribution and corrects their mismatch in reward-weighted training. Fast/slow partition competition explains routing in the optimal target; an efficient source-wise correction approximates the exact objective when response formats are well separated. Matched Qwen3-VL comparisons use identical candidate banks to isolate the objective from slow-teacher quality. DR-BOLT improves score-length trade-offs over the evaluated offline and online baselines on ScienceQA and three held-out transfer benchmarks, with a 24.1% cross-benchmark slow ratio and a 2.90-fold end-to-end training speedup over reproduced GRPO. These results support adaptive reasoning in the studied two-reference setting, while broader robustness remains open. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DuoCook: An Overcooked-Inspired Benchmark for Visual Planning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31511)**  `arXiv:2610.31511`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyi Jiao, Heng Zhang, Drishti Regmi, Xiaokang Ye, Lianhui Qin, Peter Yichen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coordinating two players in a cooking task requires a vision-language agent to track changing scene states, sequence recipe steps, and allocate shared resources under a time budget. We introduce an Overcooked-inspired benchmark for evaluating these capabilities in Unreal Engine environments constructed using coding agents. A single vision-language model controls both players from top-down visual observations \rev{through separate per-player decision calls}, selecting high-level actions \rev{and image-labeled target cells} while the engine executes navigation. The benchmark contains 20 maps in five categories. These categories support evaluation of \rev{two-player} coordination under equipment contention, changing terrain, and handoff dependencies. A prop legend and pot ingredient-count indicators provide explicit visual cues, while grid-based destinations support spatial grounding\rev{; text gives no station coordinates, so target locations come mainly from the image}. The evaluation compares \rev{five VLMs and a privileged scripted reference policy on all 20 maps}, measuring task score, order completion, delivery precision, \rev{aim accuracy}, and model API expenditure. \rev{Two paired ablations on all 20 maps toggle a shared self-authored notebook and reduce the textual prompt from about 3,600 to about 440 words while keeping the images fixed.} \rev{The strongest model completes 35\% of orders and exceeds the scripted reference, which has ground-truth station access; the notebook's effect is mixed in sign and not statistically reliable, and reducing the prompt text lowers the score of two models while leaving the other three nearly unchanged.} Together, the environment design and evaluation protocol provide a framework for investigating how visual state tracking and task scheduling contribute to coordinated cooking behavior.
  </details>

- **[DataPraxis: Scaling Analytics Tasks via Verified Workspace Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26924)**  `arXiv:2610.26924`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nan Jia, Wenxuan Shi, Jingqing Ruan, Zhizhao Zeng, Tianyi Zhai, Zhongda Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose agents are increasingly expected to perform professional analytics work, which requires sustained reasoning, iterative investigation, and reliable evidence. Training these capabilities requires diverse and challenging tasks. However, generating tasks directly from raw data often overlooks deeper questions that emerge through analysis and provides limited evidence for verification. To address these limitations, we introduce DataPraxis, an analytics task synthesis framework based on verified workspace evolution. The framework progressively enriches workspaces with validated findings and artifacts, then inherits and combines these workspaces to uncover deeper questions. It constructs tasks from accumulated findings and their dependencies, verifying analytical results both during evolution and after task assembly. Using this framework, we construct DataPraxis-5K, a dataset of 5,000 verified trajectories spanning diverse domains and output formats, to train DataPraxis-27B and DataPraxis-35B-A3B. DataPraxis-27B achieves 55.11 on DABStep and 51.9 on JobBench, matching or surpassing frontier models such as GLM-5.2. DataPraxis-5K is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[WorkWorlds: Evaluating Agents in a Company Built Before the Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23806)**  `arXiv:2609.23806`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yining Hua, Levi Lian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many knowledge work benchmarks are built around single tasks, where the context and files for each task are selected concurrently or after task definition. Research has argued that this construction method does not reflect real workplace work, yet the impact has never been measured under controlled conditions. To compare the two constructions under controlled conditions, we introduce WorkWorlds, an infrastructure that modularizes company components into revision, date, and employee seat to be separately adjusted. Using historical data donated by a real company, we reconstructed a pharmaceutical company, PharmaCo. Two experts with over ten years of industry experience each wrote eight tasks based on real events that occurred at the company, and each independently constructed a task-specific environment for every task. Across 192 controlled evaluations, when the agent was switched from the task-specific environment to all the materials truly visible to the employee, evidence access dropped from 90.4 percent to 72.8 percent, and the criterion pass rate dropped from 76.7 percent to 68.0 percent. However, in cases where the agent found the required evidence, the pass rates under both conditions were almost identical. Furthermore, switching between the environments built by the two experts for the same task resulted in an average pass rate difference of 6.6 percentage points. The three agents also gained by different amounts, which changed the gaps between them. We argue that benchmarks should report how environment materials were selected and, if possible, they should test agent score changes after changing the environment builder.
  </details>

- **[MAP: A Benchmark on Multimodal Accessibility Planning for Real World Places](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28384)**  `arXiv:2608.28384`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jason Armitage, Ioannis Tsochantaridis, Linda Mazzone, Chuqiao Yan, Srini Narayanan, Sarah Ebling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce MAP, the first benchmark to evaluate multimodal AI systems as assistants for users with accessibility requirements when planning visits to places in the real world. In our evaluation, systems are presented with requests to verify or recommend a point of interest meeting an accessibility requirement. MAP contains two novel assessments: Claim verification for accessibility planning assesses if information on places and stated accessibility features is supported and identifies places that satisfy requested accessibility features. Visual evidence retrieval for accessibility planning checks if a multimodal AI system can select visual evidence for the requested place and accessibility feature. Our methodology supports comparison of AI systems in a setting where place information and accessibility information can change over time by evaluating systems and refreshing ground truth data at scheduled times. The benchmark is based on automatic rating and human rating for a proportion of responses.
  </details>

- **[AgentAbstain: Do LLM Agents Know When Not to Act?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10059)**  `arXiv:2607.10059`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xun Liu, Yi Evie Zhang, Vira Kasprova, Parisa Rabbani, Pardis Sadat Zahraei, Tianyu Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent systems based on large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for autonomous tasks, yet existing evaluations mostly focus on task success rather than whether agents know when to abstain. This gap poses real risks: under ambiguity, conflicting constraints, or tool failures, agents may execute unintended and irreversible actions. To close this gap, we present the first systematic evaluation framework for agentic abstention: the calibrated ability of tool-using LLM agents to recognize when not to act. At its core, AgentAbstain is a paired-task benchmark built on an agent-native taxonomy of 8 abstention scenarios across pre-execution reasoning and runtime discovery. It contains 263 paired tasks across 42 executable sandbox environments, where each pair consists of a should-act task and a should-abstain variant produced through a controlled perturbation to the instruction, tool, or environment state. To scale this paired design and resist data contamination, we propose AbstainGen, a fully automated pipeline that synthesizes sandbox environments and generates paired tasks end-to-end, validated by deterministic replay and semantic LLM judges; fresh task instances can be regenerated on demand, and three independent annotators rate 94-98% of sampled tasks as well-designed. Across 17 frontier LLMs in 4 agent harnesses, the best agent (Gemini 3.1 Pro) achieves only 59.5% paired accuracy (correct on both the act and abstain sides of each paired task). More importantly, abstention capability is largely independent of general task-solving capability, indicating that scaling task-solving alone will not close this gap. We further identify failure modes such as post-hoc abstention, in which agents execute irreversible actions before recognizing abstention triggers. Our code and dataset are open-sourced atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[CXReasonAgent: Evidence-Grounded Diagnostic Reasoning Agent for Chest X-rays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23276)**  `arXiv:2602.23276`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hyungyung Lee, Hangyul Yoon, Edward Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chest X-ray plays a central role in thoracic diagnosis, and its interpretation inherently requires multi-step, evidence-grounded reasoning. However, large vision-language models (LVLMs) often generate plausible responses that are not faithfully grounded in diagnostic evidence and provide limited visual evidence for verification, while also requiring costly retraining to support new diagnostic tasks, limiting their reliability and adaptability in clinical settings. To address these limitations, we present CXReasonAgent, a diagnostic agent that integrates a large language model (LLM) with clinically grounded diagnostic tools to perform evidence-grounded diagnostic reasoning using image-derived diagnostic and visual evidence. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce CXReasonDail, a multi-turn dialogue benchmark with 1,946 dialogues across 12 diagnostic tasks, and show that CXReasonAgent produces more faithfully grounded responses than LVLMs. These findings highlight the importance of integrating clinically grounded diagnostic tools to enable reliable and verifiable diagnostic reasoning. The demo is available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>
